# Church History · Reference Card

> Open book. This card is meant to be open while you work — including during
> quizzes. Nothing here is something you're expected to recall cold.

This course tells the Church's history from the Jerusalem community to Leo XIV as professional history written from within the tradition: every source gets the same questions whoever wrote it, the scandals are told plainly, and where historians disagree the disagreement is named, not settled. Use the card mid-problem to look up a term, an episode's dates and actors, the rival positions in a historiographical crux and the evidence each leans on, a contested number exactly as the lessons hedged it, which public-domain translation a passage comes from, and where a ceded topic (what a council *defined*, what primacy *proves*) is actually taught. Cruxes here are maps, not verdicts. Where an entry touches a boss problem it stops at outline level.

## Terms

Vocabulary in first-appearance order. Terms with their own entry below (under Concepts and methods, Episodes, or Historiographical cruxes) are not repeated here; search the card for them.

| Term | Means | First used |
|---|---|---|
| Two dates | The standing habit: for every claim, hold when it happened and when someone wrote it down | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-first-generation-and-its-sources.md) |
| Prefect / procurator | Pilate's title was prefect (Caesarea inscription, 1961); Tacitus's "procurator" is the title of his own day, a slip that dates his framing | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-first-generation-and-its-sources.md) |
| Pillars | Paul's name for James, Cephas and John at Jerusalem (Galatians 2:9) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-first-generation-and-its-sources.md) |
| *Episkopos* / presbyter | "Overseer" / "elder": in 1 Clement (chs. 42–44) the same office; before about 150 check what "bishop" means in the text at hand | [1.2](lessons/01-02-bishops-canon-and-the-parting-of-the-ways.md) |
| *Potiorem principalitatem* | The disputed phrase in Irenaeus III.3.2: "pre-eminent authority" or "more powerful origin", and whose | [1.2](lessons/01-02-bishops-canon-and-the-parting-of-the-ways.md) |
| *Convenire* | In the same passage: "agree with" or "come together at, resort to" | [1.2](lessons/01-02-bishops-canon-and-the-parting-of-the-ways.md) |
| *Cognitio* | Trial at the magistrate's own judgment; the governor's broad discretion in Christian cases before 250 | [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md) |
| *Nomen Christianum* | "The name" itself as the charge: being a Christian was enough (de Ste. Croix's reading) | [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md) |
| Local and sporadic phase | 64 to about 250: action only when someone accused a Christian before a governor | [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md) |
| Little Peace | 260–303: more than forty years without general persecution; churches built openly | [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md) |
| Confessors | Christians who suffered for the faith and survived; some issued letters of peace readmitting the lapsed | [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md) |
| Sol Invictus | "The unconquered Sun": the solar god on Constantine's coinage into the 320s | [1.4](lessons/01-04-constantine.md) |
| Concord | What the Christian emperor wanted from the Church: one Church praying for one empire, no riots in its cities | [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md) |
| *Ousia* | "Substance"; the word banned from the creed of 359 | [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md) |
| Caesaropapism | The emperor as head of the Church in doctrine; the lesson says it fits 325–381 badly | [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md) |
| New Rome | Constantinople's title in canon 3 of Constantinople (381), which ranked it second after Rome | [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md) |
| *Theotokos* | "God-bearer", the title for Mary that Nestorius objected to | [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md) |
| Acclamations | Rhythmic shouts recorded by a council's notaries in an edited record; not votes or decrees | [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md) |
| *Latrocinium* | "A gathering of robbers": Leo's name for Ephesus 449 (Letter 95, July 451) | [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md) |
| *Henotikon* | Zeno's formula of 482 setting Chalcedon aside to reunite the miaphysites | [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md) |
| Diptychs | The lists of bishops prayed for at the liturgy; striking a name was a break in communion | [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md) |
| *Iudicatum* / *Constitutum* | Vigilius's condemnation of the Three Chapters (548) / his refusal to condemn the dead (first, May 553) and final acceptance (second, February 554) | [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md) |
| Anchorite | "One who withdraws": the hermit, under no superior but God; Antony the model | [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md) |
| *Koinonia* | "Fellowship": Pachomius's federation of houses under one head | [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md) |
| Mixed rules | Local combinations of rules that houses followed before the Carolingian reforms made Benedict's the norm | [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md) |
| "Semi-Pelagian" | A later label for the Massilians; the ancient name was "remnants of the Pelagians" | [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md) |
| Top-down conversion | Missionaries go to courts, kings are baptized first, peoples follow; Fletcher's typical pattern. Not the same as forced | [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md) |
| Survivals | Folk practices read as pagan relics under a Christian name; a conclusion to argue for, not a starting point (Jolly) | [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md) |
| Micro-Christendoms | Peter Brown's name for the many local churches each building a small copy of the wider Church | [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md) |
| *Ahl al-dhimma* | "People of the protection": Christians and Jews under Muslim rule | [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-rise-of-islam-and-the-christian-mediterranean.md) |
| *Kharaj* | Tribute paid by a capitulated city (Baladhuri's Hims passage) | [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-rise-of-islam-and-the-christian-mediterranean.md) |
| Leonine Wall | The wall Leo IV built round the Vatican hill after the raid on St Peter's (846) | [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-rise-of-islam-and-the-christian-mediterranean.md) |
| Iconoclasm | The Byzantine emperors' turn against the veneration of icons, from about 726–730 | [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md) |
| *Patricius Romanorum* | "Patrician of the Romans": protector of Rome, the title Stephen II gave Pepin and his sons (754) | [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md) |
| Exarchate and Pentapolis | Byzantine territory round Ravenna taken by the Lombards and given by Pepin to the pope (756) | [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md) |
| *Libertas ecclesiae* | "The freedom of the Church": the Gregorian reformers' slogan against lay control of churches | [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md) |
| Ring and staff | The symbols of a bishop's spiritual office, handed over at investiture | [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md) |
| *Regalia* | The lands and rights a bishop held from the crown, conferred *per sceptrum* (by the sceptre) under the Concordat of Worms | [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md) |

| Three questions for dating a schism | Who acted against whom (persons or churches)? With what authority? Was it received? | [4.2](lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md) |

## Concepts and methods

Tools and technical terms the lessons link to. Plain-English line first, then the detail, then the lesson.

### Four questions for a source

Ask of every text who wrote it, when, for whom and to do what, before asking what it can prove.

**Author** (who, and how did they know?), **date** (when, and how long after the events?), **audience** (who was meant to read it?), **purpose** (what was it trying to do to them?), then the payoff: **what can it establish, and what can't it?** A hostile witness is good evidence for what his side believed and poor evidence for the other side's motives; a later witness can preserve an early fact, but you need a reason to think it did. Weigh proximity and interest separately: "later" is not "false" and "early" is not "true". Genre decides what a source can answer (the Decian *libelli* never mention Christians).

*Introduced:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-first-generation-and-its-sources.md); used in every lesson, e.g. [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-the-crusades.md)

### Succession lists

Public lists of a church's leaders back to the apostles, drawn up to answer claims of secret tradition.

Irenaeus, *Against Heresies* III.3.3 (c. 180), against Gnostics who claimed a secret tradition, gives Rome's: Linus, Anacletus, Clement, down to Eleutherius, the twelfth from the apostles. What it proves: by 180 Rome had a remembered, ordered line of leaders that outsiders could check. What it does not prove by itself: that Linus or Clement governed as single bishops (see [Monepiscopate](#monepiscopate)). The list shows a remembered order, not that the names were fictions.

*Introduced:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-bishops-canon-and-the-parting-of-the-ways.md)

### Monepiscopate

One bishop over a city's church, with presbyters and deacons under him.

Attested in Asia Minor in Ignatius's letters (traditionally c. 110; T. D. Barnes, 2008, argued for the 140s): "Wherever the bishop shall appear, there let the multitude also be" (*Smyrnaeans* 8). At Rome the evidence (1 Clement, Hermas, Ignatius's silence, Irenaeus calling Anicetus and his predecessors "presbyters", *HE* 5.24.14) points to a college of presbyters until around the time of Anicetus (c. 155). Peter Lampe (*From Paul to Valentinus*; German 1987, English 2003): scattered house churches led by presbyters; a single bishop only in the second half of the century, Victor the first to act decisively as one. The Jesuit Francis Sullivan (*From Apostles to Bishops*, 2001) accepts presbyteral leadership for part of the century and argues the episcopate's emergence can still be called apostolic; that theological question uses the development tool of [`fundamental-theology` 5.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-02-newmans-theory-of-development.md).

*Introduced:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-bishops-canon-and-the-parting-of-the-ways.md)

### Libellus

The certificate of sacrifice that Decius's order required of everyone.

About 46 survive on papyrus from Egypt, most from the Fayum village of Theadelphia, nearly all dated June–July 250. Formula: the declarant has always sacrificed and has now sacrificed again before the commissioners, who sign that they saw it. One is issued to Aurelia Ammonous, a priestess of the crocodile god Petesouchos. They show the order reached villages, ran on paperwork, and bound pagans too. **None mentions Christians**, so they cannot tell how many Christians complied, bribed or died; for that, Cyprian's letters and *On the Lapsed*, and Dionysius of Alexandria in Eusebius.

*Introduced:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md)

### Lapsi

Christians who complied under persecution, above all under Decius in 250.

Three kinds: *sacrificati* (sacrificed), *thurificati* (offered incense), *libellatici* (bought or wangled a certificate without sacrificing). Some confessors issued letters of peace readmitting them; Cyprian of Carthage, who had gone into hiding, insisted the bishops decide, and a council at Carthage (251) graded penance by the seriousness of the lapse. At Rome, Novatian refused readmission to those who had sacrificed and went into schism against Cornelius. The question of who may readmit the fallen returns after 303 as the [Donatist schism](#donatist-schism).

*Introduced:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md)

### Contumacia

Obstinacy: defiance of a magistrate's order.

Pliny's stated ground for executing Christians who persisted (*Letters* 10.96): "contumacious and inflexible obstinacy certainly deserved correction" (tr. Melmoth, rev. Bosanquet). Central to Sherwin-White's reading ([De Ste Croix vs Sherwin-White](#de-ste-croix-vs-sherwin-white)).

*Introduced:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md)

### Pax deorum

"The peace of the gods": the Roman belief that public cult kept the gods favourable.

Christian refusal of the gods ("atheism") endangered it, so floods, drought and plague were read as divine anger and crowds demanded action (Tertullian, *Apology* 40, c. 197: "Away with the Christians to the lion!"). Central to de Ste. Croix's reading.

*Introduced:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md)

### Traditor

"One who handed over": a cleric who surrendered scriptures or church property in the Great Persecution.

The charge at the root of the Donatist schism: Caecilian's opponents said one of his consecrators, Felix of Aptunga, had been a *traditor*.

*Introduced:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-constantine.md)

### Labarum

Constantine's jewelled standard bearing the Christian sign.

Described by Eusebius, *Life of Constantine* 1.30–31, as made after the dream in which Christ told him to copy the sign he had seen. Not in Lactantius, who has only the sign marked on the soldiers' shields.

*Introduced:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-constantine.md)

### Homoousios

"Of one substance" with the Father: Nicaea's word for the Son.

In the creed of Nicaea (325); little invoked until the 350s, when Athanasius made it the test of orthodoxy; dropped (with *ousia* banned) from the Homoian creed of 359. What it defined belongs to [`trinity-and-god`](../trinity-and-god/syllabus.md) 3.1; its authority is on the top rung of the [ladder of doctrinal authority](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md). Here it is an event and a party badge.

*Introduced:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md)

### Homoians

The party of the 359 creed, which called the Son "like the Father" and banned the word *ousia*.

Pressed by Constantius II to unite the East: the twin councils of Ariminum (West) and Seleucia (East) accepted the formula under imperial pressure in 359; Constantinople ratified it in 360. Jerome, about twenty years later: "The whole world groaned, and was astonished to find itself Arian" (*Dialogue against the Luciferians* 19), the winning side's retrospect. Favoured by Valens (364–378); the Homoian bishop of Constantinople expelled by Theodosius in November 380. The course's name for the 359 party instead of "Arian" (see [Arianism as a label](#arianism-as-a-label)).

*Introduced:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md)

### Imperial convocation of councils

The Christian emperor's toolkit: summon a council, carry bishops on the imperial post, give its decisions force of law, exile the losers.

The pattern of 325–381: these tools worked in the short run and failed in the long run. Emperors decided who sat in a see this year; they could not make a formula stick when bishops, monks and congregations would not accept it (Constantius's 359 creed was repudiated after his death). **Convening is not defining**: no source has Constantine drafting the creed. The same machinery of emperor, council and exile runs the fifth-century councils ([2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md)), where a council's authority was made after the event by imperial enforcement and reception.

*Introduced:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md)

### Miaphysite

"One nature" (*mia physis*): the right name for the churches that rejected Chalcedon.

Egypt and much of Syria (and Armenia) rejected Chalcedon as a betrayal of Cyril's formula, "one incarnate nature of God the Word": one incarnate nature out of two, fully divine and fully human. "Monophysite" is avoided because it suggests Eutyches's view that Christ's humanity was absorbed, which these churches also condemn. Today's Oriental Orthodox. Joint declarations with the Coptic (1973) and Syriac (1984) Orthodox Churches treat the old division as largely one of terms; that theology belongs to [`christology`](../christology/syllabus.md).

*Introduced:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md)

### Cenobitic monasticism

The common life (*koinos bios*): many monks under a rule and a superior, sharing work, meals and prayer.

One of three shapes of renunciation: the **hermit** (anchorite; Antony), the **community** (cenobite; Pachomius, first community at Tabennesi between about 318 and 323), and the **middle way** of loose cell settlements around a senior monk (Nitria and Scetis, where Cassian learned). Pachomius's *koinonia* joined houses under one head with a written rule, obedience, set hours and manual work; the Catholic Encyclopedia counts nine men's and two women's houses at his death, some modern accounts fewer; Jerome put the rules into Latin in 404. Basil's *Asketikon* (Annesi, 358) put community life under Scripture and tied it to care of the poor and sick.

*Introduced:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md)

### Massilians

The monks of Marseilles and Lérins troubled by Augustine's late writings on predestination.

After the Pelagian controversy (411–418; Pelagius condemned by African councils and Popes Innocent I, 417, and Zosimus, 418). Cassian's *Conference* 13 held that a good will sometimes begins from us and grace then meets and strengthens it; Prosper of Aquitaine wrote to Augustine (428–429) and attacked Cassian c. 432 without naming him; Vincent of Lérins's *Commonitorium* (434) is widely read as part of the same quarrel ([`fundamental-theology` 5.1](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-01-vincent-of-lerins.md)). Ended at the [Council of Orange 529](#council-of-orange-529). "Semi-Pelagian" is a later label; the ancient name was "remnants of the Pelagians", and calling Cassian a Pelagian in any form is a verdict.

*Introduced:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md)

### Conversion and Christianization

Two things "conversion" runs together: a people's baptism, and the slow Christianizing of its life.

**Conversion** (narrow sense): a ruler and his people accept baptism, the Church's law and clergy; can happen in a year; an event chronicles record. **Christianization**: belief, prayer, burial, marriage, the calendar and imagination become Christian; takes generations; reconstructed from law codes, penitentials, charms, graves and complaints. Richard Fletcher (*The Conversion of Europe*, 1997): top-down conversion is the typical pattern over a thousand years, and a deliberate mission to the heathen developed slowly. Where the debate lives: did the countryside stay half-pagan under a veneer, or make a Christianity of its own? Karen Jolly (*Popular Religion in Late Saxon England*, 1996) reads Anglo-Saxon charms as a Christian popular religion, a middle ground between liturgy and folk medicine; Peter Brown (*The Rise of Western Christendom*, 1996) sees many local "micro-Christendoms". Top-down is not the same as forced: Kent and Moravia were royal decisions; Saxony is the exception.

*Introduced:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md)

### Dhimmi

A protected, subordinate non-Muslim subject under Muslim rule.

Christians and Jews became *ahl al-dhimma*: lives, property and existing churches guaranteed; in return the [jizya](#jizya) and a subordinate status. A contract between unequal parties: the protection was real, so was the inequality, and the terms could be tightened when a ruler chose (al-Mutawakkil's distinctive dress for Christians and Jews, 850, and later destruction of new places of worship; Muhammad I of Cordoba removing Christians from palace posts after 852). Early practice from surrender treaties and chronicles; later legal theory from the [Pact of Umar](#pact-of-umar). Islam as a religion belongs to [`apologetics-foundations`](../apologetics-foundations/syllabus.md) 7.1.

*Introduced:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-rise-of-islam-and-the-christian-mediterranean.md)

### Jizya

The poll tax dhimmis paid for protection.

The one condition on which the Damascus surrender (635, in al-Baladhuri, tr. Hitti, 1916) hangs: "So long as they pay the poll-tax, nothing but good shall befall them." In Baladhuri's Hims story the Muslims refund the tribute before the Yarmuk because "We are too busy to support and protect you": payment and defence as two sides of one bargain.

*Introduced:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-rise-of-islam-and-the-christian-mediterranean.md)

### Conversion curve

Bulliet's estimate of how fast conquered populations became Muslim, from names in biographical dictionaries.

Richard Bulliet (*Conversion to Islam in the Medieval Period*, 1979): Iran majority Muslim in the ninth century; Egypt, Syria and Iraq around the tenth. Critics: the dictionaries record urban, educated men; scholarship has pushed the dates later, and some historians of Egypt place the decisive shift as late as the fourteenth century. The Christian Middle East shrank over three to seven centuries; "conquered" is not "converted".

*Introduced:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-rise-of-islam-and-the-christian-mediterranean.md)

### Internal criticism

Testing a document's words against the date it claims.

Valla's method against the Donation of Constantine (1440): anachronism (a Constantinian decree speaking of "satraps"; Constantinople named as a patriarchal see three days after Constantine's baptism, when it was "not yet ... founded, nor planned") and internal contradiction (the same text later says a city "should be built"). Modern scholarship strengthens the satrap point: early medieval Latin used *satrapa* for a great lord (Bede, 731, on the Old Saxons), so the word points to the forgery's real date. Valla's motive (secretary to Alfonso of Aragon, at war with Eugenius IV) does not weaken the argument, which rests on evidence anyone can check; an author's interest counts against testimony, not against inspectable evidence ([`philosophical-method` 2.3](../philosophical-method/lessons/02-03-informal-fallacies-and-when-they-arent.md)). Contrast weighing a hostile *witness* (Liutprand; see [Saeculum obscurum](#saeculum-obscurum)).

*Introduced:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md)

### Lay investiture

A lay ruler conferring a bishop's office by handing him the ring and staff.

Around 1000 a bishop was both pastor and great lord: in the German kingdom bishops held counties, tolls and markets and owed troops and counsel, and kings chose them. To a king it was plain sense; anointed rulers did not think of themselves as mere laymen. The reformers came to see it as the root of [simony](#simony). A ban at Gregory VII's Lenten synod of February 1075 is reported only by the Milanese chronicler Arnulf; the register's record is silent, and some hold it concerned Milan only. **The first securely documented general decree is November 1078** (renewed March 1080). Investiture was the flashpoint, not the whole quarrel.

*Introduced:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)

### Simony

Buying or selling a spiritual office.

Named for Simon Magus (Acts 8). One of the reformers' two diseases, with [nicolaitism](#nicolaitism). At Reims in October 1049 Leo IX made bishops declare publicly whether they had paid for their sees.

*Introduced:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)

### Nicolaitism

The reformers' name for clerical marriage and concubinage.

The second disease the Gregorian reformers attacked. At first they wanted only a clean clergy; they came to think the root was the system in which laymen handed out churches.

*Introduced:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)

### Filioque

"And the Son": the Western addition to the Creed's line on the Spirit's procession, and a standing Eastern grievance.

Spread in Spain and Frankish Gaul. Leo III declined to add it at Rome and had the Creed without it set up on silver shields; Rome adopted it in 1014, when Henry II was crowned. Photius's encyclical of 867 condemned it. Two questions: is the doctrine true, and could the West add to a conciliar creed on its own? Both belong to [`trinity-and-god`](../trinity-and-god/syllabus.md) Module 5 and [`apologetics-catholic-claims`](../apologetics-catholic-claims/syllabus.md) 5.1. Here: a complaint that the West had changed the common Creed.

*Introduced:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md)

### Primacy of honour vs jurisdiction

The two readings of Rome's first-millennium standing: presidency within a communion, or authority over it.

**Jurisdiction** (Catholic historians such as Francis Dvornik): Byzantium long acknowledged a real Roman role as final court of appeal. **Honour** (Orthodox historians and theologians such as John Meyendorff): presidency within a communion of churches, exercised through councils, not jurisdiction over them. Chadwick (Anglican) traces a slow Western centralization around Rome built on the West's political situation and centuries of decretals and canon law that the East never shared. The joint Catholic-Orthodox **Ravenna Document** (2007, §41) agrees Rome held first place among the patriarchs in the first millennium and records disagreement on how to read the evidence for its prerogatives. See the [primacy table](#roman-primacy-the-evidence-by-era); what it proves belongs to [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md).

*Introduced:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md)

### Crusade indulgence

In its earliest form, remission of penance for the journey to Jerusalem made with pure intention.

Canon 2 of Clermont (1095), known only from participants' notes such as Bishop Lambert of Arras's (no official record survives): "Whoever, out of devotion alone and not to gain honour or money, sets out for Jerusalem to liberate the Church of God, let that journey be counted to him in place of all penance" (lesson's translation). A disciplinary canon of a regional council, not a definition. Three features: the reward attaches to the **journey**, not to killing; it is **conditional on intention**; it remits **penance** (satisfaction for confessed sins), not sin as such and not a promise of heaven. Preachers and chroniclers promised more (Fulcher's Urban: "immediate remission of sins" for those who die on the way or in battle; Thatcher, 1905), and many crusaders believed them: the canon is better evidence of what Urban offered, Fulcher of what crusaders came to believe. Later theology: [`sacraments-and-liturgy`](../sacraments-and-liturgy/syllabus.md); treasury of merit: [`theology-of-debt`](../theology-of-debt/syllabus.md). (Comparing the speech versions is Boss 4.)

*Introduced:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-the-crusades.md)

### Plenitudo potestatis

"Fullness of power": the theory behind thirteenth-century papal government.

Leo I had used the phrase to contrast his own authority with the "share of the care" he gave a delegate; Innocent III was the first pope to use it regularly for papal government, and made "vicar of Christ" the standard title. In practice limited: the weapons were spiritual ([excommunication and interdict](#interdict)), working only when a ruler's subjects and rivals cared, and Innocent said the pope acted in temporal matters only "occasionally" and for cause (*Per venerabilem*, 1202). A monarchy whose writ ran everywhere and whose army was other people's.

*Introduced:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-papal-monarchy-innocent-iii-to-boniface-viii.md)

### Interdict

Suspension of most public sacraments across a territory, to pressure its ruler.

With excommunication, the pope's main weapon. Innocent III laid England under interdict in March 1208 (excommunicating John in November 1209); lifted July 1214. It worked on John because he had other enemies (Philip Augustus preparing to invade, restless barons).

*Introduced:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-papal-monarchy-innocent-iii-to-boniface-viii.md)

### Mendicant orders

Orders of friars who lived by begging and worked in towns, under the pope rather than the local bishop.

Friars belong to an order, move where sent, beg and preach in towns; monks vow stability in one house and live on endowments. **Franciscans:** Francis received oral approval of a short rule in 1209 or 1210 (sources differ); final rule confirmed by Honorius III in *Solet annuere* (29 November 1223: "without property"); Francis died 3 October 1226; his *Testament* forbade seeking curial privileges and glosses on the Rule (tr. Paschal Robinson, 1906); Gregory IX's *Quo elongati* (28 September 1230) ruled the *Testament* not binding and allowed a *nuntius* for money. **Dominicans:** Dominic of Caleruega, after a decade preaching against the Cathars, founded a house of preachers at Toulouse in 1215; confirmed by Honorius III, *Religiosam vitam*, 22 December 1216. Herbert Grundmann (*Religious Movements in the Middle Ages*, 1935): heretics and new orders grew from the same impulse (apostolic poverty, lay preaching); what decided which a group became was largely how the hierarchy answered (Valdes condemned 1184; Francis approved).

*Introduced:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-friars-and-universities.md)

### Use and ownership

The Franciscan distinction that let friars live on goods they did not own.

*Usus* (use) versus *dominium* (ownership). Nicholas III's [Exiit qui seminat](#exiit-qui-seminat) (1279) put ownership of the friars' goods in the Holy See unless the donor kept it. John XXII's *Ad conditorem canonum* (8 December 1322) renounced papal ownership, adding that with things consumed in use (like food) use cannot be separated from ownership. Aquinas's account of property has a twin distinction ([`history-of-political-thought` 2.4](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/02-04-aquinas-on-kingship-tyranny-and-property.md)).

*Introduced:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-friars-and-universities.md)

### Conciliarism

The view that a general council representing the whole Church holds authority over the pope, at least in faith, schism and reform.

Carried Constance ([Haec Sancta](#haec-sancta)) and radicalized at Basel. Not anti-papal: Constance wanted a pope, elected one, and burned Hus. Pius II's *Execrabilis* (1460) forbade appeals from pope to council; Luther still appealed to one. Its theory (corporation law, Gerson, Tierney) is [`history-of-political-thought` 2.5](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/02-05-marsilius-and-conciliarism.md); its theological status belongs to [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md) 4.2.

*Introduced:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-avignon-the-great-schism-and-the-councils.md)

### Cuius regio eius religio

"Whose the realm, his the religion": a later summary of the Peace of Augsburg, not its words.

Coined by the Protestant jurist Joachim Stephani around 1600. It overstates the treaty: already-mixed imperial cities had to keep both confessions, and bishops' lands fell under the [ecclesiastical reservation](#ecclesiastical-reservation). What the treaty gave was the estates' choice between two religions and subjects' right to leave.

*Introduced:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)

### Confessionalization

The thesis that Lutheran, Reformed and Catholic territories built churches and states in the same way, 1555–1648.

Heinz Schilling and Wolfgang Reinhard, working in parallel from the late 1970s: each wrote a confession, trained and inspected clergy, catechized, disciplined morals (consistories or visitations) and policed its borders; rulers backed it because a uniform church made obedient, identifiable subjects; church and state built each other. Textbook case: the Electoral Palatinate, Reformed under Frederick III (Heidelberg Catechism, 1563), Lutheran under Louis VI (1576), Reformed again under John Casimir (1583). **Critics** grant the machinery, dispute the direction of force: top-down; struggles with minorities who kept their faith against the ruler and parishes that took what they wanted; fits Germany better than France; can flatten theological differences. France: counterexample, or a late case (Nantes revoked 1685)? Applied to Catholic discipline in [6.4](lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md).

*Introduced:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)

### Royal supremacy

The Crown in the place the pope had held over the English Church.

Act of Supremacy (26 Henry VIII c. 1, November 1534; Gee and Hardy, 1896): "*Albeit* the king's majesty justly and rightfully is and ought to be the supreme head": it claims to confirm an ancient right Rome had usurped, not to create one; it gives power to visit and correct "errors, heresies, abuses", jurisdiction, not orders. Elizabeth's 1559 oath: "only supreme governor" in spiritual and temporal things; "head" dropped, partly because many balked at a woman as head of the Church. Once the monarch heads the Church, dissent looks like disloyalty. Treason came to cover denying the king's titles (Fisher and More, 1535). Theory: Hobbes's sovereign as supreme pastor ([`history-of-political-thought` 3.4](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/03-04-hobbes-the-sovereign-and-the-church.md)).

*Introduced:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)

### Recusancy

Refusal to attend the Church of England's services, punished by fines.

Parliament's answers to *Regnans in Excelsis*: 1571; 1581 (20 pounds a month for recusancy); 1585 (any priest ordained by Rome's authority since 1559 staying in England more than forty days a traitor). Seminary priests from Douai (founded 1568) arrived in the 1570s, Jesuits from 1580.

*Introduced:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)

### Episcopal residence

The bishop's duty to live in his diocese, over which Trent nearly broke.

If residence was of divine law, the pope could not freely dispense from it, so the question became one about papal power over bishops. Giovanni Morone brokered a way through in 1562–63; residence decreed in Session XXIII, ch. 1. Before Trent bishops held several sees and lived away from them; Borromeo (Milan, from 1565) was the first resident archbishop there in about eighty years.

*Introduced:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)

### Conversos

Jewish converts to Christianity in Spain and their descendants, the Spanish Inquisition's first targets.

After the massacres of 1391 and decades of pressure, Castile and Aragon held many thousands. Some kept Jewish practices in secret (how many is disputed); many prospered and were resented. The Inquisition tried baptized Christians, so its early caseload was conversos suspected of judaizing.

*Introduced:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-spanish-and-roman-inquisitions.md)

### Limpieza de sangre

"Purity of blood": statutes barring people of Jewish descent from office.

First issued by rebels in Toledo in 1449 and condemned by Nicholas V; spread anyway through cathedral chapters, orders and colleges in the sixteenth century. A Church that taught that baptism made one people let its institutions sort Christians by ancestry.

*Introduced:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-spanish-and-roman-inquisitions.md)

### Auto de fe

The public ceremony at which the Inquisition's sentences were pronounced.

The first held at Seville on 6 February 1481, when six people were burned. 1559–62 autos at Valladolid and Seville destroyed Spain's small Protestant circles. A Madrid auto of 1680 sent 21 people to their deaths. The burning itself was done by the secular arm.

*Introduced:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-spanish-and-roman-inquisitions.md)

### Patronato real

Royal patronage: Rome's grant to the Castilian crown of control over the Church in the Indies.

The tithes of the Indies (1501) and the right to nominate every bishop and benefice (Julius II, *Universalis ecclesiae regimini*, 1508). No church built without royal consent; the crown claimed papal letters for the Indies needed its approval before circulating. The price: the king paid for and answered for the mission. So the fight over native peoples was fought mainly inside Spain, in the king's councils, and Charles V could make Paul III annul *Pastorale officium* (1538). The Portuguese crown's patronage carried Xavier to Goa, the Asian counterpart.

*Introduced:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)

### Encomienda

A grant of native people to a Spaniard, who took their tribute and labour and was to protect them and have them instructed in the faith.

Legally not slavery; in practice on the islands it often worked like it. Regulated, not abolished, by the Laws of Burgos (27 December 1512: hours, food, instruction, limits on beating). The New Laws (20 November 1542) banned new Indian slavery and made encomiendas lapse at the holder's death; the Peruvian encomenderos rose under Gonzalo Pizarro (viceroy killed 1546) and the inheritance clause was revoked in 1545. Clergy also held encomiendas (Las Casas in Cuba until 1514).

*Introduced:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)

### Requerimiento

The formal demand read to native peoples before an attack, making the war that followed legally their fault.

Drafted in 1513 by the jurist Palacios Rubios: they must acknowledge the Church and the pope's grant to the Spanish monarchs, or any war that followed was their fault. The first response to a question of justice was a procedure, and one that made conquest legal.

*Introduced:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)

### Conflict thesis

The story that religion and science are enemies, with Galileo as reason's martyr.

Built by John William Draper, *History of the Conflict between Religion and Science* (1874), and Andrew Dickson White, *History of the Warfare of Science with Theology in Christendom* (1896). Largely abandoned by historians of science. Draper's Galileo folds the 1633 abjuration back into 1616 and has "facts which his judges as well as himself knew to be true", when the motion was not proved in 1633.

*Introduced:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Complexity thesis

The historians of science's replacement: religion and science related in many ways, and each episode must be read on its own terms.

John Hedley Brooke, *Science and Religion: Some Historical Perspectives* (1991); Ronald Numbers (ed.), *Galileo Goes to Jail and Other Myths about Science and Religion* (2009). It does not make the affair innocent; it changes the questions: what was condemned (a claim about nature, a way of reading Scripture, one man's disobedience), by whom (council, papal definition, or congregation acting as court), and what was known.

*Introduced:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Vehement suspicion of heresy

The middle of three grades of heresy verdict, on which Galileo was sentenced in 1633.

Grades: formal heresy, vehement suspicion, light suspicion. Vehement suspicion required abjuration *de vehementi*; a second offence risked treatment as relapse, far graver. Galileo was **not** convicted of heresy. The 1633 sentence found him vehemently suspected of holding that the sun is the centre and the earth moves, and that an opinion may be held probable after being declared contrary to Scripture.

*Introduced:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Gallicanism

The conviction that the French Church had its own "liberties" against papal interference.

Codified in the **Four Articles** adopted by the Assembly of the Clergy on 19 March 1682 at Louis XIV's urging: (1) the pope has no power over kings in temporal matters; (2) the decrees of Constance on councils' authority remain in force; (3) papal power is bounded by the canons and customs of the French Church; (4) a papal judgement in faith is not irreformable until the Church consents. Behind the Civil Constitution (a Gallican like Armand-Gaston Camus could read it as ancient discipline restored) and revived by the Organic Articles (seminary teachers to subscribe to the Four Articles). Earlier, the French crown never promulgated Trent (Gallican liberties). Vatican I's clause that papal definitions are irreformable "not from the consent of the Church" was aimed at article 4 ([`fundamental-theology` 4.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-02-infallibility-and-its-conditions.md)).

*Introduced:* [8.1](lessons/08-01-enlightenment-revolution-and-napoleon.md); [8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)

### Ultramontanism

"Beyond the mountains": the view that after the Revolution only a strong, centralized papacy could protect the Church from the state.

Manifesto: Joseph de Maistre's *Du Pape* (1819). Napoleon's treatment of the Church made the case for many bishops; the Concordat began the turn (a pope removing a whole episcopate by his own act). John O'Malley's *Vatican I* (2018) reads the council as the movement's victory. Ultramontane readers took the Syllabus at face value.

*Introduced:* [8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)

### Juring and refractory clergy

Priests who swore the 1790 oath to the constitution (jurors) and those who refused (refractories).

Oath decreed 27 November 1790 (office-holders to swear to maintain the constitution, Civil Constitution included, or be deemed resigned); king's assent 26 December; sworn or refused January–February 1791. Only **seven bishops** swore. Parish jurors roughly half, estimates up to 55–60 percent depending on date and retractions. **Timothy Tackett** (*Religion, Revolution, and Regional Culture in Eighteenth-Century France*, 1986): jurors predominated in the Paris basin, centre and southeast; refusers in the west (Brittany, Anjou, the future Vendee), north and northeast (Flanders, Alsace, Lorraine) and southern Massif Central; the map matches religious practice and political allegiance into the twentieth century; priests chose within their communities (parishioners' attitudes, regional clerical cultures). Overreading Tackett: correlation does not show priests had no convictions. **Jurors were not apostates**: Rome charged lack of canonical mission, and in 1801 every constitutional bishop resigned when asked. **John McManners** (*The French Revolution and the Church*, 1969): if the Revolution "went wrong" at one point, it was the oath decree. Refractories became political suspects (deportation decree 1792).

*Introduced:* [8.1](lessons/08-01-enlightenment-revolution-and-napoleon.md)

### Modernism

*Pascendi*'s name for a single system it condemned: dogma reduced to religious experience, history cut loose from faith.

Scholars such as Alfred Loisy (France) and George Tyrrell (England) argued the Church could adopt historical criticism, history of dogma and philosophies of religious experience if doctrine was understood as growing. *Pascendi* (1907) built the system on agnosticism and vital immanence and called it the "synthesis of all heresies" (§39). Targets: philosophy of religion, history of dogma, biblical criticism, **not natural science** (the evolution condemned is of dogma). See [Was modernism a system](#was-modernism-a-system). Development as theory: [`fundamental-theology` 5.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-02-newmans-theory-of-development.md).

*Introduced:* [8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)

### Integralism

The anti-modernist tendency that policed fellow Catholics for doctrinal deviation, organized in Benigni's network.

The integralist informers of the [Sodalitium Pianum](#sodalitium-pianum) reported suspected modernists to Roman offices. Benedict XV's *Ad beatissimi* (1 November 1914) forbade branding those who merely disagreed as disloyal and told Catholics to drop party labels.

*Introduced:* [8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)

## Episodes, institutions and documents

One entry per event, institution or document the lessons link to, in course order. Plain-English line first, then the facts.

### Jerusalem meeting

A meeting in Jerusalem over whether Gentile converts must be circumcised and keep the Law of Moses.

**c. 48–50, disputed** (reckoned from Paul's "fourteen years", Galatians 2:1, which shifts with what he counted from). Two accounts: Paul's own (Galatians 2:1–10) and Luke's (Acts 15). They agree Gentiles were not required to be circumcised. They differ on whether the meeting was private or public and on what else was imposed: Acts 15:28–29 has four abstentions (idol-meat, blood, things strangled, fornication; "no further burden ... than these necessary things"); Galatians has "Only that we should be mindful of the poor" and a division of mission. Galatians 2:11–14 shows table fellowship at Antioch still unsettled afterwards. A meeting of one community's leaders with delegates from Antioch, not "the first ecumenical council".

*Introduced:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-first-generation-and-its-sources.md)

### Neronian persecution

Nero's punishment of Rome's Christians after the fire of July 64.

Known from Tacitus, *Annals* 15.44 (c. 115–120): Nero "fastened the guilt" on Christians; an "immense multitude" convicted "not so much of the crime of firing the city, as of hatred against mankind"; executions as spectacle in Nero's gardens. Suetonius, *Nero* 16.2 (c. 120), lists the punishment among Nero's regulations without connecting it to the fire. Local to Rome: no empire-wide law in 64. Neither Roman historian names Peter or Paul; their deaths under Nero rest on later Christian tradition. **The crux:** Brent Shaw ("The Myth of the Neronian Persecution", *JRS* 2015): Tacitus is the only source linking Christians and the fire, and the passage reflects Pliny's era, not the 60s; a mass execution by Nero is not established. Christopher Jones (*NTS* 2017): the name "Christian" is first-century, Romans shows a sizeable community, Tacitus had good access; no ground for doubt. They divide on whether a detail in one source fifty years later can be trusted when it fits the writer's own world so well. Most historians accept a Neronian persecution confined to Rome; the course takes that as its working account and carries Shaw's objection with it.

*Introduced:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-first-generation-and-its-sources.md)

### Flight to Pella

Eusebius's report that Jerusalem's Christians left the city before the war.

*Church History* 3.5.3, early fourth century: warned by an oracle, the Jerusalem Christians left for Pella across the Jordan before the war of 66–70. A gap of well over two centuries, a Christian interest in the mother church's survival, and an oracle; historians divide on whether it is memory or later legend. Lateness makes it unproven, not false.

*Introduced:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-first-generation-and-its-sources.md)

### Quartodeciman controversy

A dispute over when to end the paschal fast: on the 14th of Nisan, or on Sunday.

Churches of Asia ended it on 14 Nisan whatever the weekday; synods from Palestine to Gaul backed Sunday. Anicetus of Rome and Polycarp of Smyrna disagreed about it c. 155 and stayed in communion (*HE* 5.24.16–17). Under **Victor I (c. 189–198)**, Polycrates of Ephesus convened his bishops "at your desire" and refused; Victor "attempted" to cut the Asian churches off and "declared all the brethren there wholly excommunicate"; several bishops rebuked him, Irenaeus among them (*HE* 5.23–24). How it ended in Asia is unknown, and nothing shows the sentence took effect. Also a border between church and synagogue: Polycrates kept Easter by the Jewish reckoning, Victor's rule cut it loose (see [Partings of the ways](#partings-of-the-ways)).

*Introduced:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-bishops-canon-and-the-parting-of-the-ways.md)

### Great Persecution

The last and most systematic Roman persecution, begun under Diocletian and Galerius.

**23 February 303:** the church at Nicomedia destroyed. Four edicts, 303–304: scriptures surrendered and churches demolished; clergy arrested; clergy freed if they sacrificed; then (early 304) universal sacrifice on pain of death. In the West enforcement faded early (Constantius; Constantine from 306; Maxentius tolerated Christians from 306). In the East it ran on until the [Edict of Galerius](#edict-of-galerius) (311); Maximinus Daia renewed it in his territories, and it ended only with his defeat in 313. Death toll: see [Numbers](#martyr-counts).

*Introduced:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md)

### Edict of Galerius

The dying Galerius's grudging toleration of Christians, which ended the Great Persecution in the East.

**30 April 311**, issued at Nicomedia; text in Lactantius, *On the Deaths of the Persecutors* 34 (Lactantius hostile to Galerius, writing c. 315; trusted because Eusebius gives a Greek version agreeing in substance). Reasons given: persecution failed, and it left people worshipping neither the gods nor their own God. Grant: Christians may exist again and rebuild their meeting places, if they "offend not against good order"; they must pray to their God for the emperors. Framed as "clemency", "pardon" and "indulgence", not an admission of wrong. Not the "Edict of Milan".

*Introduced:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md)

### Edict of Milan

The conventional name for the 313 religious settlement agreed by Constantine and Licinius: not an edict, not issued at Milan, and not an establishment.

Constantine and Licinius met at Milan in **February 313**. On **13 June 313** Licinius posted at Nicomedia a letter to provincial governors granting "the Christians and all others" free worship and ordering restitution of Church property (Lactantius, *Deaths* 48; Eusebius, *Church History* 10.5, in slightly different forms). It put into effect a policy agreed at Milan and already in force in Constantine's West. Toleration was not new (Galerius, 311); 313 added restitution and an explicit grant of freedom to all. Establishment is Theodosius's [Cunctos populos](#cunctos-populos) (380).

*Introduced:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-constantine.md)

### Donatist schism

A North African split over a bishop allegedly consecrated by a *traditor*, which made the emperor the Church's referee.

Caecilian's consecration at Carthage (c. 311–312, disputed) was contested because a consecrator, Felix of Aptunga, was said to have been a [traditor](#traditor); the opponents set up Majorinus, soon succeeded by Donatus. Constantine's 313 grants named "the catholic Church, over which Cæcilianus presides" (*Church History* 10.6–7), giving the dispute material stakes. Judged for Caecilian by a synod at Rome under Miltiades (October 313), the Council of Arles (opened 1 August 314), and Constantine himself (316). Coercion 317–321, with confiscations and bloodshed; by 321 Constantine gave up and urged patience. The schism outlived the Western empire in Africa. Pattern set: bishops judge, the emperor enforces.

*Introduced:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-constantine.md)

### Council of Nicaea 325

The first council later counted as ecumenical, convened by Constantine over Arius.

Arius, presbyter of Baucalis in Alexandria, taught that the Son had a beginning; Bishop Alexander's synod excommunicated him c. 318 (sources allow 318–321); Eusebius of Nicomedia and synods in Palestine and Bithynia backed him. Constantine's letter of 324 to Alexander and Arius (*Life of Constantine* 2.68, 2.70, carried by Ossius of Cordoba) called the question "truly insignificant" and urged mutual forgiveness; within a year he turned from mediation to a council. Met early summer 325 (conventional opening 20 May). Eusebius of Caesarea counted more than 250 bishops; Athanasius about 300, later 318, the number that stuck; nearly all eastern; two Roman presbyters for Bishop Silvester. The creed called the Son [homoousios](#homoousios); only Secundus of Ptolemais and Theonas of Marmarica refused to sign and were exiled with Arius. It also settled the dating of Easter, dealt with the Melitian schism and issued twenty canons. It did **not** choose the books of the Bible, and no source has the bishops locked in. It started the imperial phase of the controversy rather than ending it.

*Introduced:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md)

### Exiles of Athanasius

Five exiles of the bishop of Alexandria, each following a change in imperial policy or person.

Bishop June 328 to May 373. Dates on Uta Heil's Erlangen chronology:

| Exile | Dates | Sent by | Occasion |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Feb 336 – Nov 337 | Constantine | Tyre's deposition (335); the charge of threatening to hold back the grain fleet; exiled to Trier |
| 2 | Apr 339 – Oct 346 | Constantius II | a rival bishop installed; refuge in Rome, defended by Pope Julius |
| 3 | Feb 356 – Feb 362 | Constantius II | troops enter his church; hides among the monks |
| 4 | Oct 362 – Aug 363 | Julian | ordered out of Egypt |
| 5 | Oct 365 – Feb 366 | Valens | general order against bishops Constantius had removed |

About 197 months, about 16.4 years, roughly 37 percent of his episcopate (older books say seventeen years). Stated grounds were mostly about order, not doctrine; returns followed a change of emperor or a western colleague's pressure (Constans for 346).

*Introduced:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md)

### Cunctos populos

Theodosius's law of 380 making Nicene Christianity the faith of the empire's subjects.

**27 February 380**, issued by Theodosius with Gratian and Valentinian II (Theodosian Code 16.1.2), addressed to the people of Constantinople. Subjects are to hold the faith the apostle Peter handed to the Romans, as now kept by **Damasus of Rome and Peter of Alexandria**; those who do are Catholic Christians, the rest heretics liable to divine and imperial punishment. The landmark usually meant by "Christianity became the state religion" (not 313). "Heretic" became a legal status: losing a church dispute could now cost churches and civil standing, the root of coercion that returns in [5.3](lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md).

*Introduced:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md)

### Council of Constantinople 381

Theodosius's eastern council that closed the Arian controversy by council after closing it by law.

May 381, about 150 eastern bishops, with no western bishops and no Roman legates. The creed associated with it, first attested at Chalcedon (451), added a confession of the Holy Spirit (`trinity-and-god` 3.3). Its **canon 3** ranked Constantinople second after Rome as "New Rome", a claim that returns in [canon 28](#canon-28-of-chalcedon).

*Introduced:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md)

### Ambrose and Theodosius

The bishop of Milan's refusal to celebrate before the emperor after the massacre at Thessalonica, 390.

After a riot at Thessalonica killed the military commander, troops massacred citizens (number unknown; Theodoret, about sixty years later, reports 7,000 were said to have died). Ambrose wrote a **private** letter (*Letter* 51, NPNF II/10: "you alone may read") refusing to offer the sacrifice if Theodosius intended to be present, until he repented; Theodosius did public penance before Christmas 390 (well attested). The church-door confrontation comes from the church historians of the 440s, Sozomen and at greatest length Theodoret; Neil McLynn (*Ambrose of Milan*, 1994) treats it as pious fiction and the penance as a negotiated settlement. It became the standard precedent for a bishop judging an emperor, picked up by Gelasius.

*Introduced:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md)

### Council of Ephesus 431

The third ecumenical council, which on the day was one of two rival assemblies.

Nestorius, bishop of Constantinople from 428, objected to *Theotokos*; a Roman synod condemned him in 430. Theodosius II called a council at Ephesus. Cyril of Alexandria opened it on **22 June 431** without waiting for John of Antioch and the Syrians, over the protest of the emperor's official, and deposed Nestorius that day; John arrived days later and deposed Cyril. The Roman legates arrived **10 July** and endorsed Cyril's session. The emperor at first confirmed both depositions, then abandoned Nestorius (exile). Cyril and John reconciled on the **Formula of Reunion** (433). Its authority came from reception and imperial choice, not clean procedure, which is why 449, procedurally no worse, could be undone.

*Introduced:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md)

### Robber Synod

The second council of Ephesus (August 449), later repudiated.

Eutyches, a monk of Constantinople who taught one nature after the union, was condemned by Flavian in 448. At Ephesus in August 449 Dioscorus of Alexandria presided, restored Eutyches, refused to have Leo's Tome read, and deposed Flavian, who died soon after, reportedly from ill-treatment. Leo, writing to the empress Pulcheria in July 451, called it a *latrocinium* (Letter 95); the name stuck. Third of three Alexandrian depositions of a bishop of Constantinople (Theophilus deposed Chrysostom 403; Cyril, Nestorius 431; Dioscorus, Flavian 449).

*Introduced:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md)

### Council of Chalcedon 451

The council that reversed 449, received Leo's Tome, and lost Egypt and Syria.

**8 October–1 November 451**, called by Marcian (married to Pulcheria) after Theodosius II's death (450). Several hundred bishops, almost all eastern; Leo's legates, led by Paschasinus of Lilybaeum, held first place, while imperial officials ran the proceedings. Deposed Dioscorus, received the [Tome of Leo](#tome-of-leo), and defined one and the same Christ known in two natures, without confusion or change, without division or separation (content: [`christology`](../christology/syllabus.md) 2.3; level from [`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md)). Passed [canon 28](#canon-28-of-chalcedon). The split that followed was a process, not an act of 451.

*Introduced:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md)

### Tome of Leo

Leo I's letter of 449 to Flavian on the two natures of Christ, read and received at Chalcedon.

Refused a reading at Ephesus 449. At Chalcedon the acts (NPNF II/14) record the acclamation "Peter has spoken thus through Leo ... Leo and Cyril taught the same thing". The Illyrian bishops questioned some passages; a committee under Anatolius of Constantinople examined it against Cyril's writings before the bishops affirmed it one by one. So the record shows the council honoured it in Petrine language; it does not show on what ground they accepted it (Peter's heir had spoken, or it matched Cyril). The record supports both.

*Introduced:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md)

### Canon 28 of Chalcedon

The canon giving Constantinople rank second after Rome, grounded in imperial status, which Leo refused to confirm.

Passed at a late session **with the legates absent**. As the lesson summarizes it: Constantinople gets privileges equal to old Rome's and rank second after it; both sees' privileges are grounded in their status as imperial cities; Constantinople gains the right to ordain metropolitans in Pontus, Asia and Thrace. The legates protested the next day; Leo refused to confirm it in letters of 452; the East kept it in force anyway. Text: NPNF II/14 (canons); Leo's letters: NPNF II/12. (Close reading of the canon and of Leo's grounds for rejecting it is Boss 2; outline only here.)

*Introduced:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md)

### Acacian schism

The break between Rome and Constantinople over the *Henotikon*, 484–519.

Zeno's *Henotikon* (482) set Chalcedon aside to reunite the miaphysites; Rome excommunicated Acacius of Constantinople (484). In it Pope **Gelasius** wrote to Emperor Anastasius (494) that the world is ruled by two, "the sacred authority of the priests and the royal power" (tr. J. H. Robinson, 1905): he concedes the emperor's supremacy "from heaven" in public order and claims more weight for priests because they answer for kings at the divine judgment, and obedience above all to the see "the Most High ordained to be above all others". Here the letter is a move in a quarrel over Chalcedon; the theory is [`history-of-political-thought` 2.5](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/02-05-marsilius-and-conciliarism.md). Don't read Gelasius as claiming to rule the emperor's affairs.

*Introduced:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md)

### Three Chapters

Writings Justinian condemned to win back the miaphysites, and the pope he coerced to agree.

The person and works of Theodore of Mopsuestia and certain writings of Theodoret and Ibas, which Chalcedon had not condemned. Vigilius brought to Constantinople (arriving about the start of 547); condemned them in his *Iudicatum* (548), withdrew it under Western protest, took refuge in a church at Chalcedon; in May 553 refused, in his first *Constitutum*, to condemn the dead. The council (Constantinople II, 5 May–2 June 553) met without him, condemned the Chapters, and struck his name from the diptychs. He gave way in December 553 and again (second *Constitutum*) in February 554; died at Syracuse on the way home, 555. The miaphysites were not won back, and parts of northern Italy broke with Rome over the Chapters for generations.

*Introduced:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md)

### Life of Antony

Athanasius's life of the desert hermit, the book that carried monasticism West.

Written c. 357–362 by the bishop of Alexandria, during the Arian crisis, for monks abroad (tr. H. Ellershaw, NPNF II/4, 1892). Antony (c. 251–356): heard Matthew 19:21 at about eighteen to twenty, apprenticed himself to an old ascetic, shut himself in a fort at Pispir around 285 for about twenty years; disciples colonized the desert (*Life* 14); died 356 at about 105. The *Life* has him defer to bishops (67), denounce the Arians in Alexandria (69, usually dated 338), and be "daily a martyr to his conscience" after the persecutions (47); the ascetic takes the martyr's place. It does not claim he was the first monk (he learned from an old hermit and placed his sister with virgins, ch. 3). Latin by Evagrius of Antioch before 374; the Trier officials' conversion story reached Augustine in 386 (*Confessions* 8.6). See also [Rubenson and Brakke on Antony](#rubenson-and-brakke-on-antony).

*Introduced:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md)

### Council of Orange 529

The regional synod that ended the Gallic dispute over grace.

**3 July 529**, led by Caesarius of Arles, a former monk of Lérins; confirmed by Pope Boniface II in 530 or 531. Its canons name no one. What it defined and at what weight belong to [`creation-grace-last-things`](../creation-grace-last-things/syllabus.md) 3.2; here it is the end of the [Massilian](#massilians) quarrel as an event.

*Introduced:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md)

### Rule of the Master

The anonymous, longer rule that Benedict abridged and revised.

c. 500–530. Since Augustin Genestout's 1940 argument and Adalbert de Vogüé's editions, specialists hold that Benedict's Rule depends on it, shown by comparing the texts (shared and abridged passages). Benedict's silence about it in chapter 73 does not count against dependence: that chapter is further reading for advanced monks, not a list of sources.

*Introduced:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md)

### Rule of Benedict

The rule that became the Western monastic norm, three centuries after it was written.

Benedict (c. 480–c. 547) is known only from Book 2 of Gregory the Great's *Dialogues* (c. 593–594). Monte Cassino traditionally founded 529; the Rule dated c. 530 to c. 560, disputed. A synthesis, not a first founding: it abridges the [Rule of the Master](#rule-of-the-master), and its last chapter (73; tr. O. H. Blair, 1906) points readers to Cassian's *Conferences* and *Institutes*, the Lives of the Fathers and "the Rule of our holy Father Basil" (read in Rufinus's Latin of 397). Chapter 48 calls idleness the enemy of the soul; "*Ora et labora*" is a later motto, not in the Rule. It became the norm only when the Carolingian synods of Aachen (816–817) imposed it; before that, mixed rules.

*Introduced:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md)

### Gregory's mission to Kent

The Roman mission to the English, and the model of top-down conversion.

Gregory I (pope 590–604; former prefect of Rome, monk on the Caelian, envoy at Constantinople) sent Augustine, prior of his monastery, with nearly forty monks (Bede's count) in 596; they landed on Thanet in **597**. Aethelberht of Kent already had a Frankish Christian queen, Bertha, with her own bishop; he was baptized (year uncertain) and gave them Canterbury. Gregory told Eulogius of Alexandria that more than ten thousand English were *reported* baptized at Christmas 597 (Register VIII.29; VIII.30 in the Schaff translation). Reinforcements 601. **June 601:** Gregory urges Aethelberht to "overthrow the structures of the temples" (Bede I.32). **18 July 601** (corrected manuscript tradition; Sellar's note following Plummer): the letter to Mellitus (Bede I.30): destroy the idols but not the temples; consecrate them, keep the feasts without sacrifice, because one rises "by degrees or steps, and not by leaps". A change of mind, or one line for a king and another for missionaries. Not religious tolerance: pedagogy by stages. **616:** on Aethelberht's death his son Eadbald refused baptism; those who had believed "for favour or fear of the king" went back (II.5); the East Saxons too, and Mellitus was driven from London. Kent recovered within a few years; London did not for decades. Gregory elsewhere rejected forced baptism of Jews (Register I.47, 591) yet told a Sardinian bishop to weigh pagan peasants down with heavier payments (IV.26) and punish idol-worshipping slaves with beatings (IX.65).

*Introduced:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md)

### Synod of Whitby

A Northumbrian king's choice between Roman and Irish usage, 664.

The Irish stream (Columba's Iona, 563; Columbanus in Gaul and Bobbio from about 590; Aidan's Lindisfarne c. 635) and the Roman kept Easter on different dates. King Oswiu called a synod; Wilfrid argued for Rome, Colman for Iona. Bede has the king decide for Peter, since Peter held the keys of heaven and Columba did not (III.25). A king settled a Church question, and the Roman see was not in the room.

*Introduced:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md)

### Boniface

The Anglo-Saxon monk who organized a German Church loyal to Rome under Frankish protection.

Wynfrith, a West Saxon monk, named Boniface when Gregory II commissioned him in 719. Around 723 felled the oak sacred to Donar at Geismar in Hesse (known from Willibald's *Life*, c. 768, some forty-five years later, written to honour a martyr). Killed by Frisians at Dokkum, 5 June 754. Admitted to Daniel of Winchester that without the Frankish ruler's protection he could neither govern his Church nor stop pagan rites.

*Introduced:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md)

### Massacre of Verden

Charlemagne's execution of Saxons at Verden on the Aller, 782.

The Royal Frankish Annals: after a Saxon rising destroyed a Frankish army in 782, Charles had **4,500** Saxons put to death in a single day. The number is disputed: an old emendation turning "beheaded" into "relocated" is now generally rejected; some historians (Matthias Becher among them) think a much smaller number of executions came with mass deportations; others accept the annalist's figure. Part of the Saxon wars, 772–804.

*Introduced:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md)

### Capitulatio de partibus Saxoniae

Charlemagne's law for conquered Saxony, the clearest case of forced conversion in the period.

Usually dated 782 or 785 (Yitzhak Hen argued for c. 795, which few accept); tr. D. C. Munro, *Translations and Reprints* VI.5 (1900). Chapters 3–13 each set death: among them hiding unbaptized and wishing to remain pagan (ch. 8), burning the dead, eating meat in Lent out of contempt, disloyalty to the king. Chapter 14: secret mortal crimes confessed voluntarily to a priest are freed from death on his testimony. Chapter 19: parents delaying an infant's baptism beyond a year are fined by rank. It imposed the tithe. Widukind accepted baptism in 785. Alcuin, in letters of about 796, argued faith is an act of the will and cannot be compelled, preaching must precede baptism, and the tithe had destroyed the Saxons' faith. Within two generations: Corvey (822) and the *Heliand* (c. 830); a century later Saxon kings led Latin Christendom. The Church's later judgment: *Dignitatis Humanae* (1965) §10 and §12 (weight: [`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)).

*Introduced:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md)

### Slavonic liturgy

The liturgy in the Slavs' own tongue brought to Moravia by Cyril and Methodius.

Prince Rastislav asked Constantinople in **863** for teachers who could preach in Slavonic. Constantine (who took the name Cyril as a monk shortly before his death in 869) and Methodius came with an alphabet and a Slavonic liturgy. Pope Hadrian II approved it; Methodius defended it again before John VIII in 880. Frankish clergy fought it; after Methodius died (885) his disciples were expelled from Moravia and carried the Slavonic liturgy to Bulgaria.

*Introduced:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md)

### Arab conquests

The conquest of the southern and eastern Mediterranean, 634–711, fast; the conversion that followed, slow.

**East:** Damascus 635; the Yarmuk 636; Jerusalem surrendered to Umar (sources 636–638; most modern histories 637 or 638), terms negotiated by Patriarch Sophronius; Antioch in the same years; Alexandria capitulated by treaty 641, garrison left 642; Persia over the same decade. Three of five patriarchal sees under the caliph within ten years of Muhammad's death (632), leaving Rome and Constantinople the only patriarchates under Christian rulers. **West:** North Africa over two generations, Carthage for good in 698; Spain from 711; checked near Poitiers 732; siege of Constantinople failed 717–718; Sicily 827–902 (Taormina); raiders sacked St Peter's outside Rome's walls in 846. **Egypt:** the conquest reversed the balance: the Coptic patriarch Benjamin, in hiding from the imperial patriarch Cyrus, got a safe conduct from Amr ibn al-As and returned c. 643–644; Michael the Syrian (twelfth century) could describe the conquest as deliverance from Roman cruelty (late, partisan). **Christians in the caliph's service:** Sarjun ibn Mansur headed Syria's fiscal administration until Abd al-Malik dismissed him around 700; his son **John of Damascus** (c. 675–c. 749), later a monk at Mar Saba, defended icons from outside the iconoclast empire, and his *On Heresies* (chapter 100 or 101) treats Islam as a Christian heresy.

*Introduced:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-rise-of-islam-and-the-christian-mediterranean.md)

### Pact of Umar

The classic statement of dhimmi obligations, attributed to Umar I but a later codification.

Lists no new churches, no public crosses, restrained bells, distinctive dress, no hindering of conversion to Islam. Tradition attributes it to Umar I (634–644); most modern scholars doubt it: Mark Cohen found no surviving text earlier than the tenth or eleventh century, and several historians think the rules grew up piecemeal. Evidence for what jurists later thought dhimmi status should be, not a record of 638.

*Introduced:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-rise-of-islam-and-the-christian-mediterranean.md)

### Martyrs of Cordoba

Forty-eight Christians executed at Cordoba, 850–859, most for publicly denouncing Muhammad.

The law made blasphemy against the Prophet and apostasy from Islam capital. Most of the executed had gone before a judge and denounced Muhammad; others were children of mixed marriages, legally Muslim, who professed Christianity (apostasy in law). Not refusals of forced conversion. A council of bishops convened at the emir's order in 852 discouraged seeking martyrdom; the priest Eulogius, the martyrs' champion and chronicler, denounced the bishops; his ally Paul Albar lamented that young Christians wrote polished Arabic and hardly a Latin letter. **Readings:** Jessica Coope, protest against assimilation into Arabic culture; Kenneth Wolf, a penitential ideal of martyrdom, separating the martyrs' motives from Eulogius's purposes. Either way, a community under threat of disappearing by accommodation, not by force; nearly all we know comes from the partisans Eulogius and Albar.

*Introduced:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-rise-of-islam-and-the-christian-mediterranean.md)

### Donation of Constantine

A forged imperial grant giving the pope supremacy over all churches and rule of Rome and the West.

"Constantine", cured and baptized by Sylvester, grants (tr. E. F. Henderson, 1910) two things: an **ecclesiastical** claim (supremacy over Antioch, Alexandria, Constantinople, Jerusalem and all churches) and a **temporal** one (the palace, Rome, "all the provinces ... of Italy or of the western regions"). Forged between about 750 and the mid-ninth century; place disputed (Rome in the Frankish-alliance years is the usual answer; Johannes Fried argues ninth-century Francia). It did **not** create the Papal States: no source shows it in the bargains of 754–756, and its first explicit papal use is Leo IX's letter to the patriarch of Constantinople in 1053–54. Doubted by Nicholas of Cusa (1430s); exposed by Lorenzo Valla (1440; see [Internal criticism](#internal-criticism)); rejected independently by Reginald Pecock around 1450; Luther read Valla in Hutten's printed edition in 1520. Good evidence of what someone in the eighth or ninth century wanted to be true.

*Introduced:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md)

### Frankish alliance and the anointing of Pepin

How the popes traded Byzantine masters for Frankish protectors, 726–774.

Iconoclasm from about 726–730 (the formal edict of 726 is doubted); Gregory II resisted and Gregory III's Roman synod condemned it in 731; Leo III confiscated papal estates in Sicily and Calabria and transferred southern Italy and Illyricum to Constantinople (date disputed). Aistulf's Lombards took Ravenna in 751; Constantinople sent envoys, not troops. **751:** Zacharias, asked by Pepin whether the powerless Merovingian should keep the title, answered (Royal Frankish Annals, written about forty years later) that it was better to call king the man who held the power; Pepin was elected and anointed. **754:** Stephen II (sometimes numbered III) met Pepin at Ponthion (January), then at Saint-Denis (July) anointed Pepin and his sons and named them *patricius Romanorum*; no pope had anointed a king before. The Franks elected Pepin; the papacy gave sanction and anointing, and their weight is argued. Until 731 popes still waited for the exarch's approval of their election.

*Introduced:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md)

### Donation of Pepin

Pepin's grant of the exarchate and Pentapolis to the pope, 756: the beginning of the Papal States.

After two campaigns against Aistulf, Pepin handed the pope the cities of the exarchate and the Pentapolis; the papal biography says their keys were laid at St Peter's tomb. Legally Byzantine territory. Charlemagne confirmed the grant after conquering the Lombard kingdom in 774. The Papal States lasted eleven centuries, until 1870 ([8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)).

*Introduced:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md)

### Coronation of Charlemagne

Leo III's crowning of Charles as emperor in St Peter's, Christmas Day 800.

Leo III was assaulted in Rome in 799 and fled to Charles at Paderborn. In December 800 Charles held an assembly in Rome where Leo swore an oath clearing himself; two days later, at Mass in St Peter's, Leo crowned him. The throne at Constantinople was held by a woman, Irene, and some in the West counted it vacant. Einhard's later report of how Charles took the title is disputed. (Close reading of Einhard, and the rival readings of Charles's reaction, are Boss 3; outline only here.)

*Introduced:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md)

### Pseudo-Isidorean Decretals

Ninth-century Frankish forgeries that protected bishops by routing their cases to Rome.

Forged in the province of Reims, probably in the 840s, by Frankish clergy to protect accused bishops against archbishops and kings; enlarging Roman jurisdiction was the means, not the aim. Not a papal forgery. They carried the Donation with them. Whether Nicholas I knew or used them is disputed.

*Introduced:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md)

### Diploma Ottonianum

Otto I's confirmation of the papal lands, with an imperial check on papal elections, 962.

John XII, pressed by Berengar II, called in Otto I and crowned him emperor on **2 February 962**. The *Diploma* of **13 February 962** confirmed the papal lands and required a newly elected pope to give pledges to the emperor's envoys before consecration. When John turned against him, Otto had a Roman synod depose John in late 963; John died May 964.

*Introduced:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md)

### Saeculum obscurum

The "iron century" (904–964), when Roman noble families controlled the papacy.

After Carolingian power collapsed (887–888) no protector remained. Pope Formosus's corpse was put on trial in 897. From 904 the family of Theophylact, his wife Theodora and their daughter Marozia dominated elections; Marozia's grandson Octavian became John XII in 955, still young; Otto I deposed him (963). **Sources:** much comes from Liutprand of Cremona (*History of Otto*; *Antapodosis*), Otto's bishop and envoy, who sat in the synod of 963 and wrote to justify it. His report separates what witnesses saw (a deacon ordained in a stable, bribes) from sexual charges they "knew" but had not seen. He calls John XI Sergius III's son by Marozia; Flodoard makes John XI Alberic II's brother, implying a different father. The political structure is independently attested; each scandal is testimony to be weighted. Cardinal Baronius (c. 1600) named the century iron, leaden and dark, relying mainly on Liutprand. A hostile source is not a false source.

*Introduced:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md)

### Cluny and Sutri

The reform began with a monastery given to Rome and an emperor who removed three popes.
