**Cluny, 910:** Duke William of Aquitaine gave his foundation to the apostles Peter and Paul, that is to Rome, forbidding any lord or bishop to interfere; popes exempted it from the local bishop (998, extended 1024). **Sutri, December 1046:** Henry III had three claimants set aside (Sylvester III, Gregory VI, Benedict IX) and installed his bishop of Bamberg as Clement II, who crowned him emperor at Christmas; Hildebrand followed Gregory VI into exile. **Leo IX (1049–54):** another German appointee; travelling councils across Italy, France and Germany (Reims, October 1049). The reform began with an emperor as patron; his son became its enemy.

*Introduced:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)

### Papal election decree of 1059

Nicholas II's decree giving the choice of pope to the cardinal bishops.

Lateran, April 1059. The cardinal bishops choose; the rest of the clergy and the people assent; the emperor keeps an undefined right. Gregory VII's acclamation by the Roman crowd on 22 April 1073 was hard to square with it, which gave Henry's "not pope" charge a footing (Brixen, 1080).

*Introduced:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)

### Dictatus Papae

Twenty-seven headings on papal power entered in Gregory VII's register, March 1075.

Between letters of 3 and 4 March 1075; bare headings, no argument, no addressee; never sent or published (tr. E. F. Henderson, 1910). Among them: 3 (he alone can depose or reinstate bishops), 12 (he may depose emperors), 16 (no synod general without his order), 19 (he may be judged by no one), 22 (the Roman *church* has never erred), 27 (he may absolve subjects from fealty to wicked men); 23 names Pope Symmachus and Ennodius of Pavia. An agenda Gregory dictated, or headings for a canon-law collection (Cardinal Deusdedit's, 1086/7, matches closely). Evidence of what Gregory's curia thought, not a decree anyone obeyed; proposition 22 is not a definition of papal infallibility. Says nothing of investiture.

*Introduced:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)

### Canossa

Henry IV's penance before Gregory VII at Matilda of Tuscany's castle, January 1077.

**The break:** Henry named his own archbishop of Milan and bishops for Fermo and Spoleto; Gregory's letter of December 1075 rebuked him, and for keeping excommunicated councillors. At **Worms, 24 January 1076**, Henry and his bishops renounced "Hildebrand, at present not pope but false monk" (Henry: "subject to the judgment of God alone", unless he strayed from the faith). In **February 1076** Gregory's synod excommunicated Henry, withdrew the rule of Germany and Italy, and absolved his subjects. In October 1076 the princes gave Henry a year. **Canossa:** Henry waited from 25 January, absolved on the 28th. Gregory's own letter of 28 January to the princes: "with bare feet and clad in wool, he continued for three days to stand before the gate". The penance is not legend. Absolution restored communion and removed the princes' deadline; it did not in terms restore the kingdom. Rudolf of Rheinfelden elected anti-king March 1077. **After:** second ban March 1080; synod of Brixen (June 1080) declared Gregory's election invalid and chose Wibert of Ravenna as "Clement III"; Henry took Rome March 1084 and was crowned emperor by Clement; Gregory's Normans sacked the city; Gregory died at Salerno, 25 May 1085 ("I have loved justice and hated iniquity; therefore I die in exile": traditional attribution via partisan accounts such as Paul of Bernried, c. 1128). Bismarck's "we will not go to Canossa" (Reichstag, 14 May 1872) gave the humiliation its modern form. **What it meant:** Johannes Fried (*Canossa: Entlarvung einer Legende*, 2012): a negotiated peace, the humiliation a later construction; Gerd Althoff and Stefan Weinfurter rejected his reading. Penance and absolution are secure; whether they were a defeat depends on what Henry went to get.

*Introduced:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)

### Concordat of Worms

The 1122 compromise that split the symbols of investiture between Church and crown.

**23 September 1122**; confirmed by the First Lateran Council (1123). Preceded by Paschal II's failed plan of 1111 (bishops to surrender royal lands if the king gave up investiture), Henry V's extorted grant of 1111 (repudiated 1112), and England's working bargain of 1107. Henry V renounces "all investiture through ring and staff" and grants "canonical election and free consecration" (tr. Henderson). Calixtus II grants that German elections take place in the emperor's presence; in a disputed election the emperor, with the metropolitan and provincial bishops, may back the side with the better right; the elect receives the *regalia* by the sceptre (Henderson's "lance" mistranslates *sceptrum*), **before consecration in Germany**, within six months after in Italy and Burgundy. The German timing kept a real veto. Both sides could claim a win; that is why it held. It changed the symbols and the order of steps, not royal influence over bishops.

*Introduced:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)

### East-West schism

The separation of the Latin and Greek churches: an estrangement over centuries, not a break in 1054.

Causes tangled: language (by 1000 few Latins read Greek, few Greeks Latin), rival pictures of authority (Rome as Peter's see with care for all churches; Constantinople as one of five patriarchates governing through councils), and remembered injuries. Rifts and healings alternate: iconoclasm and the Illyricum transfer (726–843); Photius and Nicholas I (863–867), healed 879–880; the filioque sung at Rome (1014); 1054; the massacre of Latins (1182) and the sack (1204); union at Lyons (1274), repudiated 1282; union at Florence (1439), not received; Constantinople falls (1453); excommunications lifted (1965). The healings are acts of rulers and councils, the rifts are felt by clergy and people. Steven Runciman (*The Eastern Schism*, 1955): intercommunion into the twelfth century. Henry Chadwick (*East and West: The Making of a Rift in the Church*, 2003): a process from apostolic times to Florence. **Lyons II (1274):** Michael VIII, who retook the city in 1261 and feared a Western crusade to restore the Latins, had his envoys accept Roman faith and primacy; Andronikos II repudiated it after Michael's death (December 1282).

*Introduced:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md)

### Photian schism

The quarrel over Photius's patriarchate and the Bulgarian mission, 863–880, which was healed.

Photius, a layman, was made patriarch (858) after Ignatius was deposed; Nicholas I rejected him in 863. In 867 Photius's encyclical condemned Latin missionaries in Bulgaria and the filioque, and his synod declared Nicholas deposed. A council of 869–870 condemned Photius; a council of 879–880 reconciled him with John VIII (second patriarchate 877–886). **Francis Dvornik** (*The Photian Schism: History and Legend*, 1948): Photius was reconciled, and 879–880 stood in Western lists of councils until eleventh-century canonists replaced it with 869–870 as the eighth; the older Western account held Photius a schismatic condemned more than once. The crux is factual, decided by the papal letters and the canonical collections.

*Introduced:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md); [4.2](lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md)

### Excommunications of 1054

Personal sentences exchanged between papal legates and a patriarch, later made a symbol of the schism.

Leo IX's legates, Humbert of Silva Candida, Frederick of Lorraine and Peter of Amalfi, came to negotiate an alliance against the Normans after Cerularius had closed the Latin churches in Constantinople. On **16 July 1054** they laid a bull on the altar of Hagia Sophia excommunicating Cerularius, Leo of Ohrid and their adherents; a synod on **20 July** excommunicated the legates. Leo IX had died on **19 April**, so the legates' commission is doubtful. Emperor Constantine IX stayed on good terms with the legates; contemporaries barely noticed. Fails all three dating tests: persons not churches, doubtful authority, not received. Leo IX's letter to the patriarch (1053–54) is the first explicit papal use of the Donation of Constantine. **Lifted:** Paul VI and Athenagoras met in Jerusalem (January 1964); on **7 December 1965** a joint declaration read in Rome and Constantinople regretted the offences and committed the sentences to oblivion. It removed a barrier; it did not restore communion.

*Introduced:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md)

### Sack of Constantinople 1204

The Fourth Crusade's capture and three-day sack of the Christian capital.

Preceded by the massacre of the city's Latin residents, mostly Genoese and Pisans, in 1182 (toll disputed, hundreds to thousands). On **12–13 April 1204** the diverted crusade took the city and sacked it for three days: churches stripped, Hagia Sophia included, killing and rape. Innocent III had forbidden attacks on Christians; at first he read the conquest as a providential chance for union, but in July 1205, fully informed, rebuked his legate: the Greeks now had reason to detest the Latins "more than dogs". He still confirmed a Venetian, Thomas Morosini, as Latin patriarch; Greek clergy were replaced by Latins in their own cathedrals. **Afterwards:** in Athens, 4 May 2001, John Paul II named it a tragedy and asked forgiveness for the sins of Catholics against the Orthodox (4.3: expressed "deep regret" that Latin Christians had done it).

*Introduced:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md)

### Council of Florence

The council whose decree of union with the Greeks (1439) was signed and never received.

Decree *Laetentur caeli*, **6 July 1439**. Its primacy clause (lesson's translation): the Apostolic See and Roman Pontiff hold primacy over the whole world; the pope is Peter's successor, vicar of Christ, head of the whole Church, and has "full power ... to feed, rule and govern the universal Church; as is also contained in the acts of the ecumenical councils and in the sacred canons"; the patriarchs' canonical order renewed (Constantinople second, then Alexandria, Antioch, Jerusalem) "saving all their privileges and rights". The two qualifying clauses can be read as confirming or as measuring the full power; the Greeks bargained over the wording (down to whether rights were saved with or without "all"); the ambiguity was partly deliberate. Signed by John VIII and nearly all Greek bishops; Patriarch Joseph II had died on 10 June. Mark of Ephesus refused; signers withdrew at home; Moscow rejected it and imprisoned Isidore of Kiev; proclaimed in Hagia Sophia only on 12 December 1452; the city fell 29 May 1453. Rome never revoked it. Whether a union existed 1439–1453 depends on the criterion (signature or reception), an ecclesiological question to flag, not decide (precedent: Nicaea II, 787, denied the iconoclast council of 754 the name ecumenical partly because Rome and the other patriarchs took no part). Vatican I renewed the definition (*Pastor Aeternus*, ch. 3), which belongs to [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md).

*Introduced:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md)

### First Crusade

Urban II's armed pilgrimage to Jerusalem, 1095–1099.

**March 1095:** envoys of Alexios I Komnenos asked Urban's council at Piacenza for help against the Seljuks (probably troops he could command, not a mass pilgrimage). **27 November 1095:** Urban preached the expedition at the close of the Council of Clermont; every surviving version of the speech (Fulcher of Chartres, Robert the Monk and others) was written after Jerusalem fell in 1099. New: a pope, not a king, summoned the army, and fighting was offered as penance. Urban was a Cluniac monk and Gregorian reformer. **15 July 1099:** Jerusalem stormed after a three-year march by way of Constantinople and Antioch; much of its Muslim and Jewish population killed (see [Jerusalem 1099 death toll](#jerusalem-1099-death-toll)). Four crusader states 1098–1109. (The speech versions are Boss 4; outline only.)

*Introduced:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-the-crusades.md)

### Rhineland massacres

The killing of Jewish communities in the Rhineland by crusading bands, May–June 1096.

Before the main armies left, crusading bands and townsmen attacked Jewish communities. **Speyer (3 May):** about a dozen killed; Bishop John sheltered the rest and punished some attackers. **Worms (18 and 25 May):** about 800 dead by the Hebrew chronicles. **Mainz (27 May):** bands under Count Emicho broke into Archbishop Ruthard's palace; about 1,100 dead by the Hebrew chronicle, about 700 by Albert of Aachen; the Hebrew chronicle says Ruthard's men fled first and he fled too. Many Jews killed their own families rather than be forcibly baptized. Known from three Hebrew chronicles and Christian writers (Albert of Aachen, tr. Krey 1921, condemns the killers for greed and forced conversion while still calling the Jews "opposed to Christ"). No papal text ordered it and bishops at Speyer, Worms, Mainz and Cologne tried to shelter Jews; but it was done by crusaders in the language of the crusade, and protection often failed. Total estimates start at about two thousand; the figures cannot be checked closely.

*Introduced:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-the-crusades.md)

### Military orders

Professed religious who fought: the permanent garrison of the Latin East.

The Hospitallers received papal protection in 1113. The Templars were founded about 1119–1120 and given a rule at the Council of Troyes (1129); suppressed in 1312 ([Vox in excelso](#vox-in-excelso)).

*Introduced:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-the-crusades.md)

### Later crusades and the fall of Acre

From Hattin to the loss of the last Latin city, 1187–1291.

Edessa fell 1144; Second Crusade 1147–49. **Hattin, 4 July 1187:** Saladin destroyed the kingdom's army; Jerusalem surrendered 2 October (those who could pay ransom left; many who could not were enslaved). Third Crusade 1189–1192 regained the coast, not Jerusalem. Fourth Crusade sacked Constantinople (1204). From 1209 crusades were declared against heretics in the West ([Albigensian Crusade](#albigensian-crusade)). **Acre** fell to the Mamluks, 18 May 1291.

*Introduced:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-the-crusades.md)

### Day of Pardon

John Paul II's liturgy of confession of the Church's sins, 12 March 2000.

Seven confessions of sins by the Church's members, among them sins "committed in the service of truth" (naming intolerance and violence against dissenters; 6.5 quotes it as violence "in the service of the truth") and sins against the people of Israel; the seventh prayed for minors who were victims of abuse only in general terms. The crusades were **not named**: historians place them under those general categories, but the text does not. It came two years before Boston and was not a response to the abuse crisis. It followed a Vatican symposium of historians on the Inquisition (October 1998; proceedings ed. Agostino Borromeo, 2004), and John Paul II's letter presenting them (15 June 2004, section 2) says asking forgiveness requires first knowing the facts exactly. Separately, in Athens on 4 May 2001 he named the sack of 1204. The course's honesty rule: an acknowledgement never substitutes for the facts, and does not stand in as the historians' verdict.

*Introduced:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-the-crusades.md); also [5.3](lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md), [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-spanish-and-roman-inquisitions.md), [9.3](lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)

### Innocent III and King John

The height of papal leverage: a king resigns his kingdom to the pope and holds it back as a fief.

Innocent III (elected 8 January 1198, died 16 July 1216). In the disputed German election of 1198 he judged between candidates and in 1201 backed Otto of Brunswick, on the ground that the empire derived its origin and final authority from the papacy. John refused Stephen Langton for Canterbury: interdict (March 1208), excommunication (November 1209). Facing French invasion and barons, John accepted terms at Dover on **13 May 1213** and on **15 May** resigned England and Ireland to the pope, receiving them back as a vassal for 1,000 marks a year. **Magna Carta** (June 1215): John appealed to his overlord; Innocent annulled it in *Etsi carissimus* (24 August 1215) as extorted from a crusader-vassal; the barons ignored him. After John's death the legate Guala Bicchieri co-sealed a revised reissue in Henry III's name (12 November 1216). The tribute lapsed for decades and Parliament repudiated it in 1366. The "sun and moon" letter (*Sicut universitatis conditor*, 1198; tr. Thatcher and McNeal, 1905): the royal power derives the *splendor* of its dignity from the pontifical authority; the metaphor pushes toward subordination, the words stop short of jurisdiction.

*Introduced:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-papal-monarchy-innocent-iii-to-boniface-viii.md)

### Fourth Lateran Council

Innocent III's great council of 1215, whose canons reached every parish.

Opened **11 November 1215**: some 400 bishops, over 800 abbots and priors, envoys of kings. Canon 1: defined the faith, using the term transubstantiation (doctrine: [`sacraments-and-liturgy`](../sacraments-and-liturgy/syllabus.md)). **Canon 3:** action against heresy: suspects who cannot clear themselves excommunicated, convicted heretics handed to the secular power, rulers who fail to act liable to have vassals released from allegiance, bishops to tour suspect parishes yearly taking sworn reports (not yet "the Inquisition"; papal inquisitors came in the 1230s). Canon 5: patriarchal order under Rome, with Latin patriarchs at Constantinople after 1204. **Canon 21** (*Omnis utriusque sexus*): annual confession to one's own priest and Easter communion; the practice was older, the universal law new. Canon 67: restrained "heavy and immoderate" Jewish usury ([`history-of-debt` 2.2](../history-of-debt/lessons/02-02-jewish-lenders-and-the-monti-di-pieta.md)). **Canon 68:** Jews and Muslims distinguished by dress, Jews barred from public in the last days of Holy Week; it set no form of badge.

*Introduced:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-papal-monarchy-innocent-iii-to-boniface-viii.md)

### Clericis laicos

Boniface VIII's bull forbidding lay taxation of clergy without papal consent, 1296.

**February 1296** (tr. Henderson, 1910): clergy who pay, and rulers who levy or receive, a share of clerical revenues "under any other name ... or clever pretense" **without the authority of that same chair** incur excommunication by the act itself. It reserves consent to the pope; it does not declare the clergy untaxable. Philip IV answered (August 1296) by banning export of money and goods from France. With the Colonna cardinals deposed (May 1297) and at war with him, Boniface retreated: ***Etsi de statu*** (31 July 1297) let the king tax his clergy without consulting Rome in case of necessity, the king to judge the necessity. August 1297: Louis IX canonized.

*Introduced:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-papal-monarchy-innocent-iii-to-boniface-viii.md)

### Unam Sanctam

Boniface VIII's bull of 18 November 1302 on the two swords and subjection to the Roman pontiff.

Second round with Philip IV: Bishop Bernard Saisset of Pamiers arrested and tried for treason (1301); *Ausculta fili* (5 December 1301) told Philip God had set the pope over kings and kingdoms (the king's ministers circulated a forged short version and a rude answer); the three estates backed Philip in Paris (April 1302), the clergy reluctantly; French defeat at Courtrai (July 1302); then *Unam Sanctam*. Here it is a move in that quarrel. Aftermath: Clement V's *Meruit* (1306) declared the bull placed France under the Roman Church no differently than before; *Rex gloriae* (1311) exonerated Philip's zeal and closed the posthumous heresy trial of Boniface. As political theory: [`history-of-political-thought` 2.5](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/02-05-marsilius-and-conciliarism.md); the status of its closing sentence: [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md) (don't grade it here). (The two swords passage and its rival readings are Boss 5; outline only.)

*Introduced:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-papal-monarchy-innocent-iii-to-boniface-viii.md)

### Outrage of Anagni

French agents' seizure of Boniface VIII, 7 September 1303.

In June 1303 the king's lawyers accused Boniface of heresy and demanded a council to judge him. On **7 September 1303**, the eve of the day Boniface meant to excommunicate Philip, Guillaume de Nogaret and Sciarra Colonna seized him at Anagni. The townspeople freed him after three days; he returned to Rome and died 11 October 1303. Boniface did not simply lose to force: he had retreated on taxation in 1297, and Philip had won his own clergy and estates first.

*Introduced:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-papal-monarchy-innocent-iii-to-boniface-viii.md)

### Parens scientiarum

Gregory IX's bull of 1231 confirming the Paris masters' rights after their strike.

A tavern brawl in 1229 ended with students killed by the crown's soldiers; the masters suspended teaching and dispersed. ***Parens scientiarum*** (**13 April 1231**) confirmed the masters' right to suspend lectures when wronged and to make statutes, and bound the chancellor to license qualified candidates. It protected and regulated a guild that already existed; it did not found the university. Bologna, a law school run by student guilds, had imperial protection from Barbarossa's *Habita* (c. 1155); in 1219 Honorius III gave the archdeacon of Bologna control of its degrees. The pope protected the universities from bishop and town and tied them to Rome.

*Introduced:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-friars-and-universities.md)

### Secular-mendicant controversy

The Paris masters' fight to exclude the friars from the university, settled by the pope in the friars' favour.

During the 1229 strike the bishop let the Dominican Roland of Cremona incept; Alexander of Hales became a Franciscan c. 1236 and brought his chair. Friars did not strike and answered to their orders. In **1253** the secular masters expelled the friar masters for refusing to join a strike. Innocent IV curbed the friars' pastoral privileges in late 1254 and died weeks later; Alexander IV reversed him in *Quasi lignum vitae* (14 April 1255), ordering the friars readmitted. William of Saint-Amour's *On the Dangers of the Last Times* (1256) cast the friars as heralds of Antichrist; condemned 1256, William banished from France. Aquinas and Bonaventure admitted to the masters' corporation, August 1257.

*Introduced:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-friars-and-universities.md)

### Condemnation of 1277

The bishop of Paris's list of 219 condemned propositions, as an event.

John XXI's letter of 18 January 1277 asked Bishop Stephen Tempier to investigate errors taught at Paris; on **7 March 1277** Tempier, working with theologians, condemned 219 propositions, some touching positions of Aquinas (who was not named). A local episcopal act, prompted by a papal letter. After Aquinas's canonization (18 July 1323), Bishop Stephen Bourret revoked it in 1325 so far as it touched him. What the articles said is [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 7.1](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/07-01-faith-reason-and-the-paris-crisis.md).

*Introduced:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-friars-and-universities.md)

### Exiit qui seminat

Nicholas III's bull of 14 August 1279 approving Franciscan poverty as taught by Christ.

Held that renouncing ownership of everything, singly and in common, was holy and taught by Christ; the friars had [use without ownership](#use-and-ownership), ownership lying with the Holy See unless the donor kept it. Rome owned the friars' houses and books, so the friars could own nothing. Dismantled by *Ad conditorem canonum* (1322).

*Introduced:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-friars-and-universities.md)

### Spiritual Franciscans

The rigorist Franciscan wing that demanded poor use, four of whom were burned in 1318.

Peter John Olivi, Ubertino da Casale, Angelo Clareno; insisted on *usus pauper*. John XXII demanded obedience in *Quorumdam exigit* (7 October 1317); on **7 May 1318** four friars who refused it were burned at Marseille.

*Introduced:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-friars-and-universities.md)

### Cum inter nonnullos

John XXII's bull of 12 November 1323 condemning the claim that Christ and the apostles owned nothing.

After the Franciscan general chapter at Perugia (1322) declared Christ and the apostles had owned nothing, and after *Ad conditorem canonum* (8 December 1322) ended papal ownership, *Cum inter nonnullos* declared heretical the "pertinacious" assertion that Christ and the apostles had nothing, individually or in common, or no right to sell or give what they had. It condemned a claim about Christ's legal condition, not the friars' vow. **Ockham:** summoned to Avignon in 1324, studied the bulls and concluded the pope was a heretic; on the night of **26 May 1328** fled with the minister general Michael of Cesena to Ludwig of Bavaria (excommunicated by John) at Pisa, then to Munich for life. Most Franciscans submitted. John's *Quia quorundam* (1324) rejected claims that *Exiit* was irreversible. See [Did John XXII reverse Exiit](#did-john-xxii-reverse-exiit).

*Introduced:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-friars-and-universities.md)

### Albigensian Crusade

The crusade Innocent III called against the heretics of Languedoc and their protectors, 1209–1229.

Called after the papal legate Peter of Castelnau was murdered in **January 1208**: the crusade indulgence turned inward. **Beziers, 22 July 1209:** the army (spiritual leader Arnaud Amalric, abbot of Citeaux and legate) took the town by assault and killed its people, including those sheltering in churches. Arnaud's own report put the dead at nearly 20,000; modern estimates of the whole population run to about 10,000–14,500 and some escaped, so the toll was in the thousands, fewer than he claimed. The massacre is not in doubt. Under Simon de Montfort a northern French conquest of the south; ended by the Treaty of Paris (1229), binding the count of Toulouse to pursue heresy. **Montsegur** fell March 1244; about 200 who refused to recant were burned. On "Kill them all", see [Kill them all](#kill-them-all).

*Introduced:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md)

### Papal inquisitors

Papal commissions to individual inquisitors, mostly friars: not yet a single institution.

"Inquisition" first names a procedure (*inquisitio*), not an office. Lucius III's *Ad abolendam* (Verona, 4 November 1184) condemned Waldensians and Cathars. Gregory IX's *Excommunicamus* (1231) set penalties; from 1233 he commissioned Dominicans for southern France. In 1245–1246 two inquisitors questioned more than five thousand people from the Lauragais at Toulouse. **Procedure** (from the manuals): general sermon; period of grace; suspects cited by name; interrogation written in Latin by a notary; names of others, feeding new citations; confess and abjure (penance: crosses, pilgrimage, prison) or be handed to the secular arm; relapse meant the secular arm; burning by lay authorities. The Church "relinquished" and never itself executed; the distinction was real to canonists and does not reduce its responsibility (Lateran IV canon 3 threatened rulers who failed to act). **Bernard Gui**, inquisitor at Toulouse 1307–1323, finished his manual the *Practica* probably by early 1324; for his figures see [Inquisition numbers](#inquisition-numbers). A central Roman office came only in 1542.

*Introduced:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md)

### Ad extirpanda

Innocent IV's bull of 15 May 1252 authorizing torture in heresy cases.

Issued after the inquisitor Peter of Verona was murdered in Lombardy (April 1252). Authorized the civil authorities of Italian cities to torture suspected heretics to extract confessions and names, with limits: no loss of limb and no danger to life. Lay officials, not friars, applied it. Roman law already used torture; the bull brought it into heresy proceedings with papal sanction.

*Introduced:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md)

### Talmud trial and the expulsions

The burning of the Talmud in Paris and the royal expulsions of Jews from England and France.

**Talmud:** Nicholas Donin, a convert, sent Gregory IX charges against the Talmud (1239); of the rulers the pope wrote to, only Louis IX acted. After a hearing in Paris in June 1240 where rabbis led by Yehiel of Paris answered Donin, some twenty-four cartloads of Hebrew manuscripts were burned in Paris in 1242. **Expulsions:** Edward I expelled the roughly 2,000–3,000 Jews of England by edict of **18 July 1290**; Philip IV expelled France's far larger community on **22 July 1306**, seizing its property and the debts owed to it. Royal acts with fiscal motives, not papal ones ([`history-of-debt` 2.2](../history-of-debt/lessons/02-02-jewish-lenders-and-the-monti-di-pieta.md)). Jews were not heretics (never baptized); inquisitors could act against them only in narrow cases such as converts who returned to Judaism.

*Introduced:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md)

### Fournier register

The record of Bishop Jacques Fournier's inquisition at Pamiers, 1318–1325.

Fournier, bishop of Pamiers and later Pope Benedict XII (from 1334): 578 interrogations over roughly seven years, five burned. The register survives in the Vatican Library (the syllabus's "Montaillou register"). Among the fullest inquisitors' records, and so central to what the [Cathar church debate](#cathar-church-debate) turns on: testimony gathered by fixed questions and written down in Latin by notaries.

*Introduced:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md)

### Avignon papacy

The papal residence at Avignon, 1309–1377, under seven southern French popes.

**Clement V** (Bertrand de Got, archbishop of Bordeaux), elected at Perugia 5 June 1305 after an eleven-month deadlock, crowned at Lyon, never went to Rome, settled at Avignon March 1309, under heavy pressure from Philip IV (demanding a posthumous trial of Boniface). Avignon belonged to the count of Provence, next to the papal Comtat Venaissin, until Clement VI bought it in 1348. **John XXII** (1316–34) and successors centralized appointments: reservations, annates, the Apostolic Camera. Urban V went to Rome in 1367 but returned in 1370. Catherine of Siena went to Avignon in June 1376; Gregory XI entered Rome 17 January 1377 and died there 27 March 1378. Petrarch (letter 1340–1353, tr. Robinson 1904): "the Babylon of the West", a moral and rhetorical charge, not a claim that the popes obeyed France; "Babylonian captivity" stuck. **Guillaume Mollat** (*Les Papes d'Avignon*, 1912), from the registers: popes ran their own policy, spent heavily reconquering the Papal States, kept planning a return; the novelty was centralizing and fiscal reach, not subservience. The Templars are the strongest evidence of French pressure. (Whether the Avignon popes were the crown's puppets is Boss 5; outline only.)

*Introduced:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-avignon-the-great-schism-and-the-councils.md)

### Vox in excelso

Clement V's bull suppressing the Knights Templar, announced 22 March 1312.

Philip IV had every Templar in France arrested on **13 October 1307**; confessions to heresy and obscenity were extracted under torture. Clement protested, then took over the process; his cardinals heard the leaders at Chinon (August 1308): a parchment rediscovered in the Vatican archives in 2001 appears to record their absolution (of named leaders who confessed and asked pardon; not an acquittal of the order, and its weight is argued). At the Council of Vienne (1311–12) most of the commission held the order should be allowed a defence and the evidence would not support condemnation. With Philip at Vienne, Clement suppressed it anyway "by apostolic provision", not judicial sentence; property to the Hospitallers. Jacques de Molay retracted his confession and was burned at Paris in March 1314 on the king's order. The Paris Temple had long served as a royal treasury; historians weigh how far money drove the arrests ([`history-of-debt` 2.4](../history-of-debt/lessons/02-04-lending-to-kings.md)).

*Introduced:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-avignon-the-great-schism-and-the-councils.md)

### Disputed election of 1378

The election of Urban VI, which the same cardinals later declared void, starting the Great Schism.

Sixteen cardinals, most French, entered conclave **7 April 1378** with a Roman crowd demanding a Roman or Italian pope. On **8 April** they elected Bartolomeo Prignano, archbishop of Bari, as Urban VI; when the crowd broke in they briefly presented an elderly Roman cardinal as pope. In the following weeks they enthroned and crowned Urban (18 April), asked him for favours, and announced his election to the princes. Urban proved harsh and abusive. By summer they withdrew to Anagni; in **August** declared the election void as made under fear; at **Fondi, 20 September**, elected Robert of Geneva as Clement VII (in Avignon by 1379). **The crux:** Urbanists must hold the cardinals' acts after the vote were free and ratified it; Clementists that fear lasted until they left Rome. Evidence that would move it: what cardinals did or wrote once beyond the crowd's reach, and whether the reversal tracked Urban's conduct rather than danger (a valid pope does not become invalid by being unpleasant). The official list counts the Roman line and lists Avignon and Pisan claimants as antipopes, but the *Annuario Pontificio* still printed Alexander V and John XXIII as popes in the early 1940s; Roncalli's choice of "John XXIII" (1958) settled Cossa's status in practice; the Alexander of 1492 had numbered himself VI. Noel Valois at first judged the question beyond history's power, later inclined to Rome. A settled tradition on a strong but not conclusive case.

*Introduced:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-avignon-the-great-schism-and-the-councils.md)

### Great Western Schism

Two, then three, rival lines of popes, 1378–1417, following political alliances.

Rome: Urban VI, Boniface IX, Innocent VII, Gregory XII. Avignon: Clement VII, Benedict XIII (Pedro de Luna). Around 1390: Rome had England, most of the Empire with Bohemia, Hungary, Poland, northern and central Italy, Flanders, Portugal from 1385, Naples from 1381; Avignon had France, Scotland, Savoy, Castile from 1381, Aragon from 1387, Navarre from 1390, Naples under Joanna I until 1381. The Hundred Years' War runs through it (England Roman, so Scotland Avignonese); rulers chose, clergy and people followed, university theologians mostly sided with their own country. **Pisa (1409):** cardinals from both sides; opened 25 March, deposed both popes 5 June, elected Alexander V 26 June; neither yielded, so three popes; John XXIII (Baldassare Cossa) succeeded Alexander in 1410. For no party did the answer involve doing without a pope.

*Introduced:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-avignon-the-great-schism-and-the-councils.md)

### Council of Constance

The council of 1414–18 that ended the Great Western Schism.

Called by John XXIII under pressure from King Sigismund; voted by "nations". John promised to resign, then fled **20 March 1415**. **6 April 1415:** [Haec Sancta](#haec-sancta). **29 May:** John deposed (by the council's own authority; he submitted). **4 July:** Gregory XII's envoy formally reconvoked the council in his name, then resigned for him. Benedict XIII, abandoned by the Spanish kingdoms (Treaty of Narbonne, December 1415), was tried and deposed **26 July 1417**. **9 October 1417:** [Frequens](#frequens). An electoral body of the cardinals plus thirty deputies of the nations chose Oddo Colonna as **Martin V, 11 November 1417**. Method: remove every doubt, then elect. **Hus:** came under Sigismund's safe-conduct; arrested 28 November 1414, before Sigismund arrived (Bohemians called it a breach at once); condemned and burned **6 July 1415** after trial; Sigismund acquiesced by January 1415; the decree that princes' safe-conducts do not bar trial for heresy came only on 23 September 1415. John Paul II expressed regret in 1999. Whether Gregory XII's reconvocation was the legal hinge or a courtesy is disputed (see the primacy table).

*Introduced:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-avignon-the-great-schism-and-the-councils.md)

### Haec Sancta

Constance's decree of 6 April 1415 that the council holds power immediately from Christ, which even the pope must obey.

Tr. J. H. Robinson, *Translations and Reprints* III.6: the council, "legitimately assembled in the Holy Ghost ... representing the Catholic Church militant, has its power immediately from Christ", and everyone, "even if it be the Papal dignity itself", must obey it in matters of "the faith and the healing of the said schism, and ... the general reformation of the Church of God, in head and members"; disobedience to it "or ... any other general council, legitimately summoned" is punishable. Its **standing is disputed**: a standing rule, an emergency measure, or invalidly enacted because only John XXIII's obedience was present in April 1415. Martin V's approval of what was done "in a conciliar way" (*conciliariter*) is ambiguous and part of the dispute. Don't assign it a doctrinal level: [`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md), [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md).

*Introduced:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-avignon-the-great-schism-and-the-councils.md)

### Frequens

Constance's decree requiring regular general councils, 9 October 1417.

Councils after five years, then seven, then every ten. **Basel** (1431) was convoked by Martin V under it. Basel quarrelled with Eugenius IV, who moved the council to Ferrara (1438) and then [Florence](#council-of-florence). The Basel rump deposed Eugenius and elected Felix V (1439); he abdicated in 1449 and the rump dissolved.

*Introduced:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-avignon-the-great-schism-and-the-councils.md)

### St Peters indulgence

The indulgence for rebuilding St Peter's whose preaching in 1517 set off Luther's protest.

Proclaimed by Julius II (who laid St Peter's foundation stone in 1506), renewed by Leo X: a normal instrument of church finance. **Albrecht's bargain:** Albrecht of Brandenburg, archbishop of Magdeburg and administrator of Halberstadt (1513), elected archbishop of Mainz (1514), holding three sees against canon law; the dispensation and fees were paid with money borrowed from the Fuggers (figures vary in the literature); a papal grant of 1515 let the indulgence be preached in his provinces with half the proceeds to Albrecht to repay them; a Fugger agent held a key to the chest. **Tetzel**, a Dominican, preached it in 1517; Frederick the Wise barred it from Saxony, but Tetzel preached at Juterbog from April and Wittenbergers went. The Catholic Encyclopedia (1912), following Nikolaus Paulus: Tetzel's teaching for the living was correct; for the dead he rested on a school opinion beyond the bulls that Cajetan rejected. The couplet "As soon as the gold in the casket rings..." survives in no text of Tetzel's: attributed, not attested. Doctrine: [`theology-of-debt`](../theology-of-debt/syllabus.md) 3.5–3.6.

*Introduced:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-the-late-medieval-church-and-luthers-protest.md)

### Ninety-five Theses

Luther's Latin disputation on indulgences, 31 October 1517.

*Disputation on the Power and Efficacy of Indulgences* (tr. *Works of Martin Luther*, Philadelphia, 1915). Luther sent them to Albrecht with a covering letter dated 31 October (it survives); Albrecht forwarded them to Rome; by the turn of the year in print at Leipzig, Nuremberg and Basel. Thesis 27: "They preach man [*hominem praedicant*, a human invention] who say that so soon as the penny jingles into the money-box, the soul flies out"; thesis 50: the pope, *if he knew* the exactions, would rather St Peter's went to ashes. In 1517 Luther wrote as a critic of the preachers, appealing to the pope. Spring 1518: the German *Sermon on Indulgence and Grace*, reprinted about fourteen times in 1518 (Pettegree, *Brand Luther*, 2015). (Sorting the theses by target is Boss 6; outline only.)

*Introduced:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-the-late-medieval-church-and-luthers-protest.md)

### Exsurge Domine

Leo X's bull of 15 June 1520 condemning Luther.

Condemned 41 propositions from Luther's writings under a string of censures (heretical, scandalous, false, offensive to pious ears, misleading to the simple) without saying which censure fit which proposition; gave him sixty days. Luther burned it at Wittenberg on 10 December 1520. *Decet Romanum Pontificem* excommunicated him on 3 January 1521. Preceded by Cajetan's examination at Augsburg (October 1518) and the Leipzig disputation (June–July 1519), where Johann Eck drew Luther into saying papal primacy was of human right and Constance erred in condemning Hus: the quarrel moved from preaching to authority.

*Introduced:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-the-late-medieval-church-and-luthers-protest.md)

### Diet of Worms

The imperial diet at which Luther refused to recant, April 1521.

Summoned under safe conduct, Luther appeared before Charles V on **17 April 1521**, asked for time, and on **18 April** refused to recant unless convinced by Scripture or plain reason. The questioner was Johann von der Ecken of the archbishop of Trier's household (not Johann Eck of Leipzig). The notes taken in the hall end with a plea for God's help and an Amen; "Here I stand, I cannot do otherwise" first appears in the earliest printed Wittenberg report (Bainton conceded it was "not recorded on the spot"). The Edict of Worms (May 1521) put him under the imperial ban; Frederick had him hidden in the Wartburg. Survival ran on politics: Hus had a safe conduct and burned; Luther had a prince and an emperor who could not alienate the princes.

*Introduced:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-the-late-medieval-church-and-luthers-protest.md)

### Peasants War and the radicals

The commons' and the Anabaptists' answers to "who decides reform", both crushed.

**Peasants' War (1524–25):** Swabia, Franconia, Thuringia; the Twelve Articles (Memmingen, March 1525) grounded demands on Scripture (an elected pastor, end of serfdom). Luther's *Admonition to Peace* (April) blamed both sides; *Against the Robbing and Murdering Hordes of Peasants* (May) urged the princes on. Frankenhausen, 15 May 1525; Thomas Muntzer executed later that month; around 100,000 dead (a commonly cited, rough figure). **Zurich:** Zwingli preaching at the Grossmunster from 1519; council-run disputations (1523); Mass abolished 1525; Marburg (1529): agreed fourteen of fifteen articles, split on the Eucharist; Zwingli killed at Kappel 1531. **Anabaptists:** first adult baptisms in Zurich, January 1525; Zurich drowned Felix Manz (January 1527); Schleitheim articles (February 1527, mainly Michael Sattler); Catholic Austrian authorities burned Sattler that May. **Munster** (1534–35): apocalyptic Anabaptists, polygamy and a "king"; besieged by the Catholic bishop with Lutheran help; fell June 1535. An exception, not "the Anabaptists".

*Introduced:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)

### Calvin's Geneva

The Reformed church ordered by consistory and synod, exported by trained pastors.

Calvin expelled 1538, returned September 1541; *Ecclesiastical Ordinances*: pastors, doctors, elders, deacons, and a consistory. Geneva burned Michael Servetus for denying the Trinity (1553) and opened its Academy (1559). Its pastors carried Reformed Christianity to France, the Netherlands, Scotland, Hungary and the Palatinate: an international church ordered by synods, not by the local prince.

*Introduced:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)

### Peace of Augsburg

The 1555 religious peace between Catholic and Lutheran estates of the Empire.

**25 September 1555**, signed by King Ferdinand on Charles V's authority (tr. Emil Reich, 1905), after the Augsburg Confession (1530), Muhlberg (1547) and the princes' revolt and Treaty of Passau (1552). **Art. 15:** protects the *estates* of the Augsburg Confession. **Art. 17:** all not of the two religions excluded (Anabaptists, and, as Catholics and Lutherans argued for ninety years, Calvinists). **Art. 18:** the [ecclesiastical reservation](#ecclesiastical-reservation). **Art. 24:** subjects of either religion may sell up and emigrate without loss of honour: freedom of exit, not of conscience. Already-mixed imperial cities kept both confessions. A truce between two recognized churches enforced by princes, not toleration. See [Cuius regio eius religio](#cuius-regio-eius-religio).

*Introduced:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)

### Ecclesiastical reservation

The clause of the Peace of Augsburg that kept a converting bishop's lands Catholic.

Article 18: a Catholic bishop who turned Protestant had to give up his see, so church lands would not follow him. He could change his own religion but not take the territory with him.

*Introduced:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)

### Edict of Nantes

Henry IV's settlement of the French wars of religion, April 1598.

Eight wars followed the massacre at Vassy (1562). **St Bartholomew's Day** (from 24 August 1572): Parisian crowds and royal troops killed perhaps 2,000–3,000 Protestants in Paris and thousands more in the provinces (totals disputed); it began as a royal strike at Huguenot leaders and spread into popular killing the court did not control; Gregory XIII, told a Huguenot plot had been foiled, had a *Te Deum* sung and a medal struck. Henry of Navarre became Catholic in 1593 ("Paris is well worth a Mass" is attributed with no contemporary source). **Nantes:** Catholic worship everywhere, Reformed worship in named places, civil rights, fortified towns for the Huguenots. Revoked by Louis XIV in 1685. John Paul II, at World Youth Day in Paris (1997), said Christians had done things the Gospel condemns in the massacre.

*Introduced:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)

### Peace of Westphalia

The 1648 settlement of the Thirty Years' War, which admitted the Reformed and fixed a normative year.

Thirty Years' War (1618–48) from the Bohemian revolt; Ferdinand II's **Edict of Restitution** (6 March 1629) tried to reclaim church lands held by Catholics at Passau (1552) and confined the peace to the 1530 Augsburg Confession, shutting out Calvinists; Catholic France fought the Habsburgs alongside Protestant Sweden; the Empire lost roughly a fifth of its population, mostly to plague and hunger (Peter Wilson; others higher). **Westphalia (24 October 1648):** the Reformed admitted; church property and public worship fixed as of 1 January 1624, curbing a ruler's power to convert his subjects. Where [`international-relations`](../international-relations/syllabus.md) starts the state system (its 1.2 asks how much "Westphalian sovereignty" the treaties created).

*Introduced:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)

### Zelo domus Dei

Innocent X's protest against the Peace of Westphalia.

A **brief**, not a bull, dated 26 November 1648 and published about two years later, declaring the clauses that harmed the Church null. A juridical protest over rights and properties; it defined no doctrine. The treaty had already declared such protests void, and it changed nothing on the ground.

*Introduced:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)

### Henry VIII's break with Rome

The "great matter" and the Henrician Church, neither Roman nor Protestant.

Henry sought annulment of his marriage to Catherine of Aragon from Leviticus; Clement VII, pressed by her nephew Charles V after the sack of Rome (1527), would not grant it. Act in Restraint of Appeals (1533); Cranmer pronounced the marriage void (May 1533); Clement ruled it valid (March 1534); Act of Supremacy (November 1534; see [Royal supremacy](#royal-supremacy)). **Fisher** beheaded 22 June 1535, **More** 6 July 1535 (both canonized 1935; during More's chancellorship six people were burned for heresy with his approval). Six Articles (1539) kept Catholic doctrine. **30 July 1540:** three Protestants burned at Smithfield as heretics and three priests hanged as traitors on the same day: what Henry imposed was royal control of the Church, not Protestantism.

*Introduced:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)

### Dissolution of the monasteries

The closure of England's religious houses, 1536–1540.

Cromwell's visitors collected reports of monastic vice in 1535–36, knowing such findings were wanted (exaggeration and selection, not wholesale invention). The act of 1536 dissolved houses with incomes under 200 pounds a year (an income test, not a moral one); larger houses followed by "surrender" and a second act (1539). The abbots of Glastonbury, Reading and Colchester were executed in 1539; Waltham, the last abbey, fell 23 March 1540; over 800 houses in all. **Pilgrimage of Grace** (October 1536): Lincolnshire, then Yorkshire under Robert Aske, perhaps 30,000 men, demanding the abbeys be saved and restoring monks to some; Henry promised pardon, then used a fresh rising in 1537 to execute about 216. It followed from the supremacy and the Crown's need for money, not a Protestant programme.

*Introduced:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)

### Edward and Mary

England made Protestant, then reconciled to Rome, 1547–1558.

**Edward VI (1547–53):** chantries dissolved, images removed, Prayer Books of 1549 and 1552; the Prayer Book Rebellion in Devon and Cornwall (1549) crushed at the cost of thousands of lives. **Mary I (1553–58):** Cardinal Pole absolved the realm and reconciled it to Rome (November 1554); from February 1555 to November 1558 about 280–290 Protestants burned for heresy (usual counts 283–287), Cranmer among them (March 1556). John Foxe's *Acts and Monuments* (1563) made them the founding martyrs of Protestant England. Pole's London synod of 1556 ordered a *seminarium* at each cathedral.

*Introduced:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)

### Elizabethan settlement

The Protestant settlement of 1559 that lasted.

New Act of Supremacy ("supreme governor") and Act of Uniformity restoring a Protestant prayer book (1559). Of Mary's bishops only Kitchin of Llandaff took the oath. The Northern Rising (November 1569) failed; the queen demanded at least 700 executions and several hundred were carried out. On the usual count about 189 Catholics were executed under Elizabeth, some 126 priests and 63 lay people, most after 1581 (Cuthbert Mayne 1577, Edmund Campion 1581); most died under treason statutes that made the priesthood itself treasonable. The Forty Martyrs of England and Wales canonized 1970.

*Introduced:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)

### Regnans in Excelsis

Pius V's bull of 25 February 1570 excommunicating and deposing Elizabeth I.

Declared her a heretic (spiritual sentence) and "deprived of her pretended right" to the kingdom, her subjects absolved of allegiance and forbidden to obey her on pain of anathema (political sentence; lesson's translation from the 1570 Latin). Issued **after** the Northern Rising had failed; it did not start it, but made the loyalty question far worse. John Felton posted it in London (May 1570), executed that August. A 1580 explanation attributed to Gregory XIII let Catholics obey the queen as things stood, without removing the deposing claim. The deposing power was a claim of the medieval canonists, never defined by a council; its theological standing belongs to [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md). Locke later excluded churches whose members serve "a foreign prince" ([`history-of-political-thought` 4.2](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/04-02-locke-limited-government-and-toleration.md)).

*Introduced:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)

### Catholic reform before Trent

The program of residence, preaching and clerical training that existed before 1517 and lacked enforcement.

Francisco Jimenez de Cisneros (archbishop of Toledo from 1495): residence and preaching, reformed religious orders, founded Alcala, paid for the Complutensian Polyglot. The Oratory of Divine Love (Genoa 1497; Rome about 1514–1517), whose members included Gaetano da Thiene and Gian Pietro Carafa, founders of the Theatines (1524). The Fifth Lateran Council (1512–17), reform decrees barely enforced, closed March 1517; Giles of Viterbo's opening sermon (1512): "men must be changed by holy things, not holy things by men" (lesson's translation). Paul III's reform commission, *Consilium de emendanda ecclesia* (1537); Jesuits approved 1540 (for missions, preaching, sacraments and charity; not founded to fight Luther); Roman Inquisition 1542. Renaissance popes: Alexander VI (1492–1503, acknowledged children), Julius II (1503–1513, led armies), Leo X (1513–1521, cardinal at thirteen).

*Introduced:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md); [6.1](lessons/06-01-the-late-medieval-church-and-luthers-protest.md)

### Council of Trent

The council of 1545–1563, in three periods, that wrote the laws of Catholic reform.

Late because the popes feared a council above them (Constance, Basel), Charles V wanted one in Germany and France none; Mantua (1537) and Vicenza (1538) failed. **First period (1545–47, Paul III):** opened 13 December 1545 with about thirty prelates; emperor wanted reform first, pope doctrine first, done side by side; Session IV (April 1546) Scripture and tradition ([`fundamental-theology` 3.3](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-03-the-canon-as-defined.md)); Session VI (13 January 1547) justification ([`creation-grace-last-things`](../creation-grace-last-things/syllabus.md)); moved to Bologna March 1547; suspended 1549. **Second (1551–52, Julius III):** Session XIII (October 1551) Eucharist ([`sacraments-and-liturgy`](../sacraments-and-liturgy/syllabus.md)); Protestant envoys came, no theological meeting; suspended April 1552. **Gap (1555–59):** Paul IV reformed from Rome. **Third (1562–63, Pius IV):** the residence crisis; Session XXIII (15 July 1563) residence and seminaries; Session XXIV (11 November 1563) marriage (*Tametsi*); closed 4 December 1563; more than two hundred signed; missal, breviary, catechism and Index handed to the pope; confirmed January 1564. **Implementation:** Tridentine Index 1564, Roman Catechism 1566, Breviary 1568, Missal of Pius V (*Quo primum*, 14 July 1570, largely standardizing the existing Roman use); Spain received with royal reservations; the French crown never promulgated, the French clergy accepted on their own in 1615. **Borromeo** (Milan): vicar's synod and seminary 1564; entered September 1565, first resident archbishop in about eighty years; eleven diocesan synods; stayed through the plague of 1576; died 1584; canonized 1610. **France:** Ordinance of Blois (1579) made marriage law on royal authority; about sixteen seminaries 1580–1620 for more than a hundred dioceses; the wave after the 1640s (Vincent de Paul, Olier). Trent did not write the Missal, Catechism or Index, and "Tridentine" often names a century of bishops, kings and orders. (Whether Trent was a reaction to Protestantism is Boss 6; outline only.)

*Introduced:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)

### Seminary decree of Trent

Session XXIII, reform decree, chapter 18 (15 July 1563): every cathedral to maintain a college training boys for the priesthood.

Tr. Waterworth (1848): cathedral, metropolitan and greater churches, each according to its means, to maintain and train "a certain number of youths", at least twelve, legitimate, literate, "the children of the poor ... principally", "a perpetual seminary of ministers of God". A chapter of a reform decree, not a canon ("canon 18" is a slip). The duty falls on the cathedral (bishop and chapter pay); the rest of the chapter taxes benefices, monasteries and confraternities, hence resistance; no deadline. The word was not new: Pole's London synod (1556) ordered a *seminarium* at each cathedral, and its decrees circulated at Trent in 1562.

*Introduced:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)

### Tametsi

Trent's decree of 11 November 1563 making clandestine marriages invalid.

Session XXIV, reform of marriage, ch. 1 (Waterworth): a marriage is valid only if contracted before the parish priest (or a priest with his or the Ordinary's permission) and two or three witnesses; otherwise null. The problem: men who married secretly then publicly married another, which the Church, which "judges not of what is hidden", could not correct. The parish priest keeps a register (names, witnesses, day, place). Bound only where published (so not straightforwardly in France). Discipline, not dogma: clandestine marriages had been valid "so long as the Church has not rendered them invalid"; replaced by *Ne temere* (1907, in force 1908). Trent did not invent the parish register (Italian baptismal registers from 1379; Cromwell's order of 1538; the Roman Ritual of 1614 prescribed the five books).

*Introduced:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)

### Index of Prohibited Books

The papal list of forbidden books.

Paul IV's Index (1559), the first papal Index, so severe it was softened within months; the Tridentine Index (1564); a Congregation of the Index from 1571; enforced by the Roman Inquisition. Copernican works were suspended in 1616 and dropped in stages between 1758 and 1835 ([7.3](lessons/07-03-the-galileo-affair.md)).

*Introduced:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)

### Spanish Inquisition

A crown tribunal with papal sanction, 1478–1834.

Sixtus IV's bull of **1 November 1478** let Ferdinand and Isabella appoint the inquisitors; first named 1480; the Suprema created 1483. Jurisdiction only over the **baptized**. **Founding terror (1480–1530):** first auto de fe at Seville, 6 February 1481 (six burned); Torquemada inquisitor general 1483–1498; Hernando del Pulgar put the burned by about 1490 at 2,000; a 1524 inscription at the Seville tribunal claimed more than 20,000 abjurations and nearly 1,000 burnings. Property confiscated at arrest; torture lawful when guilt was half-proven, with a physician present and ratification required afterwards; prison, *sanbenito*, loss of office for descendants. **Expulsions (royal):** unbaptized Jews by decree of 31 March 1492 (numbers disputed; Torquemada reportedly urged it); Moriscos 1609–1614, roughly 300,000 on older estimates. **Later:** 1559–62 autos destroyed Protestant circles; then mostly Moriscos, blasphemy, bigamy, propositions, clerical misconduct; death sentences rare. **Witches:** at Logrono (November 1610) six burned in person and five in effigy; Alonso de Salazar Frias investigated in 1611 and found no proof any witchcraft had occurred; the Suprema's instructions of 1614 adopted his rules and executions of witches effectively stopped. **Portuguese** Inquisition 1536, Goa tribunal 1560–1812. **End:** suppressed by Joseph Bonaparte (1808) and the Cortes of Cadiz (1813), restored 1814, suspended 1820; last execution for heresy, Cayetano Ripoll, 26 July 1826 (by a diocesan "junta of faith"); abolished 15 July 1834.

*Introduced:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-spanish-and-roman-inquisitions.md)

### Roman Inquisition

A papal congregation of cardinals supervising tribunals across Italy, from 1542.

Paul III's *Licet ab initio*, **21 July 1542**. Broke the Italian evangelical circles of the 1540s–60s; enforced the Index; sent **Giordano Bruno** to the stake in Rome, 17 February 1600; tried Galileo ([7.3](lessons/07-03-the-galileo-affair.md)). Andrea Del Col: roughly 1,250 death sentences in some 51,000–75,000 cases in Italy after 1542. Survived as the Holy Office (renamed 1908), today's Dicastery for the Doctrine of the Faith. A claim about Rome is not evidence about Spain.

*Introduced:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-spanish-and-roman-inquisitions.md)

### Inter caetera

Alexander VI's bull of 1493 granting Castile the lands beyond a line in the Atlantic, with the duty to convert their peoples.

Dated 4 May 1493: lands beyond a line 100 leagues west of the Azores and Cape Verde islands; moved by treaty to 370 leagues at Tordesillas (7 June 1494). Castile's title rested on a papal grant tied to evangelization, which is why evangelization became the critics' ground: a friar who showed that conquest and forced labour blocked conversion attacked the crown's title. In 2023 a joint note of two Vatican dicasteries repudiated the "doctrine of discovery", saying *Inter caetera* and the earlier Portuguese bulls were never part of the Church's teaching and had been used by colonial powers to justify immoral acts.

*Introduced:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)

### Montesinos and Las Casas

The Dominican denunciation of the conquest, and the colonist who became its chief critic.

**Antonio Montesinos**, Dominican, preached in Santo Domingo on the fourth Sunday of Advent, 21 December 1511: "Are these not men? Do they not have rational souls?" No text of his survives; the words are Las Casas's reconstruction decades later (*Historia de las Indias* 3.4). The argument's form: a question of **right**, not only cruelty (unjust wars, unjust servitude, fellow humans owed love). Followed by the Laws of Burgos (1512). **Bartolome de las Casas** (1484–1566): priest and *encomendero* in Cuba; in 1514, preparing a sermon on Ecclesiasticus 34, gave up his Indians; Dominican 1522–23; bishop of Chiapas (consecrated 1544). In memorials of 1516–18 he proposed African slaves be imported to release the Indians (a licence for 4,000 was granted and sold on to Genoese merchants); later (*Historia* 3.129) repented, judging "the captivity of the blacks was as unjust as that of the Indians". His *Brevisima relacion* (written 1542, printed Seville 1552) fed the Black Legend.

*Introduced:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)

### Sublimis Deus

Paul III's bull of 2 June 1537 declaring the Indians "truly men", not to be enslaved.

Rejects the claim that the Indians are "dumb brutes" incapable of the faith; they are "truly men", capable of the faith and desirous of it; they are not to be deprived of liberty or property, "even though they be outside the faith", nor enslaved; any enslavement "null and of no effect" (tr. MacNutt, 1909, who misdates *quarto nonas Junii* as 4 June; it is 2 June). It does not name the encomienda. A companion brief, *Pastorale officium*, threatened excommunication; after Charles V protested it infringed his patronage, Paul III annulled the brief in 1538; whether *Sublimis Deus* fell with it is disputed. "We define and declare" does not settle its weight ([`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md)).

*Introduced:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)

### Valladolid debate

The junta of 1550–51 before which Sepulveda and Las Casas argued whether war on the Indians was just.

Charles V ordered conquests suspended in 1550 while a junta of theologians and jurists heard Juan Gines de Sepulveda, who argued from Aristotle's natural slavery that war to subject the Indians was just ([`history-of-political-thought` 1.3](../history-of-political-thought/lessons/01-03-aristotle-the-city-exists-by-nature.md)), and Las Casas, who denied every premise. The junta issued **no formal verdict**.

*Introduced:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)

### Demographic collapse of the Americas

The fall of the native population after 1492, among the most disputed numbers in historical demography.

On most estimates something like nine-tenths within a century. Hemisphere in 1492: from about 8 million (Kroeber) to over 100 million (Dobyns); Denevan's synthesis (1992) roughly 43–65 million. Central Mexico (Cook and Borah): about 25 million in 1519 to near one million by 1605 (the high start contested, the scale of the fall not). Most deaths from diseases without immunity: smallpox on Hispaniola 1518–19, Mexico 1520. But Livi-Bacci ("Return to Hispaniola", 2003): the Taino were near extinction *before* smallpox, from forced labour in the mines, war, famine, broken households and falling births. "Mostly disease" is not "no one's fault". **Africa:** Church institutions took part in the Atlantic trade (Jesuit estates in coastal Peru on enslaved labour; Cushner, *Lords of the Land*, 1980); Pedro Claver (1580–1654) met the slave ships at Cartagena for nearly forty years, final vows 1622 as "slave of the Ethiopians forever", his interpreters enslaved Africans owned by the Jesuit college. **Acknowledgement:** Francis in Bolivia (2015) asked forgiveness for crimes against native peoples in the conquest.

*Introduced:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)

### Jesuit missions in Asia

Accommodation in Japan, China and India, 1542–1640s.

**Xavier:** under Portuguese patronage, Goa May 1542; Pearl Fishery Coast; Kagoshima August 1549 (letter of 5 November 1549, tr. Coleridge 1872: the Japanese best of the "barbarous nations", "desirous of honour and rank"); first rendered God *Dainichi* (Shingon), then *Deusu*; died on Shangchuan Island, December 1552. **Valignano** (Visitor from 1573; died 1606): language required, Japanese clergy trained, an etiquette code ranking Jesuits like Zen monks; sent Ruggieri and Ricci to Macau. **Ricci** (1583–1610): Zhaoqing 1583 as a Buddhist monk; Confucian literati robes from 1595; *True Meaning of the Lord of Heaven* (1603): the classics knew *Shangdi*; Beijing from 1601; treated ancestor and Confucius rites as civil; buried in Beijing. **Japan:** Nagasaki handed to the Jesuits 1580; perhaps 300,000 Christians by about 1600 (an estimate); Hideyoshi's expulsion order 1587; twenty-six crucified at Nagasaki on 5 February 1597 (six Franciscans, three Japanese Jesuits, seventeen Japanese laypeople); Tokugawa ban 1614; Shimabara rising 1637–38. **Goa:** the Portuguese Inquisition sat 1560–1812 (gap 1774–78): accommodation where the Church did not rule, otherwise where it did.

*Introduced:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-accommodation-in-asia.md)

### Kakure Kirishitan

The hidden Christians of Japan, who kept the faith underground after the Tokugawa ban.

After the ban of 1614 and the Shimabara rising (1637–38), Christianity survived only underground. They showed themselves to a French priest at Nagasaki in 1865.

*Introduced:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-accommodation-in-asia.md)

### Malabar rites

Roberto de Nobili's accommodations to high-caste custom in South India, and Rome's rulings on them.

De Nobili in Madurai from 1606 lived as a Brahmin *sannyasi*; let high-caste converts keep the sacred thread, tuft of hair and sandalwood paste; held separate churches by caste. Gregory XV allowed thread and tuft with conditions in *Romanae Sedis Antistes* (1623). Benedict XIV's *Omnium sollicitudinum* (1744) settled them, confirming most of Rome's restrictions.

*Introduced:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-accommodation-in-asia.md)

### Chinese rites controversy

A century-long dispute over whether Chinese ancestor and Confucius rites were worship or civil respect.

Both sides agreed no Christian may join idolatry; they disagreed on the **facts** (what an act meant; which informants to trust; origin or present use). Dominicans and Franciscans from the Philippines reported Jesuit practice to Rome. Rulings swung with the question put: against the rites 1645, for them 1656, both decrees stand "according to circumstances" 1669. Kangxi's toleration edict 1692; the Jesuits obtained his statement in 1700 that the rites were civil. Holy Office against the rites 1704 (*Cum Deus optimus*). After the legate Tournon's embassy, Kangxi required from 1706 a permit pledging Ricci's practice; Tournon published the ban 1707, was expelled to Macau and held by the Portuguese until his death in 1710. [Ex illa die](#ex-illa-die) (1715). The legate Mezzabarba presented it; Kangxi's written comment of 1721 said Westerners should no longer preach in China (a comment on a court translation, not a promulgated edict). Yongzheng proscribed Christianity in 1724 (after a governor's complaint; he distrusted the Jesuits' ties to a defeated rival). Benedict XIV's *Ex quo singulari* (11 July 1742) confirmed the ban and required every missionary's oath. Reversed by [Plane compertum est](#plane-compertum-est) (1939). About 200,000 Christians by 1700 on the missionaries' counts.

*Introduced:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-accommodation-in-asia.md)

### Ex illa die

Clement XI's bull of 19 March 1715 forbidding the Chinese rites.

Forbade *Tian* and *Shangdi* as names for God (kept *Tianzhu*), "Revere Heaven" plaques in churches, and any part in the sacrifices to Confucius and to ancestors, including officials' temple worship; a narrow allowance for passive presence where absence would provoke hostility; a tablet bearing only the name with a Christian inscription allowed, offerings before it not. It did not ban accommodation as such (dress and language untouched; Propaganda Fide's 1659 instructions had told missionaries not to import Europe). A disciplinary ruling ([`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)).

*Introduced:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-accommodation-in-asia.md)

### Suppression of the Jesuits

The dissolution of the Society of Jesus by Clement XIV in 1773, driven by the Catholic courts.

Pombal expelled the Jesuits from Portugal and its empire (1759), France followed (1764), Spain (1767); the Bourbon courts pressed Rome to finish the order (reasons: regalism, Jansenist hostility, money, colonial conflict in Paraguay). Clement XIV's brief *Dominus ac Redemptor* (21 July 1773) dissolved it for the Church's peace; it lists mission-rite quarrels among many others and does not condemn the Society's doctrine. Russia and Prussia refused to publish it. Pius VII restored the order on 7 August 1814. Not a verdict on the rites, which Rome had settled in 1715, 1742 and 1744.

*Introduced:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-accommodation-in-asia.md)

### Plane compertum est

Propaganda Fide's instruction of 8 December 1939 permitting the Chinese rites.

Approved by Pius XII: Catholics may attend Confucius ceremonies, bow before the dead, and keep a Confucius tablet in Catholic schools; the 1742 oath dropped. Stated ground: customs and ideas had changed over the centuries, so ceremonies once bound up with pagan rites now carried only civil meaning (piety to ancestors, love of country, courtesy). Built on instructions for Manchukuo (1935) and Japan (1936). It did not say 1715 was wrong, or that the Jesuits had been right. Rome changed its judgement of the facts, not the rule.

*Introduced:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-accommodation-in-asia.md)

### Bellarmine to Foscarini

Cardinal Robert Bellarmine's letter of 12 April 1615 to the Carmelite Foscarini on Copernicus and Scripture.

Occasioned by Foscarini's book reconciling Copernicus with Scripture, as Galileo's Letter to Castelli (21 December 1613: Scripture cannot err but its interpreters can; sent to the Inquisition by the Dominican Niccolo Lorini, February 1615) was expanded into the Letter to the Grand Duchess Christina (1615, printed 1636). Bellarmine's point 3 addresses what a "true demonstration" of the sun's centrality would require of Scripture's interpreters and distinguishes saving the appearances from showing how things are; point 2 holds that everything in Scripture is of faith on the part of the speaker. Galileo credited Baronius with the line that the Holy Spirit teaches "how one goes to heaven, and not how heaven goes". Lesson's own translation from the Italian (Favaro, vol. XII). The fight was now over who may interpret Scripture: Trent's Session IV (1546) forbade interpretations in faith and morals against the unanimous consent of the Fathers. (Reading the two letters against each other, and where they part, is Boss 7; outline only.)

*Introduced:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Index decree of 1616

The Congregation of the Index's decree of 5 March 1616 against the Copernican doctrine.

The telescope first: *Sidereus Nuncius* (March 1610; mountains on the Moon, moons of Jupiter; then Venus's phases), confirmed by the Roman College's Jesuits in 1611; it finished Ptolemy but did not prove the earth moves (Tycho fitted too). **24 February 1616:** eleven Holy Office consultors judged the sun's immobility absurd in philosophy and formally heretical, the earth's motion absurd and at least erroneous in faith (advice, not repeated in the decree). **26 February:** Galileo warned at Bellarmine's residence. **5 March:** the Index decree (approved by Paul V) called the doctrine false and wholly contrary to Scripture, suspended *De revolutionibus* until corrected (corrections 1620), prohibited Foscarini's book, banned all others teaching it. Galileo was not named, tried or made to abjure; Bellarmine's certificate of 26 May said he had been told of the declaration only. A Church body did rule a scientific proposition contrary to Scripture (no myth), without making geocentrism dogma. **Reversal:** the 1758 Index dropped the general ban; 1820 the Holy Office let Canon Giuseppe Settele teach the motion as fact; 1822, with Pius VII's approval, such books permitted generally; the 1835 Index omitted Copernicus and the *Dialogue*.

*Introduced:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Special injunction of 1616

The disputed minute recording a stricter order to Galileo in 1616, on which the 1633 prosecution rested.

Paul V's order of 25 February 1616 was **conditional**: only if Galileo refused Bellarmine's warning was the Commissary, before a notary and witnesses, to forbid him to teach, defend or discuss the doctrine. The file's minute says the Commissary, Michelangelo Seghizzi, gave the stricter order "immediately thereafter" ("nor henceforth to hold, teach, or defend it in any way whatsoever, verbally or in writing"; tr. Gebler 1879); it records no refusal and bears no signatures. Bellarmine's certificate of 26 May says only that the Index's declaration was intimated to him. Emil Wohlwill (1870) and Karl von Gebler thought the minute forged in 1632; Gebler examined it in 1877 and concluded it was written in 1616, though still a false record. Sergio Pagano (Prefect of the Vatican Archives since 2007) argues it authentic; Maurice Finocchiaro agrees, but insists authentic is not accurate or legitimate.

*Introduced:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Galileo's trial of 1633

The prosecution of Galileo for the *Dialogue*, ending in abjuration and confinement.

Maffeo Barberini, an admirer, became **Urban VIII** in 1623; from audiences in 1624 Galileo understood he might discuss the two systems as hypotheses, and Urban wanted his own argument included (God could produce the same effects by means beyond our knowing). The *Dialogue on the Two Chief World Systems* (Florence, 1632), licensed by censors ignorant of the injunction, was a brilliant case for Copernicus; its tidal proof was wrong; Urban's argument went to Simplicio. First examined **12 April 1633** on the minute; produced Bellarmine's certificate; after an arrangement with the Commissary Vincenzo Maculano conceded the book could read as a defence of Copernicus. 16 June: examination on intention under threat of torture ordered; the "rigorous examination" of 21 June went no further than the threat. **22 June 1633**, Santa Maria sopra Minerva: [vehemently suspected of heresy](#vehement-suspicion-of-heresy); the sentence noted the certificate lacked "to teach" and "in any way"; abjured on his knees; *Dialogue* banned; formal imprisonment, commuted within days to confinement, from December 1633 at Arcetri; seven of ten cardinal judges signed. *Two New Sciences* (1638); died 8 January 1642. "Eppur si muove" first appears in Baretti's *Italian Library* (London, 1757). Causes on offer: the Copernican question, Urban's sense of betrayal, Counter-Reformation politics (Cardinal Gaspar Borgia's attack in consistory, 8 March 1632); Redondi's 1983 atomism-and-Eucharist thesis mostly rejected. (Weighing the causes is Boss 7; outline only.) **1992:** John Paul II asked for re-examination in 1979; on 31 October 1992 told the Pontifical Academy of Sciences most theologians then made a question of fact one of faith, Galileo read Scripture more perceptively, and the sentence was not irreformable, the debate having closed in 1820 (§§5, 9, 12); "rehabilitation" does not appear. Direct evidence of the earth's motion: aberration of starlight (Bradley, 1729), stellar parallax (Bessel, 1838).

*Introduced:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-the-galileo-affair.md)

### Unigenitus

Clement XI's bull of 8 September 1713 condemning 101 propositions of the Jansenist Pasquier Quesnel, which became a political weapon.

In 1752 Archbishop Christophe de Beaumont of Paris had the sacraments refused to the dying who could not show they accepted it; the Parlement of Paris intervened for the dying; the crown exiled the Parlement (1753–54), imposed silence (September 1754), and exiled the archbishop too. **Dale Van Kley** (*The Religious Origins of the French Revolution*, 1996): these fights did much of the Revolution's ideological work in advance, teaching magistrates and public to resist crown and bishops in the name of the nation and the "true" Church, and producing the Jansenist-Gallican program the Civil Constitution wrote into law. Critics doubt it outweighs fiscal and social causes: a strong thesis, not a settled result.

*Introduced:* [8.1](lessons/08-01-enlightenment-revolution-and-napoleon.md)

### Civil Constitution of the Clergy

The French Assembly's reorganization of the Catholic Church, voted 12 July 1790.

After the tithe's abolition (August 1789), church property placed at the nation's disposal (2 November 1789) and solemn vows denied legal recognition (February 1790). One diocese per department (83 in place of about 130), elected clergy, state salaries. Title II (tr. Robinson, in Anderson 1908): election by the same lay electors as the departmental assembly, with no requirement they be Catholic (art. 3); the metropolitan gives canonical institution (art. 17); the new bishop writes to the pope "as the Visible Head of the Universal Church" but seeks no confirmation (art. 19). Thirty bishop-deputies' *Exposition des principes* (October 1790): the objection was not to reform but that the Assembly reorganized the Church with no Church authority taking part. **Rome:** Pius VI's *Quod aliquantum* (10 March 1791) and *Charitas* (13 April 1791): jurors suspended if they did not retract within forty days, the new bishops' elections void and consecrations illicit (Talleyrand, bishop of Autun, consecrated the first on 24 February 1791). France had two Catholic churches. Don't grade *Charitas*'s weight here.

*Introduced:* [8.1](lessons/08-01-enlightenment-revolution-and-napoleon.md)

### Dechristianization

The campaign of 1793–94 against Christian worship itself, beyond the refractory clergy.

Preceded by the **September massacres** (2–6 September 1792): crowds killed between about 1,100 and 1,600 Paris prisoners, over two hundred of them priests (191 beatified in 1926).
