Dechristianization closed churches, pressed priests of both kinds (jurors too) to renounce orders, brought in a republican calendar without Sundays, and staged the Festival of Reason in Notre-Dame (10 November 1793); Robespierre answered with the Festival of the Supreme Being (8 June 1794). **The Vendee:** a rising of March 1793 over conscription and religion crushed with mass killings, about 170,000 dead in the insurgent region on the standard estimate (others vary widely); most specialists reject the label genocide. **Pius VI:** French troops proclaimed a Roman Republic (February 1798) and carried him off; he died at Valence, 29 August 1799.

*Introduced:* [8.1](lessons/08-01-enlightenment-revolution-and-napoleon.md)

### Concordat of 1801

The treaty between Pius VII and Bonaparte that restored the Church in France on the state's terms.

Pius VII elected at Venice, 14 March 1800; signed **15 July 1801**. Catholicism recognized as the religion of most French citizens, not of the state; the First Consul nominates bishops, the pope institutes them "following the forms established ... before the change of government" (arts. 4–5, the Bologna 1516 pattern); the state pays the clergy; the Church never reclaims its sold lands (art. 13). The pope called on every sitting bishop to resign; several dozen old-regime bishops refused (counts from about 36 to 45); every constitutional bishop resigned. Pius VII attended Napoleon's coronation (2 December 1804), where Napoleon crowned himself. Papal States annexed May 1809; the pope excommunicated the invaders and was arrested on the night of 5–6 July 1809, held at Savona and Fontainebleau; re-entered Rome May 1814, restored the Jesuits (7 August 1814); Consalvi recovered most of the Papal States at Vienna (1815). Restoration or surrender depends on what is measured: the Church's legal position in France, or the pope's authority within the Church (the Catholic Encyclopedia, 1909: "the most dazzling manifestation of the pope's supreme power"). Ended by the [French Separation Law of 1905](#french-separation-law-of-1905).

*Introduced:* [8.1](lessons/08-01-enlightenment-revolution-and-napoleon.md)

### Organic Articles

Bonaparte's unilateral regulations published with the Concordat on 8 April 1802, which Rome never accepted.

Article 1: no bull, brief or other document from Rome may be received, published or put into effect without the government's authorization (the Gallican *placet*); seminary teachers to subscribe to the Four Articles of 1682. Added unilaterally to a bilateral treaty and published as if part of it.

*Introduced:* [8.1](lessons/08-01-enlightenment-revolution-and-napoleon.md)

### Pius IX and the loss of the Papal States

The liberal beginning, the exile, and the shrinking state, 1846–1870.

Elected 16 June 1846 (reigned 31 years, to 7 February 1878, the longest secure papal reign); amnesty 16 July 1846; constitution for the Papal States 14 March 1848; allocution of 29 April 1848 refused war on Catholic Austria for Italian unity. His minister Pellegrino Rossi stabbed 15 November 1848; Pius fled in disguise to Gaeta (24 November); Roman Republic proclaimed 9 February 1849, abolishing his temporal power; French troops took Rome that summer; Pius returned 12 April 1850, no longer a liberal; a French garrison guarded Rome most of the next twenty years. 1859–60: the Romagna, Marches and Umbria went to Piedmont after Castelfidardo (18 September 1860). *Ineffabilis Deus* (8 December 1854): the Immaculate Conception defined after consulting bishops by letter, a pope defining without a council (content: [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md); solemnly defined, rung 1 on [`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md)). **Porta Pia:** France declared war on Prussia 19 July 1870, recalled its garrison in early August; after Sedan (2 September) Italian troops entered Rome on **20 September 1870**; plebiscite 2 October. The theological censures of the era: Gregory XVI's *Mirari vos* (August 1832) against Lamennais's *L'Avenir* (unnamed); *Dum acerbissimas* (September 1835) against Georg Hermes; Louis Bautain made to sign propositions (8 September 1840); Anton Gunther on the Index (8 January 1857) ([`fundamental-theology` 2.3](../fundamental-theology/lessons/02-03-fideism-and-rationalism.md)).

*Introduced:* [8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)

### Mortara case

The seizure in 1858 of a six-year-old Jewish boy in Bologna, said to have been secretly baptized, whom Pius IX raised as a Catholic.

In June 1858 papal police in Bologna, acting for the local inquisitor Pier Gaetano Feletti, took Edgardo Mortara from his parents after a Catholic servant, Anna Morisi, said she had baptized him during an infant illness. Church law held a baptized child must be raised Catholic; papal law already forbade Jews to employ Christian servants. Pius refused every appeal (the family, Rome's Jews, Moses Montefiore in 1859, protest across Europe and America) and raised the boy under his own patronage. Bologna left papal rule in 1859; in April 1860 Feletti was tried and **acquitted** as having acted under the government of the day. Edgardo became a priest of the Canons Regular of the Lateran and died in Belgium in 1940. It hardened opinion, Napoleon III's included, against papal rule. **Sources:** David Kertzer, *The Kidnapping of Edgardo Mortara* (1997), from the Bologna inquisition, Vatican and family archives; Mortara's memoir published by Vittorio Messori (Italian 2005, English 2017), which Kertzer showed in 2018 departed from the Spanish manuscript (praise of Feletti added, hostile remarks about Jews dropped; publishers called the changes minor). An adult formed from six in papal custody is a real source for what he came to believe, a poor one for whether the removal was just. The Church has made no statement; at Pius IX's beatification (3 September 2000) the Mortara family and Jewish groups protested.

*Introduced:* [8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)

### Syllabus of Errors

A list of eighty propositions censured in earlier papal documents, issued with *Quanta cura* on 8 December 1864.

A list drawn from earlier statements, so each entry must be read against its source. Bishop Felix Dupanloup of Orleans published a moderating reading in 1865 (thesis and hypothesis); ultramontane readers took it at face value. Leo XIII never withdrew its principles; his ralliement can be read as Dupanloup's distinction put into practice. (The final proposition, on reconciling the Roman Pontiff with progress, liberalism and modern civilization, and the rival readings are Boss 8; outline only.)

*Introduced:* [8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)

### Vatican I votes of July 1870

The test vote of 13 July and the final vote of 18 July on *Pastor Aeternus*.

The council opened 8 December 1869; about 700 bishops and prelates at the first public session. *Dei Filius* passed 24 April 1870. Infallibility, not on the original agenda, was added on the majority's petition; Pius made no secret he wanted it; the majority closed the general debate in June with dozens still waiting to speak; a minority deputation's last appeal to Pius failed. **13 July:** 601 voting: 451 *placet* (75.0 percent), 88 *non placet*, 62 *placet iuxta modum*. **17 July:** some 55 to 60 of the minority signed a letter renewing their votes and staying away out of "filial piety and reverence", unwilling to say *non placet* "to our father's face", and went home (Latin in "Quirinus", *Letters from Rome on the Council*, 1870; lesson's translation). **18 July:** 535 present; *Pastor Aeternus* passed **533 to 2**, the vote of those present, not near-unanimity; the fall of 66 in attendance is net, not the number who left. Within a few years every minority bishop accepted the decrees. Suspended 20 October 1870, never reconvened or formally closed. The definition (primacy of jurisdiction; irreformability of *ex cathedra* definitions) is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md)'s; its conditions [`fundamental-theology` 4.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-02-infallibility-and-its-conditions.md)'s. See [Was Vatican I free](#was-vatican-i-free).

*Introduced:* [8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)

### Law of Guarantees

Italy's 1871 statute offering the pope honours and income, which Pius IX refused.

**13 May 1871:** sovereign honours, use of the Vatican palaces, an annual endowment of about 3.2 million lire. Pius rejected it in *Ubi nos* (15 May 1871): no guarantee granted by another sovereign by ordinary statute (amendable next year) could secure the pope's freedom. He never again left the Vatican ("prisoner in the Vatican"). The Roman Question stayed open until the [Lateran pacts](#lateran-pacts) of 1929.

*Introduced:* [8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)

### Old Catholics

The body formed by Catholics who refused Vatican I, with bishops from the Jansenist church of Utrecht.

Ignaz von Dollinger, Munich historian, refused the decrees and was excommunicated in April 1871; he inspired but **never joined** the Old Catholics. First congress Munich, September 1871; bishop Joseph Hubert Reinkens consecrated at Rotterdam by the Jansenist bishop of Deventer, 11 August 1873.

*Introduced:* [8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)

### Kulturkampf

Bismarck's campaign against the Catholic Church in the new German Empire, 1871–78, ended by negotiation with Leo XIII.

Jesuits expelled (1872); Prussia's May Laws (1873) put church appointments under state control; bishops imprisoned or exiled; eased after Pius IX's death (1878). Leo XIII negotiated its end: peace laws of 1886 and 1887 dismantled most of the anti-Catholic legislation; Bismarck asked Leo to mediate the Carolines dispute with Spain (1885). Bismarck's 1872 "we will not go to Canossa" belongs to it ([4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)).

*Introduced:* [8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)

### Leo XIII's programme

Engagement on the Church's terms: diplomacy, Thomism, social teaching, archives and biblical study, 1878–1903.

Elected at 67 on 20 February 1878. *Aeterni Patris* (4 August 1879): Aquinas the model ([`thomistic-synthesis`](../thomistic-synthesis/syllabus.md)). Vatican archives opened to researchers 1881. *Rerum Novarum* (15 May 1891) on industrial workers ([`catholic-social-teaching`](../catholic-social-teaching/syllabus.md)). *Providentissimus Deus* (18 November 1893): encouraged Catholic biblical study but taught inspiration of the whole of Scripture excluding error ([`fundamental-theology` 3.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-02-inerrancy.md)); John Paul II quoted its "truth cannot contradict truth" in 1992. Pontifical Biblical Commission (30 October 1902). Not a liberal reversed by Pius X: he condemned Americanism, taught strict inerrancy, kept the Syllabus's principles; the difference was posture and priority.

*Introduced:* [8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)

### Ralliement

Leo XIII's call for French Catholics to accept the Republic while fighting its anticlerical laws.

After Cardinal Lavigerie's toast at Algiers (12 November 1890), *Au milieu des sollicitudes* (16 February 1892): accept the Republic as the constituted power, oppose its legislation from inside. Accepted a regime, not a theory of the state.

*Introduced:* [8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)

### Americanism

The cluster of views Leo XIII rejected in *Testem benevolentiae* (1899), which American bishops said no one held.

Occasioned by a French translation of a life of Isaac Hecker (Paulist founder) with Abbe Felix Klein's preface presenting him as a model of adapting the Church to the age. *Testem benevolentiae* to Cardinal Gibbons (22 January 1899) rejected softening doctrine to win converts, dispensing with external spiritual direction, preferring active to passive virtues; it named no American and explicitly did not condemn American political institutions. The bishops' reply that no one held the views was a claim about its object, not defiance. Klein later called it *Une heresie fantome* (1949). A Roman document may define a position so as to condemn it; whether anyone held it is a separate historical question.

*Introduced:* [8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)

### French Separation Law of 1905

The law of 9 December 1905 ending the Concordat of 1801 in France.

No state salaries for clergy; church buildings owned by the state and communes, to be run by lay "associations for worship". Pius X condemned it in *Vehementer nos* (11 February 1906) and forbade the associations (August 1906). The French Church lost its endowments and gained freedom from state appointment of bishops.

*Introduced:* [8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)

### Pascendi Dominici Gregis

Pius X's encyclical of 8 September 1907 condemning modernism as a system.

Preceded by the Holy Office decree *Lamentabili sane exitu* (3 July 1907), condemning 65 propositions, many from Loisy. **Loisy:** *The Gospel and the Church* (1902; English 1903), a Catholic reply to Harnack's *What Is Christianity?*: "Jesus foretold the kingdom, and it was the Church that came", a defence of growth against Harnack's original-form principle, at the price of denying that Jesus planned the Church's constitution; five of his books on the Index December 1903; excommunicated *vitandus* 7 March 1908; his *Simples reflexions* (1908) called the system "a compilation of bits and pieces". **Tyrrell:** dismissed from the Jesuits 1906, cut off from the sacraments 1907, refused Catholic burial 1909. *Pascendi* §3 judges tenets, speech and conduct, leaving interior disposition to God, and turns the modernists' learning and good repute into part of the danger; §39 the "synthesis of all heresies". Remedies: scholastic philosophy in seminaries (and, repeating Leo, natural sciences), diocesan censors, secret Councils of Vigilance every two months, a sworn report from each bishop every three years. English version ed. T. E. Judge (1908). Its weight: [`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md); this course assigns no level.

*Introduced:* [8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)

### Oath Against Modernism

The oath imposed on clergy by Pius X's motu proprio *Sacrorum antistitum*, 1 September 1910.

Required of clergy, confessors, preachers, religious superiors and seminary professors; required until 1967. It affirmed specific propositions (among them that Christ personally instituted the Church, and that God can be known by reason). A formula imposed by papal act, not a doctrinal definition ([`fundamental-theology` 1.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/01-02-natural-knowledge-of-god.md)). Not of 1907, and not "against the modern world".

*Introduced:* [8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)

### Sodalitium Pianum

Mgr Umberto Benigni's partly secret network of integralist informers, 1909–1921.

Known in France as *La Sapiniere*; never more than about fifty members; reported suspected modernists to Roman offices. Pius X blessed its aims in handwritten letters of 1911, 1912 and 1914 (and it received an annual subsidy), but it never had canonical approval. Papers seized by German troops in occupied Ghent reached Rome in 1921 and helped end it; suppressed November 1921. Distinct from *Pascendi*'s official Councils of Vigilance (1907): don't date the informers to 1907. Lagrange's Ecole biblique (Jerusalem, founded 1890) was under suspicion; Lagrange recalled to France in 1912, allowed back a year later. How far Pius X knew its methods is disputed (the *New Catholic Encyclopedia*: blessed its aims, did not know the full extent; the other reading: its methods extended his own policy). Relief: Benedict XV's *Ad beatissimi* (1914); *Divino Afflante Spiritu* (1943) opened biblical study.

*Introduced:* [8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)

### Benedict XV's peace note

The pope's 1917 proposal for ending the First World War, which no government accepted.

"To the heads of the belligerent peoples", 1 August 1917: "absolute impartiality"; the moral force of law for arms; simultaneous reduction of armaments; arbitration with sanctions; mutual renunciation of war damages; evacuation of Belgium and occupied France; return of Germany's colonies; the war a "useless massacre" (*inutile strage*). Each side heard it as a plea for the other. Article 15 of the secret Treaty of London (1915) had the Allies back Italy in excluding the Holy See from peace talks; Wilson's reply (27 August 1917) refused to negotiate with the German regime; the Holy See was absent from Versailles.

*Introduced:* [9.1](lessons/09-01-the-church-and-the-totalitarian-states.md)

### Lateran pacts

The three instruments of 11 February 1929 that ended the Roman Question.

Signed by Cardinal Gasparri and Mussolini: a **Treaty** creating the sovereign State of Vatican City (about 44 hectares; its Article 1 reaffirmed Catholicism as the sole religion of the state); a **Concordat** regulating the Church in Italy (Article 43 on Catholic Action; civil effect for church marriages); a **Financial Convention**: 750 million lire cash and 1 billion lire in 5 percent state bonds as compensation for 1870 (compare the 3,225,000 lire a year Pius IX refused in 1871). **First clash:** the regime dissolved Catholic Action's youth groups; Pius XI's *Non abbiamo bisogno* (29 June 1931), written in Italian and carried to Paris by Mgr Francis Spellman, condemned the regime's worship of the state without condemning the party; settlement 2 September 1931 restored Catholic Action in reduced, diocesan form. *Quadragesimo Anno* (1931) was read against interwar corporatism ([`catholic-social-teaching`](../catholic-social-teaching/syllabus.md) 2.2).

*Introduced:* [9.1](lessons/09-01-the-church-and-the-totalitarian-states.md)

### Reichskonkordat

The Holy See's 1933 treaty with Nazi Germany: institutional guarantees bought with political withdrawal.

**23 March 1933:** the Enabling Act passed with the Catholic Centre Party's votes; **28 March:** the bishops withdrew their warnings against National Socialism; **5 July:** the Centre dissolved itself; **20 July:** Cardinal Pacelli and Vice-Chancellor von Papen signed; ratified **10 September**. Article 31 protected Catholic organizations with religious, cultural and charitable aims; Article 32 barred clergy and religious from membership of and work for political parties; Article 4 promised pastoral letters could be published without hindrance. The regime broke it from the start (youth groups, schools, press). Signed after the Enabling Act and the Centre's end, so it caused neither; whether the negotiations motivated the Centre's vote is the Scholder–Repgen question: Klaus Scholder argued a link; Konrad Repgen that the Centre voted on its own reckoning; later work in the Vatican files (Brechenmacher) has not proved the link. Still open.

*Introduced:* [9.1](lessons/09-01-the-church-and-the-totalitarian-states.md)

### Mit brennender Sorge

Pius XI's 1937 encyclical against the Nazi regime's breaches of the concordat, smuggled in and read from German pulpits.

Drafted from Cardinal Faulhaber's text, revised by Pacelli; dated 14 March 1937; **in German**. Smuggled in by courier, printed secretly in some 300,000 copies, read from the pulpits on Palm Sunday, **21 March 1937**. Charged the government with breaking the treaty; condemned anyone who raised race, people or state to an idol; named neither Hitler nor the party; did not renounce the concordat. The Gestapo seized the twelve presses and arrested people involved. Five days later *Divini Redemptoris* (19 March) condemned atheistic communism. An encyclical is not a definition: most encyclical teaching sits at the authentic, non-definitive rung ([`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md)).

*Introduced:* [9.1](lessons/09-01-the-church-and-the-totalitarian-states.md)

### Spanish Civil War and the Church

A Church both victim of one side and public ally of the other, 1936–1939.

In the Republican zone, above all in the war's first months, **6,832** clergy and religious were killed (Antonio Montero Moreno, 1961): twelve bishops and an apostolic administrator, 4,184 diocesan clergy, 2,365 male religious, 283 women religious. The bishops' collective letter dated 1 July 1937 backed the Nationalists; Cardinal Vidal i Barraquer refused to sign; Bishop Mugica of Vitoria objected that it passed over the Basque priests shot by Franco's forces (about fourteen to sixteen, depending on the count). One fact does not cancel the other.

*Introduced:* [9.1](lessons/09-01-the-church-and-the-totalitarian-states.md)

### Dutch bishops protest

The Dutch Catholic bishops' 1942 protest against the deportation of Jews, and the reprisal that followed.

In July 1942 the Dutch churches protested the deportations; the occupiers offered to exempt baptized Jews; the Reformed leadership largely held back, but the Catholic bishops had their letter read on **26 July 1942**. On **2 August** Catholics of Jewish descent were arrested in reprisal (about 200–250 on the usual counts), among them the Carmelite **Edith Stein**, murdered at Auschwitz on 9 August. (What the Dutch case can and cannot show about a public papal protest is part of Boss 9; outline only.)

*Introduced:* [9.1](lessons/09-01-the-church-and-the-totalitarian-states.md)

### Pius XII and the war

The record of Pius XII's public words and private action, 1939–1945, as the lesson sets it out.

Pacelli elected **2 March 1939**. **14 December 1942:** the Jesuit Lothar Konig wrote to the pope's secretary that thousands were being killed daily at Belzec, asking that the source be protected (letter found in 2023). **24 December 1942**, Christmas broadcast: hundreds of thousands condemned to death or slow decline, without fault, sometimes only because of nationality or race; named neither Jews nor killers. **Rome, 16–18 October 1943:** the SS arrested 1,259; after releases (non-Jews, baptized Jews, Jews in mixed marriages) 1,023 deported to Auschwitz; sixteen came back. Cardinal Maglione spoke privately to the German ambassador von Weizsacker on the day; no public papal protest followed. In the following months some 155 religious houses sheltered about 4,300 people, more than 3,200 of them Jews (a Jesuit's list of 1944–45, found in 2023, the source of Renzo De Felice's 1961 figures). The Pius XII archives opened on **2 March 2020**; they added documents on both sides (the Konig letter and the private channel to Hitler in 1939; the requests-for-help files and the convent lists). See [The silence debate](#the-silence-debate). Later Church texts: *Nostra Aetate* (1965), *We Remember* (1998; weighed in [`apologetics-foundations`](../apologetics-foundations/syllabus.md) 6.4).

*Introduced:* [9.1](lessons/09-01-the-church-and-the-totalitarian-states.md)

### Decree against communism

The Holy Office's decree published 1 July 1949 forbidding Catholics to join or support communist parties.

Those who professed materialist communism incurred excommunication as apostates. A Holy Office response imposing a penalty, not a dogmatic condemnation. Context: communist governments tried Archbishop Stepinac (sentenced October 1946 to sixteen years; his Ustasa-era record contested: forced conversions of Orthodox Serbs and closeness to the regime against sermons on racial persecution and interventions for victims; beatified 1998; a joint Catholic–Serbian Orthodox commission of 2016–17 could not agree) and Cardinal Mindszenty (arrested 26 December 1948, life sentence 8 February 1949).

*Introduced:* [9.1](lessons/09-01-the-church-and-the-totalitarian-states.md)

### Second Vatican Council

The council of 1962–1965, in four sessions, that produced sixteen documents and defined no new dogma.

**Announced** by John XXIII (elected October 1958) on 25 January 1959 at St Paul Outside the Walls. Antepreparatory commission (May 1959); ten preparatory commissions, mostly under curial cardinals; the Theological Commission under Cardinal Alfredo Ottaviani (Holy Office) wrote the doctrinal schemas; convoked by *Humanae Salutis* (25 December 1961). **First session** (11 October–8 December 1962): about 2,400 bishops; opening address dismissed the "prophets of doom", distinguished the substance of the faith from its expression, asked for the medicine of mercy. **13 October:** Cardinal Lienart moved, Frings seconded, to postpone commission elections; held 16 October from conference lists (see [European alliance](#european-alliance)). Liturgy schema taken as basis (only 46 against). **Revelation, 20–21 November 1962:** Ottaviani's *De fontibus revelationis* (two sources); the motion to interrupt got 1,368 yes, 822 no, 19 invalid of 2,209 (Wiltgen), short of two-thirds; next day John XXIII withdrew the schema to a mixed commission under Ottaviani and Bea; it became *Dei Verbum* ([`fundamental-theology` 3.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-04-tradition.md)). **Paul VI** (Montini, elected 21 June 1963, after John XXIII's death 3 June). Second session (29 September–4 December 1963): *Sacrosanctum Concilium* (2,147 to 4) and *Inter Mirifica*. Third (14 September–21 November 1964): see [Black Week](#black-week); *Lumen Gentium* 2,151 to 5. Fourth (1965): *Nostra Aetate* (28 October, 2,221 to 88): from John XXIII's meeting with Jules Isaac (13 June 1960), dropped from the 1962 agenda after the "Wardi affair", protested by Arab governments, folded into a declaration on non-Christian religions, "deicide" removed while still denying the Passion could be charged against all Jews. *Dignitatis Humanae* (7 December, 2,308 to 70), largely shaped by John Courtney Murray, six drafts; the minority cited Syllabus 77–79 against it (whether it developed or reversed that teaching is [`catholic-social-teaching`](../catholic-social-teaching/syllabus.md) 5.2 and `fundamental-theology`'s Boss 5). 7 December: joint declaration with Athenagoras on 1054 read at the council and in Istanbul (a joint act, not a conciliar decree). Closed **8 December 1965**.

| Genre | Documents (promulgated) |
|---|---|
| Constitutions (4) | *Sacrosanctum Concilium* (4 Dec 1963); *Lumen Gentium* (21 Nov 1964); *Dei Verbum* (18 Nov 1965); *Gaudium et Spes* (7 Dec 1965) |
| Decrees (9) | *Inter Mirifica* (1963); *Orientalium Ecclesiarum*, *Unitatis Redintegratio* (1964); *Christus Dominus*, *Perfectae Caritatis*, *Optatam Totius* (28 Oct 1965); *Apostolicam Actuositatem* (18 Nov 1965); *Ad Gentes*, *Presbyterorum Ordinis* (7 Dec 1965) |
| Declarations (3) | *Gravissimum Educationis*, *Nostra Aetate* (28 Oct 1965); *Dignitatis Humanae* (7 Dec 1965) |

Rhythm: none after the first session, two after the second, three after the third, eleven in the fourth session's last six weeks. Weight of the texts: [`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md).

*Introduced:* [9.2](lessons/09-02-the-second-vatican-council.md)

### Coetus Internationalis Patrum

The organized conservative minority at Vatican II.

"International Group of Fathers", formed October 1963, founded by Archbishop Geraldo de Proenca Sigaud of Diamantina; steering committee included Archbishop Marcel Lefebvre and Bishop Luigi Carli. Its letters drew signatures in the hundreds against majorities of two thousand, but it knew the rules well (its appeal to the rules postponed the religious-liberty vote in 1964).

*Introduced:* [9.2](lessons/09-02-the-second-vatican-council.md)

### Nota explicativa praevia

The "preliminary explanatory note" Paul VI attached to chapter 3 of *Lumen Gentium* in November 1964.

Communicated 16 November 1964: episcopal collegiality never acts without its head and does not diminish papal primacy. Collegiality and the *Nota* as doctrine: [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md) 4.5.

*Introduced:* [9.2](lessons/09-02-the-second-vatican-council.md)

### Black Week

The Dutch name for the last week of Vatican II's third session, November 1964, when papal interventions checked the majority.

The *Nota explicativa praevia* (16 November); on 19 November Cardinal Eugene Tisserant announced the religious-liberty vote postponed to the next session, after Coetus members appealed to the rules (a majority petition to the pope with 441 signatures failed to reverse it); Paul VI required nineteen late changes to the decree on ecumenism. *Lumen Gentium* passed 21 November, 2,151 to 5. Paul VI was nobody's rubber stamp.

*Introduced:* [9.2](lessons/09-02-the-second-vatican-council.md)

### Humanae Vitae

Paul VI's encyclical of 25 July 1968 reaffirming the exclusion of contraception, and its contested reception.

The majority of a papal birth-control commission had favoured change (reports to Paul VI June 1966; leaked April 1967). 30 July 1968: Charles Curran and colleagues at the Catholic University of America issued a public dissent, eventually signed by hundreds of theologians; the Canadian bishops' Winnipeg Statement (27 September) counselled those who found it impossible to accept. Weight: an encyclical is at least authentic ordinary magisterium owed religious submission ([`fundamental-theology` 4.3](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-03-religious-submission.md)); John Ford and Germain Grisez (1978) argued it infallible through the ordinary universal magisterium, disputed; this course does not settle it. The teaching: [`moral-theology`](../moral-theology/syllabus.md).

*Introduced:* [9.3](lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)

### Econe consecrations

Archbishop Lefebvre's consecration of four bishops without papal mandate, 30 June 1988.

Paul VI's *Missale Romanum* (3 April 1969) put the new Order of Mass in force on 30 November 1969 (full missal 1970, itself a Latin book; the council kept Latin and widened the vernacular). Lefebvre founded the Society of St Pius X at Econe in 1970 (named after the pope of *Tra le sollecitudini*, 1903: "active participation" and Latin chant both). A decree of the Congregation for Bishops (1 July 1988) and John Paul II's *Ecclesia Dei* (2 July) declared automatic excommunication. Benedict XVI freed the older missal (*Summorum Pontificum*, 2007) and had the four bishops' excommunications remitted (21 January 2009); Francis restricted it again (*Traditionis custodes*, 2021).

*Introduced:* [9.3](lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)

### John Paul II and the fall of communism

The Polish pope's role in the end of communist rule, 1978–1989.

Karol Wojtyla elected 16 October 1978, the first non-Italian pope since 1522. Poland, 2–10 June 1979; at Mass in Warsaw on 2 June he prayed that the Spirit renew the face of the earth, "of this land" (religious language with political resonance, not a call to rise). Gdansk strikes August 1980 produced Solidarity (some ten million members within a year); martial law December 1981; semi-free elections 4 June 1989; the Berlin Wall fell that November. Gorbachev (1992) wrote the changes would not have been possible without this pope (paraphrased). Causal weight disputed: George Weigel (*The Final Revolution*, 1992) for the pope; Stephen Kotkin (*Uncivil Society*, 2009) for the regimes' implosion, with Poland's Solidarity the exception. Rival explanations of 1989: [`international-relations`](../international-relations/syllabus.md) 1.6.

*Introduced:* [9.3](lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)

### Recent popes

Benedict XVI's resignation, Francis, and Leo XIV.

Benedict XVI elected 19 April 2005; announced resignation 11 February 2013, effective 28 February, the first since Gregory XII in 1415. Francis elected 13 March 2013; died 21 April 2025. Robert Prevost, an Augustinian long a missionary and bishop in Peru, elected **8 May 2025** as **Leo XIV**, the first pope from the United States.

*Introduced:* [9.3](lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)

### John Jay reports

The US bishops' commissioned studies of clerical sexual abuse, 2004 and 2011.

The facts came out through journalists, commissions and grand juries: *Boston Globe*, 6 January 2002, on John Geoghan (accused of abusing more than 130 children over three decades, moved from parish to parish); Cardinal Bernard Law resigned 13 December 2002. **Nature and Scope (2004):** allegations not withdrawn or known to be false against 4,392 priests who served 1950–2002, about 4 percent of 109,694 (a second count, CARA, 1960–2002: 4,127 of 94,607, about 4.4 percent; the report prints 4.3); 10,667 people made allegations (mean 2.43 per accused; 56 percent of accused had one; 149 with more than ten accounted for about a quarter); 81 percent of victims male; incidents rose from the mid-1960s, peaked in the 1970s; two-thirds of allegations made only since 1993; 44 percent of the accused ordained before 1960. Allegations are not findings, and the count is a floor. **Causes and Context (2011):** no single cause; the social disruption of the 1960s–70s and thin seminary formation; celibacy and an all-male priesthood, constant across rise and fall, cannot explain the curve; the preponderance of boys largely opportunity. Critics: William Donohue (Catholic League): it explained away homosexual behaviour given 81 percent male victims; A. W. Richard Sipe: mandatory celibacy sustained a culture of secrecy; Francis named clericalism as the root of concealment (Letter to the People of God, 2018). None has settled it; no comparable data from other institutions exists. **Elsewhere:** Ireland's Ryan Report (20 May 2009: abuse "endemic" in boys' industrial schools run by orders, mostly 1936–70); Murphy Report on Dublin (26 November 2009: secrecy, avoidance of scandal and reputation put ahead of children; a sample of 46 of 102 priests, 1975–2004, from the archdiocese's own files); Cloyne Report (13 July 2011: the 1996 guidelines ignored until 2008); Pennsylvania grand jury (14 August 2018: 301 priests, more than 1,000 identifiable victims, six dioceses, seven decades); Theodore McCarrick removed from ministry June 2018, dismissed from the clerical state February 2019; Vatican report November 2020. The failure: the crime was the abusers'; the institutional failure was the bishops', a failure of choice, not ignorance.

*Introduced:* [9.3](lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)

### Vos estis lux mundi

Francis's motu proprio of 7 May 2019 obliging clergy and religious to report abuse and its cover-up.

Set procedures for investigating bishops; made permanent in 2023. Preceded by the acknowledgements: John Paul II to the US cardinals (April 2002); Benedict XVI meeting victims in Washington (2008) and writing to the Catholics of Ireland (19 March 2010); Francis admitting "grave errors" over Chile (all its bishops offered resignations 18 May 2018) and summoning the heads of bishops' conferences (February 2019).

*Introduced:* [9.3](lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)

## Historiographical cruxes

Each entry gives the rival positions at their strongest with the historians who hold them, the evidence each leans on, the premise they divide on, and what would move it. The card takes no side.

### Partings of the ways

How and when Christianity and Judaism became two religions.

**Dunn** (*The Partings of the Ways*, 1991; the plural deliberate): the break came issue by issue, over Temple, Torah, election and above all Christology, and for most purposes was complete after the Bar Kokhba revolt (132–135); Victor's Easter rule tidies the calendar of a church already separate. **Boyarin** (*Border Lines*, 2004): the border was *drawn*, not discovered, by heresiologists (Justin, Irenaeus) and rabbis out of a spectrum of practice; ordinary believers mixed into the fourth century; Victor's campaign is border-drawing from above. **They divide on** whether the separating texts describe a separation that had happened or prescribe one that had not. **Would move it:** evidence of ordinary practice outside bishops' and rabbis' writings: complaints in sermons and canons about Christians keeping Jewish feasts (still found in fourth-century Antioch), mixed burial inscriptions, Easter still reckoned by the Jewish calendar. Both agree there is no single date: not 70, not 135.

*Live in:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-bishops-canon-and-the-parting-of-the-ways.md)

### De Ste Croix vs Sherwin-White

Why Rome punished Christians: for the name, or for defying the magistrate.

The *Past and Present* exchange (1963–64). **G. E. M. de Ste. Croix:** the charge was the name itself; the motive religious: refusal of the gods endangered the [pax deorum](#pax-deorum), and crowds demanded action. Leans on Trajan's "they must be punished" for the name alone, pardon for those who sacrifice (so the offence was the religion, not crimes), Tertullian's crowds. Weak point: explains why accusers came forward, less well why governors convicted. **A. N. Sherwin-White:** [contumacia](#contumacia), refusing a magistrate's order, turned a religious oddity into defiance of Roman authority; the motive administrative and legal. Leans on Pliny's stated ground and the repeated questioning. Weak point: explains the courtroom, less well why anyone was accused. **The crux:** was refusing to sacrifice the *ground* of punishment or only the test that revealed the real offence? Perpetua's trial (203) has both halves: sacrifice offered first as a way out, then the question of the name. (Close reading of Pliny and Trajan, *Letters* 10.96–97, is Boss 1; outline only here.)

*Live in:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md)

### Decius's intent

Whether Decius's order of 250 was an anti-Christian law.

In form a loyalty sacrifice for all (the *libelli* bind even a pagan priestess and never name Christians); in effect the first empire-wide persecution, since Christians could not comply (Fabian of Rome died January 250; mass lapse). The text of the decree is lost, so intent is inferred: J. B. Rives ("The Decree of Decius and the Religion of Empire", *JRS* 1999) reads it as a new empire-wide act of religious unity, not aimed at Christians as such. The open question is intent; judge by wording, target or effect and say which.

*Live in:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-persecution-and-martyrdom.md)

### Burckhardt vs Barnes on Constantine

Whether Constantine believed or calculated.

**Burckhardt** (*Die Zeit Constantins des Grossen*, 1853): essentially unreligious, a genius of power who backed an organized, useful Church; points to the Sol Invictus coinage into the 320s, the law of March 321 honouring the day of the Sun, the executions of Crispus and Fausta (326), the late baptism; dismisses Eusebius as panegyrist. **Barnes** (*Constantine and Eusebius*, 1981): a Christian from 312 pursuing a consistently Christian policy (even a ban on pagan sacrifice after 324, which many reject). **Middle:** Alföldi (*The Conversion of Constantine and Pagan Rome*, 1948): sincere but superstitious, understanding growing slowly; Drake (*Constantine and the Bishops*, 2000): whatever his faith, his policy sought consensus in a broad monotheism and restrained militant Christians too. **The premise at issue:** whether politically useful (or ruthless) acts count as evidence *against* belief (Burckhardt needs it; Barnes denies it), and whether solar imagery means paganism or Christ the true Sun. Modern scholarship has largely abandoned Burckhardt's cynic but not his question. The deathbed baptism is weak evidence either way (delay was common; Ambrose was unbaptized when chosen bishop in 374).

*Live in:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-constantine.md)

### Constantine's vision

Lactantius's dream or Eusebius's daylight cross, and whether it was one event.

**Lactantius** (*Deaths* 44, c. 314–315, within about three years): a dream directing the sign to be put on the shields. **Eusebius** (*Life of Constantine* 1.28–31, 337–339, from Constantine's own oath "long afterwards"): a cross of light above the sun at noon seen by the whole army, "Conquer by this" (Greek; "In hoc signo vinces" is a later Latin rendering), then a dream, then the labarum. His earlier *Church History* 9.9 tells the battle with no vision. **Peter Weiss** ("The Vision of Constantine", 2003), accepted by Barnes (2011): Eusebius's cross and the Gallic panegyric's vision of Apollo (310) are one event, a solar halo seen on campaign in 310 and reread as Christ decades later. Strains: Lactantius, the nearest witness, has no sky vision, and identifying the event does not settle what happened in Constantine.

*Live in:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-constantine.md)

### Arianism as a label

Whether there was an "Arian" party from 318 to 381, or only a polemical label.

**The older narrative** (beginning in Athanasius's polemic): one fight, orthodoxy against a heresy founded by Arius and carried on by his party. Leans on the fact that the same bishops readmitted Arius, deposed his chief opponents and, 355–360, enforced a creed dropping Nicaea's key word. **Ayres** (*Nicaea and its Legacy*, 2004, building on R. P. C. Hanson and Rowan Williams): no continuous Arian party; Athanasius's opponents held several theologies; Nicaea's creed was little invoked until the 350s; a coherent "pro-Nicene" theology formed only in the 360s–380s. Leans on the Dedication Council of Antioch (341): "We have not been followers of Arius — how could Bishops, such as we, follow a Presbyter?" (Athanasius, *De Synodis* 22), and on the 359 creed avoiding Arius's signature claims. **The crux:** whether a shared enemy makes a party. The course keeps "Arian" as the period's name in quotation marks and calls the 359 party [Homoian](#homoians).

*Live in:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md)

### Leo and Attila

Whether Leo I turned back Attila in 452.

Attila invaded northern Italy and destroyed Aquileia in 452. **Prosper of Aquitaine** (contemporary, close to Leo's circle): an embassy of Leo with two senior laymen, Avienus and Trygetius; credits God's help and Leo. **Hydatius** (Spanish chronicler): famine and disease among the Huns, and troops sent by Marcian. **Leo himself** never mentions it. Raphael's apostles with swords come from later medieval tellings. Defensible: the embassy happened with Leo in it; that he alone caused the withdrawal cannot be shown. Prosper had reason to magnify Leo, Hydatius had none, and Leo's silence cuts both ways.

*Live in:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md)

### Rubenson and Brakke on Antony

Which Antony: the *Life*'s unlettered hero, or the Platonist teacher of the letters.

The *Life* gives an Antony who never learned his letters (72) yet defeated philosophers, bowed to bishops (67) and shunned Arians and Meletians. **Samuel Rubenson** (*The Letters of St. Antony*, 1990): seven letters under his name are genuine and show a teacher at home in Platonist self-knowledge and Origenist allegory. **David Brakke** (*Athanasius and the Politics of Asceticism*, 1995): the *Life* is part of Athanasius's campaign to bind Egypt's monks to his episcopate against rival groups. Strains: the letters are also a selection, and "unlettered" in Greek hagiography can mean without rhetorical schooling. The *Life* still establishes that Antony existed and was famous, what practice was admired around 360, and how a bishop wanted monks to relate to bishops; its limit is the private Antony. The passage in *Life* 69 also shows the other side claimed Antony too.

*Live in:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md)

### The holy man

Peter Brown's thesis that late-antique holy men had power as outsiders.

Brown, "The Rise and Function of the Holy Man in Late Antiquity" (*JRS* 1971): Syrian holy men, outsiders to village kinship, worked as patrons and arbiters; they had power because they stood outside society. Brown revisited and qualified it in 1998. Critics pressed the harder point: we meet these men only in hagiography, so the "function" we see may be the one their biographers wanted to show. Same limit as the *Life of Antony*.

*Live in:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md)

### Was monasticism a protest against the imperial Church

The popular claim that the desert fled Constantine's Church.

**For:** the mass growth came after 313; the *Life* casts the monk as the martyr's successor (47); withdrawal from cities and wealth implies a judgment on a comfortable Church. **Against:** Antony withdrew c. 270 and entered Pispir c. 285, before Constantine; the *Life* has him bow to bishops and fight for Nicaea; Basil became a bishop; Athanasius allied with the monks. The distinction any verdict needs: protest against the *world* (or wealth and status, inside the Church too) versus protest against the Church or its bishops. The pro-episcopal evidence comes from a bishop's text with a purpose.

*Live in:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-desert-and-the-monastery.md)

### What forced conversion achieved in Saxony

Whether coercion worked, or Christianization succeeded despite it.

The narrow conversion clearly happened by force: Verden, the *Capitulatio*, tithes, counts. Within two generations came Corvey (822) and the *Heliand* (c. 830); a century later Saxon kings led Latin Christendom. **One reading:** coercion worked, and the lesson is grim. **The other:** what worked was later Christianization by monks, parishes and a vernacular Gospel, *despite* a conversion Alcuin already thought had failed. The evidence cannot separate them: there is no unconquered Saxony to compare.

*Live in:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md)

### Pirenne thesis

Henri Pirenne's claim that Islam, not the Germanic invasions, ended the Roman Mediterranean and made Charlemagne's Europe.

*Mohammed and Charlemagne* (1937, posthumous), as three claims: (1) the Germanic kingdoms did not end the Roman Mediterranean (Merovingian Gaul still traded, minted gold, wrote on Egyptian papyrus); (2) the Arab conquests broke that unity (papyrus, spices and eastern merchants vanish from Gaul in the seventh and eighth centuries; gold gives way to silver); (3) cut off, the West turned north and inland, which made Charlemagne's Europe ("without Muhammad ... no Charlemagne", paraphrased). **Critics, each hitting a different claim:** Richard Hodges and David Whitehouse (*Mohammed, Charlemagne and the Origins of Europe*, 1983), from excavation: the western Mediterranean economy had already decayed badly in the sixth century (claim 1). Michael McCormick (*Origins of the European Economy*, 2001), from travellers, coins and goods: contact thinned but did not end, and trade revived from the late eighth century, with European slaves sold into the Islamic world among the main exports (the "closed sea" of claim 2). A data point the lessons use: the last surviving Merovingian royal papyrus is from 692, while the papal chancery used papyrus from Muslim-ruled lands until 1057. What survives of the thesis is Boss 3; outline only here.

*Live in:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-rise-of-islam-and-the-christian-mediterranean.md)

### Papal revolution

How to read the investiture contest as a whole: a legal revolution, or a struggle for right order.

**Harold Berman** (*Law and Revolution*, 1983): a "papal revolution" that created the first modern Western legal system, the Church's canon law, and by contrast the secular state. **Gerd Tellenbach** (*Libertas*, 1936): a religious struggle for "right order in the world", with investiture a symptom. Either way, the 1075–76 break came over Milan, excommunicated councillors and Gregory's claim to judge a king, and the first certain general ban on lay investiture is from 1078.

*Live in:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md)

### Crusader motives debate

Why men went: penitential piety, material gain, or a mix.

**Materialist** (older; Georges Duby's essay on aristocratic "youth", 1964, English in *The Chivalrous Society*, 1977): landless younger sons after estates and plunder. **Jonathan Riley-Smith** (*The First Crusaders, 1095–1131*, 1997), from charters in which departing knights sold or pledged land: crusading was ruinously expensive (several years' income for a knight), ran in families, drew heads of households as well as younger sons; the motive was penitential piety. **Christopher Tyerman** (*The Invention of the Crusades*, 1998; *God's War*, 2006): skeptical of any single motive; lordship, kinship, money and social pressure alongside devotion; the "crusade" as a defined institution took shape gradually after 1095. (Relating the speech versions to motives is Boss 4.)

*Live in:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-the-crusades.md)

### Jerusalem 1099 death toll

How many were killed when the crusaders took Jerusalem.

*Gesta Francorum*: crusaders waded in blood to their ankles. Raymond of Aguilers (chaplain; tr. Krey 1921): "rode in blood up to their knees and bridle reins", God's just judgment, an echo of Revelation 14:20, a biblical way of saying *judgment*, not a depth measurement. Fulcher: almost ten thousand in the Temple alone. Ibn al-Athir (early thirteenth century): seventy thousand, probably more than the city's whole population. **Benjamin Kedar** (*Crusades* 3, 2004) brought in Abu Bakr ibn al-Arabi, who had lived in Jerusalem shortly before: about three thousand killed in al-Aqsa. Kedar also argued the sources stress killing and say little about captives, so the massacre was more thorough than usual for a stormed town. The large numbers are inflated; the massacre was real and exceptional; deflating seventy thousand is not a defence.

*Live in:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-the-crusades.md)

### Did John XXII reverse Exiit

Whether *Cum inter nonnullos* (1323) contradicted *Exiit qui seminat* (1279).

**The Franciscans: yes.** *Exiit* praised total renunciation as taught by Christ, and *Cum inter nonnullos* made denying Christ's ownership heretical to assert obstinately. **John and his defenders: no.** *Exiit* approved a way of life (renouncing *dominium*) and said nothing binding about Christ's legal rights; *Ad conditorem* added that with consumables use cannot be separated from ownership, so the fiction was empty. **Brian Tierney** (*Origins of Papal Infallibility, 1150-1350*, 1972): the quarrel helped produce the idea of papal infallibility: Olivi wanted *Exiit* irreversible, and John, seeing such claims as limits on his sovereignty, rejected them in *Quia quorundam* (1324). Critics: canonists held versions of the idea earlier. Whether the bulls contradict, and what that implies, belongs to [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md); historically, each side could cite words.

*Live in:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-friars-and-universities.md)

### Persecuting society

R. I. Moore's thesis that Latin Europe learned to persecute between about 950 and 1250.

*The Formation of a Persecuting Society* (1987; revised 2007): heretics, Jews and lepers were defined as threats and pursued by similar methods, driven less by popular hatred than by the clerks of expanding papal and royal governments, for whom a defined enemy justified new power. **Critics** grant the pattern but doubt the direction: the Rhineland massacres of 1096 were popular violence some bishops tried to stop.

*Live in:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md)

### Cathar church debate

Whether an organized dualist Cathar church existed in Languedoc, or was largely the prosecutors' construct.

**Mark Pegg** (*The Corruption of Angels*, 2001; *A Most Holy War*, 2008): no organized dualist church; the "good men" were local holy men in a world where orthodox and heterodox piety mixed; the dualist system was supplied largely by polemicists and inquisitors' questions. Leans on the 1245–1246 depositions: villagers describe honouring good men, rarely a theology; questions fixed in advance; answers translated from Occitan into Latin by notaries. Moore's *The War on Heresy* (2012) moved to this side. **Bernard Hamilton** (on Saint-Felix, 1978, and on the Bogomils): a real dualist church with bishops and dioceses, linked to the Bogomils of the Balkans and Byzantium. Leans on texts by dualists themselves (the Italian *Book of the Two Principles*, Cathar rituals in Latin and Occitan) and the account of a council at Saint-Felix where the Bogomil *papa* Nicetas ordained bishops, which he defends as genuine and dates to about 1174–1177. Argued out in *Cathars in Question* (ed. Antonio Sennis, 2016). **The crux:** what the inquisitors' records can bear: if testimony was shaped by the questions, the records show the prosecutors' categories; if independent dualist texts describe the same church, the categories were not invented. Independent evidence for Pegg's side is scarce by nature. Neither side denies the persecution.

*Live in:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md)

### Kill them all

Whether Arnaud Amalric said "Kill them all; the Lord knows his own" at Beziers.

The only source is Caesarius of Heisterbach, *Dialogue on Miracles* 5.21 (tr. Scott and Swinton Bland, 1929): a Rhineland Cistercian who never went to Languedoc, writing c. 1219–1223 for novices; he misdates the crusade to 1210, inflates the town to more than a hundred thousand, and hedges: the abbot "is said to have replied". Neither Arnaud's own report nor the contemporary chronicler Peter of Vaux-de-Cernay records the words; most historians treat the line as unverified and probably apocryphal. The opposite error: dismissing the quotation acquits no one; the massacre is documented by the legate's own letter reporting the killing as victory, and Caesarius's gloss shows a Cistercian audience could find such an order reasonable. Weak evidence about Arnaud, strong evidence about attitudes.

*Live in:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md)

### The decay debate

Whether the Church on the eve of 1517 was in decay.

**At the top** the scandals are real and undisputed (Renaissance popes, pluralist absentee bishops, Lateran V's unenforced decrees). **At the bottom:** Bernd Moeller (Protestant; 1965 essay): Germany around 1500 one of the most churchly societies of the Middle Ages (pious foundations, confraternities, church building booming); Eamon Duffy (*The Stripping of the Altars*, 1992), from wills, churchwardens' accounts and devotional books: English traditional religion vigorous and popular; Diarmaid MacCulloch agrees the old Church was strong. Current consensus: real abuses and thriving piety at once. Keep two questions apart: was there decay, and why did *this* protest become a schism (a particular indulgence, theology, prince, and the press)? Arguing from Alexander VI to "people had stopped believing" changes evidence halfway.

*Live in:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-the-late-medieval-church-and-luthers-protest.md)

### The posting dispute

Whether Luther nailed the 95 Theses to the Wittenberg church door.

Luther never describes it. The first account is **Melanchthon's** 1546 preface, after Luther's death; he reached Wittenberg only in August 1518. **Erwin Iserloh** (Catholic, 1961): no posting; Luther went through proper channels (the letter to Albrecht) and published only when the bishops did not reply. **Martin Treu** (2006) drew attention to a note by Luther's secretary **Georg Rorer**, probably 1544: theses posted on the doors of the Wittenberg *churches* on the eve of All Saints 1517; not an eyewitness either, but written in Luther's lifetime, and its plural matches the university rule that disputation notices went up on church doors. Many historians now think a posting probably happened as routine academic practice. At stake: whether 31 October was a public challenge or a loyal appeal; the theses spread by print, not by the door.

*Live in:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-the-late-medieval-church-and-luthers-protest.md)

### Fast and slow Reformation

How quickly, and how willingly, the English people became Protestant.

| Position | Speed and direction | Source of Protestantism | Key evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. G. Dickens, *The English Reformation* (1964) | Fast, welcomed from below | Lollardy, anticlericalism, Lutheran books; rooted by 1553 in the south-east | Heresy trials; Protestant wording in will preambles |
| J. J. Scarisbrick, *The Reformation and the English People* (1984) | Slow; most did not want it | The Crown | Lay giving to guilds, chantries and fabric up to the break |
| Christopher Haigh, *English Reformations* (1993) | Slow, imposed from above | A series of reversible acts of state | Visitation records; regional studies (Lancashire) |
| Eamon Duffy, *The Stripping of the Altars* (1992); *The Voices of Morebath* (2001) | Slow; a violent break with a vigorous religion | The Crown, obeyed with grief | Churchwardens' accounts, primers, rood screens, bequests |
| Ethan Shagan, *Popular Politics and the English Reformation* (2003) | Neither simply welcomed nor imposed | Negotiated collaboration for people's own ends | Local disputes; purchases of church goods and lands |

**Morebath** (Devon): vicar Sir Christopher Trychay (1520–1574) kept the accounts; Duffy reads a parish that spent on its saint, Sidwell, hid its goods under Edward, sent five men to the rebel camp outside Exeter in 1549, welcomed Mary, then conformed under Elizabeth. Critics: one articulate vicar is one parish, and quick conformity may show shallow attachment. Most historians now accept a slow Reformation in most regions; what remains disputed, the lesson says, is why people conformed and what conformity meant. (Naming the crux and its evidence is Boss 6; outline only.)

*Live in:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)

### Religion or treason

Whether Elizabethan Catholic priests like Campion died for religion or for treason.

**Government's case** (strongest after 1570): the bull told subjects not to obey; William Allen and Robert Persons later backed invasion plans; war with Spain from 1585; a priest acting on Rome's orders was a possible enemy agent; the 1580 explanation attributed to Gregory XIII left the deposing claim standing. **Catholic case** (strongest on the trials): Campion convicted on a charge of plotting abroad historians generally regard as fabricated; the "bloody question" tried a hypothetical; after 1585 merely being a priest ordained since 1559 in England was treason. The loyalty problem was real and created by the bull, but the statutes made the priest's office proof of treason. Both descriptions fit different parts of the record.

*Live in:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)

### Catholic Reform and Counter-Reformation

Jedin's two names for one movement, and whether either fits.

Hubert Jedin, *Katholische Reformation oder Gegenreformation?* (1946, for the council's four-hundredth anniversary): **Catholic Reform**, the Church's self-renewal from its own sources beginning in the late Middle Ages; **Counter-Reformation**, its self-defence against Protestantism from the mid-sixteenth century; used together, roughly as soul to body, meeting at Trent. John Bossy (*Past and Present*, 1970): Tridentine discipline tried to make the parish, rather than kin and community, the unit of religious life. The confessionalization historians set Catholic and Protestant discipline side by side. See [Early modern Catholicism](#early-modern-catholicism).

*Live in:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)

### Early modern Catholicism

O'Malley's umbrella term for everything Catholics did in the period.

John O'Malley, *Trent and All That: Renaming Catholicism in the Early Modern Era* (2000): both of Jedin's labels distort. Test case, the Jesuits: fighting Protestantism was incidental to their program until about the mid-1550s and marginal in most missions; "reforming the Church" was not their aim, since Trent's instruments of reform were bishops and parish priests, offices they refused. "Early modern Catholicism" lets reform, counter-reformation and confessional age sit together with missions, art, schooling and new orders. France's slow reception is his point: "Tridentine" often names a century of bishops, kings and new orders.

*Live in:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)

### Kamen vs Lea

What the Spanish Inquisition was and did to Spain: not a dispute about body counts.

**Henry Charles Lea** (*A History of the Inquisition of Spain*, 1906–07): already rejected Llorente's inflated totals; read the Inquisition as the instrument of a pervasive clerical intolerance that deformed Spanish religion and intellectual life and contributed to Spain's decline. **Henry Kamen** (his "revision", 1990s): driven as much by crown politics and Old-Christian social conflict as by religion; touched most Spaniards only lightly; not a cause of decline; the secular courts of Antwerp alone executed 103 heretics in 1557–62, more than Spain's tribunals in those years. They agree roughly on scale after 1540. **Would move it:** regional and comparative evidence: density of the tribunal's network (familiares, rural contact, case density), and whether places with more inquisitorial activity show lower literacy, book output or scholarship (recent quantitative municipal studies).

*Live in:* [6.5](lessons/06-05-the-spanish-and-roman-inquisitions.md)

### Black Legend

The anti-Spanish caricature built on Las Casas, and the argument over how to judge Spain's empire.

Las Casas's *Brevisima* was translated and reprinted across Protestant Europe, above all in the Netherlands during the revolt; de Bry's engravings (1598) made its atrocities images. Julian Juderias popularized *la leyenda negra* in 1914 (the term used earlier, Emilia Pardo Bazan, 1899). **Lewis Hanke** (*The Spanish Struggle for Justice in the Conquest of America*, 1949): no other empire argued so publicly about whether its conquest was just; the debates, laws and reformers set Spain apart. **Benjamin Keen** (1969, 1971): a "White Legend", judging Spain by its theorists instead of its practice (enforcement, labour, mortality). What would move it: enforcement records and comparison with empires that held no such debates. The irony: the Legend's key text was written by a Spanish friar for the Spanish king. The inflated Inquisition numbers fed it too ([6.5](lessons/06-05-the-spanish-and-roman-inquisitions.md)).

*Live in:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-conquest-and-conscience-in-the-americas.md)

### Did the rites ban destroy the China mission

Whether Rome's 1715 ruling closed China to Christianity.

**For:** the mission grew under accommodation to about 200,000 by 1700 (missionaries' counts) and collapsed after 1715; the ban removed the one argument that made Christianity acceptable to officials (compatibility with being Chinese); Nicolas Standaert shows Chinese Christians wrote into the controversy, mostly on the Jesuit side. **Against:** timing (Kangxi's permits date from 1706, provoked by Tournon, nine years before the bull); causes (the effective proscription was Yongzheng's in 1724, with other motives); survival (Liam Brockey, *Journey to the East*, 2007: converts mostly not literati, communities persisted; Eugenio Menegon, *Ancestors, Virgins, and Friars*, 2009: a Fujian community served by Dominicans). **Deeper:** Jacques Gernet (*China and the Christian Impact*, 1982): Chinese critics saw an incompatibility accommodation papered over, so no ruling could have saved it. Verdict open; the honest claim is "made a hard mission harder". The underlying crux of the rites: whose understanding fixes a rite's meaning, the scholar's or the villager's.

*Live in:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-accommodation-in-asia.md)

### Where the Revolution broke with the Church

Whether the break was made certain by the Enlightenment or was an avoidable mistake of 1790.

**The oath** (McManners): the first reforms were Gallican and many Catholic deputies voted for them; the oath of 27 November 1790 made a dispute over authority a test of loyalty, split the clergy and made refractories suspects. **Climate** (Voltaire; Van Kley's Jansenist-Gallican quarrels): the clergy was already a problem the state felt entitled to solve. The complication for both: dechristianization in 1793–94 struck jurors too and owed much to war and Terror. The Civil Constitution dispute was not reform against no reform but who had authority to reform.

*Live in:* [8.1](lessons/08-01-enlightenment-revolution-and-napoleon.md)

### Was Vatican I free

Whether pressure on the minority made the council unfree.

**August Hasler** (*How the Pope Became Infallible*, English 1981): the pressure made the council unfree (a pope who was party to the question and lobbied for it; debate closed; the minority deputation refused). **Klaus Schatz** (*Vaticanum I*, 1992–94): defends the participants' freedom (88 voted *non placet* and the letter renews it; reverence, not threat, is the stated reason for leaving). **The minority's case** was largely historical: Karl Joseph Hefele, historian of the councils, pressed Pope Honorius's condemnation by the Sixth Council (681); others appealed to Constance and asked for infallibility tied to the churches' witness; many, Dupanloup among them, thought a definition possibly true but inopportune. Freedom to vote is not freedom from pressure; evidence of sanctions or the minority's private correspondence would move it.

*Live in:* [8.2](lessons/08-02-pius-ix-vatican-i-and-the-fall-of-rome.md)

### Was modernism a system

Whether "modernism" described real persons' views or was a construct of the document that named it.

*Pascendi* §39: the modernists' ideas form one tightly joined body. **Loisy** (*Simples reflexions*, 1908): he supplied more material than any Catholic, but the system was "a compilation of bits and pieces" assembled by Roman theologians from writers who disagreed (Blondel's philosophy of action, Le Roy on dogma, Loisy's exegesis). **Gabriel Daly** (*Transcendence and Immanence*, 1980) defines a modernist loosely: a Catholic whose thinking challenged the reigning neo-scholastic scheme at some significant point. **Defender:** a pope condemning a tendency must state it at the level of its premises, and the isolated premises (knowledge cannot pass phenomena; faith begins in felt need; dogma the changing expression of it) did run through several writers. The crux: judged as a description of persons (Loisy wins) or as a logical reconstruction of shared premises (the method is defensible).

*Live in:* [8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)

### The silence debate

Whether Pius XII failed the Jews of Europe by not speaking out publicly.

**The silence critique** takes the absence of public protest as the fact that matters: from Rolf Hochhuth's play *The Deputy* (1963) to John Cornwell's *Hitler's Pope* (1999), a charge Cornwell softened in 2004. **The prudence defence** takes the sheltering and the private channel as the fact that matters: Ronald Rychlak; the Vatican archivist Johan Ickx (*Le Bureau*, 2020, from about 2,800 requests for help handled by the Secretariat of State). **David Kertzer** (*The Pope at War*, 2022), from the files opened 2 March 2020: a pope driven by fear for the institution and cautious to the point of paralysis, not by antisemitism; on his reading the Vatican's interventions in the October 1943 roundup went chiefly to the baptized and those in mixed marriages. Nobody disputes the facts in [Pius XII and the war](#pius-xii-and-the-war); "the archives proved..." in either direction overstates the case. (Separating public from private evidence, the crux, and the verdict are Boss 9; outline only.)

*Live in:* [9.1](lessons/09-01-the-church-and-the-totalitarian-states.md)

### Issues under the issues

John O'Malley's three questions beneath all Vatican II's quarrels.

*What Happened at Vatican II* (2008): how may the Church change; how should the centre (pope and Curia) relate to the periphery (the bishops); in what **style** should the Church speak. His distinctive claim is the third: the council gave up the juridical, condemnatory genre of earlier councils for a rhetoric of invitation and praise. His position in the continuity debate: rupture in style within doctrinal continuity (see [Hermeneutic of reform](#hermeneutic-of-reform)).

*Live in:* [9.2](lessons/09-02-the-second-vatican-council.md)

### European alliance

Ralph Wiltgen's thesis that a Rhine-country alliance of bishops captured Vatican II.

Wiltgen, a Divine Word priest who ran a news service at the council, *The Rhine Flows into the Tiber* (1967): tallies, letters and backroom detail found nowhere else in English, much checkable against the acts; on his figures 79 of the northern slate's 109 candidates were elected on 16 October 1962, nearly half the elected seats. Its framing (a planned campaign, chapters counted as "victories", sympathy in places for the Coetus; reissued by traditionalist publishers) is one reading: use it for what an insider could document. **O'Malley and most recent histories:** a majority and a minority; the northern bishops led opinion that already existed; early evidence before papal pressure (46 against the liturgy schema; 1,368 for stopping the revelation schema). **Minority's reply:** the lopsided final votes (2,147 to 4, 2,151 to 5) followed concessions and pressure for near-unanimity, measuring consent to finished texts, not opening opinion. The crux: what a vote in a council measures.

*Live in:* [9.2](lessons/09-02-the-second-vatican-council.md)

### Was the abuse crisis post-conciliar

Whether the abuse crisis was a consequence of the council, or older.

The claim comes from opposite directions: the council's aftermath loosened discipline, or pre-conciliar formation and secrecy produced both abuse and concealment. The John Jay curve rises from the mid-1960s ("post-conciliar"); but 44 percent of the accused were ordained before 1960, the Irish industrial-school abuse was mostly before 1970, and the habit of secrecy is older than both. The council, the sexual revolution and the wider rise in deviance share the same years; a date is not a cause. Separating them needs comparable data from other institutions, which John Jay noted does not exist.

*Live in:* [9.3](lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)

### Bologna school

The reading of Vatican II that finds the council in the event, not only the texts.

Giuseppe Alberigo (ed.), *History of Vatican II* (5 vols, 1995–2001): the council is found in the **event**, the assembly's experience and drive to renewal, which the compromise texts only partly record; a real, welcome novelty ("rupture" is mostly its critics' word). Evidence treated as decisive: diaries, drafts, votes, the overturned preparatory schemas. Embarrassed by texts whose plain sense repeats earlier teaching where no compromise explains the wording. The Lefebvrist Society also sees a rupture (in the texts: religious liberty, ecumenism, the new Mass) and deplores it. See [Hermeneutic of reform](#hermeneutic-of-reform).

*Live in:* [9.3](lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)

### Hermeneutic of reform

Benedict XVI's reading of Vatican II as reform in continuity, against a "hermeneutic of discontinuity and rupture".

Address to the Curia, 22 December 2005 (also Agostino Marchetto, 2005): the council is found in the **texts** as promulgated, read within the tradition; reform is renewal in the continuity of the one Church, with discontinuity only in contingent applications (his example: religious liberty). Must deny that the event or "spirit" can outrank the texts; embarrassed by a reversal at the level of principle, not application. The Society of St Pius X and the Bologna school both see a rupture and judge it oppositely; Benedict denies it against both; O'Malley: a rupture in style within doctrinal continuity. Continuity and rupture as theory, and Newman's tests: [`fundamental-theology` 5.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-02-newmans-theory-of-development.md). The first form of the dispute: the modernist crisis ([8.3](lessons/08-03-leo-xiii-and-the-modernist-crisis.md)); the liturgy became its flag.

*Live in:* [9.3](lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)

## Roman primacy: the evidence by era

The syllabus's primacy thread, gathered into one timeline. For the two readings that recur in every row, see [Primacy of honour vs jurisdiction](#primacy-of-honour-vs-jurisdiction). Each row is the historical evidence as the lessons give it, with what it shows and what it does not. **What the record proves theologically is not argued here**: that belongs to [`ecclesiology-and-mariology`](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/syllabus.md) (and [`apologetics-catholic-claims`](../apologetics-catholic-claims/syllabus.md), where Orthodox and Protestant readings are stated at their strongest).

| Era | Evidence | What it shows | What it doesn't | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| c. 96–c. 200 | 1 Clement admonishes Corinth, possibly unasked, warning of danger to the disobedient (ch. 59); Ignatius greets a church that "presides over love"; Irenaeus III.3.2 (*potiorem principalitatem*); Polycrates convenes an Asian synod at Victor's request; Victor attempts to excommunicate Asia | A Roman sense of responsibility for other churches, and other churches' sense that Roman communion mattered (Schatz); Rome could set a wider agenda | No named single bishop at Rome until mid-century; the letters come from a *church*, not a person; the Irenaeus Latin supports rival readings; Victor was resisted and rebuked, and nothing shows his sentence took effect. Schatz grants no one in 100 or 200 would have called Rome's bishop head of all Christians; Afanasiev reads a primacy of witness and love, many Protestant historians size, wealth, capital and apostles' graves | [1.2](lessons/01-02-bishops-canon-and-the-parting-of-the-ways.md) |
| 339–346 | Athanasius's refuge in Rome and Pope Julius's defence of him; *Cunctos populos* (380) defines orthodoxy by the faith of Damasus of Rome and Peter of Alexandria | Rome as a refuge and a reference point for Nicene orthodoxy | The same law names Alexandria alongside Rome; Constantinople I (381) met with no Roman legates and ranked Constantinople second by canon 3 | [2.1](lessons/02-01-nicaea-and-the-imperial-church.md) |
| 431–553 | Roman synod condemns Nestorius (430) and legates endorse Cyril's session (431); Leo's anniversary sermons: Peter's care lives on in his see, "whose dignity is not abated even in so unworthy an heir" (Sermon 3.4); at Chalcedon the Tome shapes the definition, the legates hold first place, the acts record "Peter has spoken thus through Leo"; Gelasius (494) claims obedience due to the see "above all others" | Leo stated the Roman claim more fully than any predecessor; the council honoured his letter in Petrine language. Catholic historians read a real primacy of teaching the East honoured without theorizing it in Rome's terms; Schaff called Leo the first pope in the proper sense (the papacy took shape in him) | The Tome was examined against Cyril before acceptance; canon 28 passed over the legates' protest and grounded rank in a city's political status, not Peter, and the East kept it; Vigilius was detained, excluded and struck from the diptychs until he signed. Orthodox historians read a council that tested Rome's teaching and found it orthodox, Rome first in honour | [2.2](lessons/02-02-ephesus-chalcedon-and-leos-rome.md) |
| 664 | Whitby: Oswiu decides for Rome's Easter because Peter held the keys (Bede III.25) | The appeal to Peter carried weight in a royal decision | A king settled the question; the Roman see was not in the room | [3.1](lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md) |
| 750–964 | Nicholas I (858–867) judges kings, archbishops and a patriarch (Lothar II; Cologne and Trier deposed; Hincmar reversed; Photius refused); the forged Donation and Pseudo-Isidore invoke Roman authority | Unusually direct evidence of what Rome *claimed*; ninth-century churchmen found Roman authority the most useful to invent documents for. Schatz: the claims were older than the forgeries, and the see's standing survived the iron century | Not that the claims were accepted: Constantinople declared Nicholas deposed (867), Frankish bishops resisted, and in the tenth century lay families and an emperor controlled the see. Meyendorff reads Nicholas as a break with the first millennium's conciliar ordering; Protestant readers since Luther see the forgeries as scaffolding of papal power | [3.3](lessons/03-03-popes-franks-and-emperors.md) |
| 1049–1122 | Legates preside over councils and depose bishops; the 1059 decree governs papal elections; Gregory VII sentences a king (1076) and a large party of his subjects acts on it; *Dictatus Papae* lists the curia's claims | The bishop of Rome claimed supreme jurisdiction in the Latin Church and largely exercised it. Catholic historians stress that the reformers cited older texts (Gelasius, earlier canon collections; *Dictatus* 23 names Symmachus and Ennodius) | That the claims were accepted as settled even in the West: Henry's bishops rejected Gregory at Worms and Brixen, an antipope held Rome, the deposition worked only where princes wanted it; some cited texts were Pseudo-Isidorian forgeries; nothing speaks for the East. Papadakis with Meyendorff (1994) treat the reform ecclesiology as the real novelty behind the estrangement; Reformation polemic made "Hildebrand" the type of usurpation | [4.1](lessons/04-01-the-reform-papacy-and-the-investiture-contest.md) |
| 858–1965 (East) | Nicholas I judges a patriarchal election; Photius needs Rome's recognition (879–880); Greek signatures at Lyons (1274) and Florence (1439) on texts affirming Roman primacy and "full power" | Dvornik: Byzantium long acknowledged a real Roman role as final court of appeal; Ravenna (2007, §41): Rome held first place among the patriarchs in the first millennium | Photius's synod deposed a pope; both unions were rejected; Constantinople claimed second rank on political grounds (canon 28); Florence's clauses can be read as measuring the full power; signed is not received. Meyendorff: primacy of honour; Chadwick: slow Western centralization the East never shared | [4.2](lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md) |
| 1198–1303 | Popes judge imperial elections (1201), hold kingdoms in fief (England 1213), tax clergy across Europe, take appeals from everywhere; Lateran IV legislates for every parish; *plenitudo potestatis*; *Unam Sanctam* (1302) | The high point of papal jurisdiction in the West; no earlier era shows Roman primacy exercised so widely or accepted as ordinary by Latin bishops. Duffy: a zenith carrying the seeds of overreach | That rulers conceded papal authority over temporal affairs as of right: kings used it when it served (John) and defeated it when not (Philip IV); *Per venerabilem* conceded the French king's temporal independence. Nor does it speak for the whole Church: Orthodox historians see canon 5's Latin patriarchs as the fruit of 1204; Protestant historians, a turn to worldly lordship | [5.1](lessons/05-01-papal-monarchy-innocent-iii-to-boniface-viii.md) |
| 1378–1449 | For forty years no party proposed doing without a pope; every expedient aimed at one undoubted pope; Constance preserved the Roman line (Gregory XII reconvoked before resigning) | The papal office itself was never in question, only who held it | Whether the reconvocation was the legal hinge or a courtesy to a pope with few followers.
